R E A L  S M A R T  M O N E Y
T H E  U N C E N S O R E D  H I S T O R Y 
E
veryone Talks About Smart Money... But Almost No One Understands It. You hear it 
everywhere: Smart Money Concepts, SMC patterns, liquidity grabs, market structure 
shifts. But here’s the truth:  
 
“Smart Money Concepts” (SMC) became a buzz term—popularised by influencers, not 
professionals. SMC was sold as the secret to “trading like the banks,” and sounds elite, 
secretive, and powerful. In reality, it became a watered-down narrative repackaged for 
beginners—desperate for structure, certainty, and shortcuts.
While some “Smart Money Concepts” are valid, they’re often stripped of their context, 
missing the data and depth needed to be effective.This book isn’t written for retail traders 
chasing lifestyle dreams on YouTube. It’s for those who want to understand how markets 
really work — and how to trade like the professionals. If you're reading this, you're here 
for real data over fantasies, and facts over feelings.
LTA Concepts 2.0 is a system that fuses institutional-level data with advanced technical 
frameworks. It’s more than a book; it’s a gateway into a community of traders who build 
elite skills.
The Other Side Of The Market
Smart Money is the most abused term in trading. The result? A generation of retail trad-
ers who think they're trading with institutions... but are simply following retail repackages.
LTA Concepts 2.0 exists to turn this narrative around. We show you what Smart Money 
really means — and how to track it, measure it, and use it to make strategic decisions in 
real-time. And we’re not the only ones. Top traders, such as Larry Williams, Linda  
Raschke, and Tom Dante, all focus on data-backed trading.
Many former Robbins World Cup Trading Champions and finalists have utilized concepts 
such as volume profile, COT positioning, and order flow to gain their edge. Again, you 
hear the term “Smart Money” all over the internet. But have you ever asked where it 
came from? The majority of retail traders have no clue.
I’m going to reveal the history of real smart money:
The First Publicly Traded Stock 
In the early 1600’s, Amsterdam had become the financial center of the world. The Dutch 
East India Company (VOC) introduced the first publicly traded stock in 1602, and the 
Amsterdam Stock Exchange became the first official securities exchange.
Investors, traders, and merchants could now buy and sell shares, speculate on prices, 
and even trade derivatives (yes—futures and options existed!)
1
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 12 =====
At this point, “Smart Money” was not a term used, but the concept was very real. VOC 
executives had access to insider knowledge, like shipping routes, trade routes, and 
political developments. They could buy ahead of good news or quietly sell into strength 
before the public caught on. The smart money operators, such as Wealthy Merchants, 
Financiers, Bankers, and Sophisticated Traders, had an unfair advantage.
In 1688, Joseph de la Vega wrote Confusion of Confusions, the first book ever on stock 
trading. His famous quote was, "The players who make the wind blow." — a poetic way 
of describing market manipulators and insiders who moved markets while the public 
guessed.
The use of rumors, false orders, and manipulation to bait the public into bad  
positions. The contrast between the “discreet investor” (patient, informed, calculated)  
vs. the “blind speculator” (emotional, reactive, easily misled).
Politically connected insiders and financiers benefited: They received early access to 
shares before the public, sold into public euphoria, and helped manufacture hype in the 
press. These operators used a strategy very similar to what is now known as “pump and 
dump”.
Isaac Newton, one of the most brilliant minds in history, was famously wiped out in the 
crash. When asked why he couldn’t avoid it, he said: “I can calculate the motions of the 
heavenly bodies, but not the madness of men.” This highlights how emotional public 
traders were no match for informed insiders.
2

The New Capital Of Finance
In the 1700s–1800s, as London became the global capital of finance, a new class of 
“smart money” emerged:
Families like the Rothschilds built immense fortunes through information asymmetry. 
During the Napoleonic Wars, Nathan Rothschild used fast courier networks to get news 
of Wellington’s victory at Waterloo before anyone else. He famously dumped bonds to 
create panic, then bought them all back at a cheap price—a classic smart money play.  
 
The London Stock Exchange (LSE) was formalized in 1801, with jobbers (market  
makers) and brokers executing trades. Jobbers controlled order flow and pricing—they 
were the ones who “saw the tape” and understood which way the market was leaning. 
Retail investors, often referred to as "the public," were again second to information and  
positioning. 
 
Wealthy traders would leak fake news to newspapers to move prices. “Bear raids” and 
“cornering the market” became tactics—long before SEC rules existed. The phrase “buy 
the rumor, sell the news” came directly from this era. 
 
Also, in the 1800’s, you can trace early signs of the term being used on the race track, 
describing the insiders who knew what horse was going to win.
3
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
I  N  T  R  O  D  U  C  T  I  O  N


===== PAGE 14 =====
The Wall Street Takeover 
Wall Street later borrowed the term to describe bankers, big speculators, and insiders. 
 
In the 1920’s to 1950’s traders like Jesse Livermore and Richard D. Wyckoff used terms 
like the “composite man” and the “big operator” to describe smart money.  
The Philosophy was, “follow the big money, not the crowd.” 
 
As Futures markets grew in both size and complexity, the demand for better oversight 
also increased. Fast forward to 1962, the government released the first monthly COT 
report, providing the public a more regular look into how traders were positioned.  
 
The Commitments of Traders (COT) report began with the simple goal to shed light on 
who was truly participating in the U.S. Futures markets. 
 
There were three categories: Commercial hedgers, Non-Commercial (Large-Specula-
tors), and Non-Reportables (Small-Speculators). You’ll learn about how they all operate 
throughout this book.
Below is an example of the COT detailed report—these reports are published weekly by 
the CFTC and cover all major Futures contracts.
In 1969, a young Futures trader named Larry Williams was shown a copy of the weekly 
COT tables by an older floor veteran, Bill Meehan. What struck him was the clear split 
between commercial hedgers—the companies that actually produced or consumed the 
commodity—and the small speculators who often chased prices without inside knowl-
edge.  
 
Williams realized that if you could see when commercials were quietly loading up during 
sell-offs and lightening up into rallies, you had a real-time map of smart-money behavior. 
 
4
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
I  N  T  R  O  D  U  C  T  I  O  N


===== PAGE 15 =====
Larry began publishing his COT research in print in 1970, writing brief notes about this 
idea in his market newsletter and in Commodities magazine, coining what he called the 
COT Index—a simple percentage scale showing how today’s commercial net position 
ranked against its one-year range. A high reading indicated that commercials were  
historically long (bullish); a low reading indicated that commercials were historically short 
(bearish).
The concept was radical in 1970 because almost no one outside grain companies and 
regulators paid attention to the report. Through the 1970s and early ’80s, Williams  
refined the index, mailing weekly chart packets to students in his mail-order trading 
courses. Then in 1987, he cemented his reputation by turning $10,000 into $1.1 million 
(an 11,376 % gain) to win the Robbins World Cup Futures Championship—using, among 
other tools, his COT work to time major commodity swings.
Trading With The Insiders
Then in the 2000’s, the COT became a weekly publication, released every Friday, 
showing positions as of the prior Tuesday. This made the report timely enough to be 
useful for active traders. It wasn’t until 2005 that Larry gathered his research into a 
comprehensive book, Trade Stocks & Commodities with the Insiders, with the subti-
tle “Secrets of the COT Report.” This book gave ordinary traders a framework to follow 
institutional footprints while trading against the emotional crowd. At LTA, we build on this 
legacy. 
 
Whenever traders speak about “smart money vs. dumb money,” they are—knowingly or 
not—drawing on the path Larry Williams first mapped out more than fifty years ago. 
 
The COT report has continued to evolve and has become a core dataset for economists, 
hedge funds, and retail traders alike. Today, it remains one of the most trusted ways to 
measure what large players are doing behind the scenes—offering a glimpse into how 
capital is truly positioned across the global futures markets. The COT report marked the 
beginning of “quantifying” smart money.
The "Smart Money vs. Dumb Money" narrative became mainstream as retail trading 
platforms and online forums gained popularity. 
• Smart money = institutions, insiders, hedge funds, COT commercials. 
 
• Dumb money = retail traders, news-driven buyers, technical chasers. 
The term began appearing in numerous trading books, forums, and early blogs as  
traders searched for ways to track institutional behavior.
LTA traders don't study this history as trivia; we do it to gain valuable market intelligence. 
These patterns repeat, and those who understand the past can anticipate the future.

From 2010 onwards, "smart money" became a term used in social media clickbait and 
lost its original meaning. And then SMC went viral—marketed as "the hidden secrets.” 
With the rise of Trading Platforms, YouTube, and retail education, "smart money" took on 
many meanings. The problem? Most people teaching it are unaware of the REAL smart 
money.  
 
It’s like that old kid's game called “Telephone,” where kids sit in a circle, and one whis-
pers into another’s ear, and by the time it reaches the last person, it loses all meaning.
The Truth About The Trading Industry
The retail trading industry is growing by 20% year over year, and with that growth comes 
a flood of traders, all following the same watered-down mainstream strategies.  
Yes—many of these are valid standalone technical tools. But without a data-backed  
narrative, they collapse. They’re empty without context. 
 
Meanwhile, REAL smart money tracking includes COT positioning, Open Interest  
(Futures), Volume Analytics, Market Profile / Auction Theory, Sentiment vs.  
Positioning Divergence, Liquidity-Engineering and more.  
 
This is the foundation of LTA 2.0. Traders like Jason Shapiro (popularised in "Unknown 
Market Wizards") and Peter Brandt use similar sentiment and positioning models to track 
when retail is trapped and institutions are accumulating.  
 
If you're not using data to support your technicals, you're not trading with smart money. 
And even though COT data is available to the public, most retail traders don’t use it, and 
fewer understand it. 
 
That’s your edge. That’s our edge. By becoming an LTA Trader—you’ll unlock the  
data-driven narrative behind the moves. You’ll learn how to read it, interpret it, and trade 
it with precision key levels. This book isn’t written for the herd; it’s written for those who 
want facts over feelings. And the truth is, most retail traders want comforting lies, soft 
pillows to rest their emotions on at night. 
 
But the trading game is war. It’s player vs player—a zero-sum game. For you to win, 
someone else has to lose. Sound ruthless? It is, but the market doesn’t care about your 
feelings. You must learn to see with intense realism.  
 
Since you’ve read this far, you’re not like the others. They skimmed this page, rushed 
ahead, or quit reading altogether. You’re still here.  
 
Welcome to LTA, and cheers to new beginnings.

E X P A N D I N G  T H E  E D G E
T H E  S T O R Y  B E H I N D  L T A  C O N C E P T S
I  
spent years obsessing over technical analysis, including chart patterns, candlesticks, 
and market structure. And I was sharp. I could break down a chart with precision, find 
the imbalance, spot the setup, and time the entry.
Like most traders, I thought the edge came from mastering the chart. If a trade failed, I 
assumed it was because I didn’t read the structure cleanly enough, or missed a  
confluence, or entered too early. So, I doubled down and went deeper into the  
technicals. Better execution. Better entries. Better discipline.
But over time, something didn’t add up. Everything would be aligned, all the signals 
green, a textbook setup. And then, the market would nuke the other way. Not just a  
minor shakeout but a complete reversal. At first, I chalked it up to probabilities. No setup 
is ever guaranteed. Right? But these weren’t just losses; they were contradictions.
They were telling me I was missing vital information.
That’s when it hit me.
The problem wasn’t with my technicals. No, I was looking for answers in the wrong 
place. The chart gave me the setup, but it didn’t explain why. It didn’t show me the data 
behind the charts, the hidden levels. It didn’t reveal how institutions were positioned, 
how risk sentiment was shifting, or how a fundamental catalyst could flip the entire  
landscape.
That realization became the seed for what grew into LTA Concepts 2.0.
8


===== PAGE 19 =====
Initially, LTA focused on execution. I provided traders with a clean, structured execu-
tion framework and demonstrated how to apply it with precision. That was the mission, 
and it worked. Hundreds of traders shared their testimonials. The entries became  
cleaner, the exits better, and the confidence stronger.
But over time, I noticed something. Many traders got tunnel-visioned. They applied the 
execution models on one asset only and used the strategy the same way in every  
market condition.
They didn’t understand why some trades worked like magic, while others completely 
failed. They weren’t reading sentiment or accounting for macro forces or considering 
where we were in the broader cycle.
They were trading in a vacuum.
Even with higher timeframe analysis, they lacked context. That’s when I realized  
something had to change. Not because the original framework was wrong but because 
too many traders missed the bigger picture.
I expected they would understand the context. Hoping they would execute with  
awareness. But most were so focused on perfecting the setup they forgot to ask: 
Should I even look for this setup right now?
That’s when I paused the YouTube livestreams and took a step back. I went all-in with 
my private community. It was time to build something more complete for them. We dove 
deep. I showed them how to read COT data, and we studied how the big players  
positioned themselves and how retail traders often served as the contrarian signal.
We examined how seasonality influenced market rhythm, how valuation created  
asymmetry, and how macro cycles dictated when risk-on became risk-off.
Everything clicked for them.
They knew which assets to trade and when. They knew when to be a contrarian and 
when to ride momentum. They didn’t just trade technical setups; they saw the market 
with X-ray vision.
This is what LTA Concepts 2.0 is all about. Information, preparation, and precision. It’s 
about evolving from a trader who reacts to price into a strategist who understands why 
the price is moving.
The original gave you the tools to execute.
LTA 2.0 shows you the truth behind the charts.

T H E  F O U N D A T I O N
T H E  F I V E  P I L L A R S  O F  M A R K E T  A N A L Y S I S
W
hen I was building this framework, I didn’t set out to create five pillars.  
They revealed themselves naturally through experience. Every mistake, every 
breakthrough, every winning trade or sudden loss pointed me toward something more  
profound. Eventually, those lessons began to form a logical sequence. And that structure 
became the five core pillars of LTA Concepts. 
Pillar 1: Macro Fundamentals
We begin by anchoring our view in macro fundamentals. And when I say fundamentals, 
I’m not talking about news headlines or forecasts. I’m talking about real data.  
Data that gives us a true window into the underlying forces of the market.
The first and most powerful piece of information is the COT data, which reveals the 
positioning of the most prominent market participants, including commercial hedgers and 
large speculators. The REAL Smart Money. And through this lens, we can understand 
the sentiment. Are institutions building long positions? Are retail traders trapped on the 
wrong side of the trade? 
Then comes seasonality. Patterns that repeat month after month, year after year.  
Behavior emerges not by chance but through macrocycles, institutional rebalancing, 
and historical tendencies. Some analysts call it a self-fulfilling prophecy, yet these cycles 
repeat themselves over and over again.
Next is valuation. Are we overvalued or undervalued relative to a correlated asset?  
An indication of whether a market has room to run or is poised to snap back.  
Finally, we layer in key macroeconomic data, including interest rates, inflation, 
employment, and GDP.
Once I understood these elements, I stopped trading what I hoped would happen and 
started aligning with what was unfolding. Not headlines, but the real fundamental  
underlying forces: COT data, seasonality rhythms, valuation extremes, and key  
economic drivers.
Tracking where the big players and retail traders are stacked, identifying when the crowd 
is trapped, and knowing when emotion outweighs logic.
11
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 22 =====
Pillar 2: Macro Technicals
Once the macro narrative is straightforward, I need to know precisely where to place my 
trades. This is where macro technicals come in, specifically supply and demand and 
market structure.
Institutions don’t buy or sell randomly. They accumulate and offload positions in specific 
zones. By identifying these areas on monthly, weekly, and daily charts, I could see where 
real decisions took place.
The market structure helped even more. Was the price trending or consolidating?  
Was momentum building or losing steam? These answers gave me a war map.
I learned to see price through this lens, and I stopped guessing where reversals or  
continuations would happen. I could anticipate them. 
 
Pillar 3: Intra Day Trend 
With the big picture in place, it was time to zoom in. Intra-day trends helped me bridge 
the gap between higher timeframe conviction and what’s happening now. This is where 
the market reveals its short-term intentions—and where timing becomes everything.
I learned to track intraday structure, such as higher highs, lower lows, and areas of con-
solidation, within the larger context. I used trendlines to map momentum and micro-shifts 
in direction.
The best entries rarely occur randomly. They unfold as part of an intraday pattern within 
a macro narrative. This kept me from fighting the trend and helped me sync my entries 
with real-time market behavior.
Pillar 4: Micro Execution
This is where everything comes together. All the context, the macro, and the structure 
have to lead somewhere. And that somewhere is the trade.
Micro execution is what we built LTA 1.0 around. Volume profiles. Hidden key levels. 
Session highs and lows. Entry models. This provided me with the precision of a surgeon.
But unlike before, I was now entering with full awareness of the bigger picture. I knew 
why price was here, who was behind the move, and where the confirmation sat.
Execution went from hopeful to structured, from risky to calculated. We refine our entries 
with intraday execution models, trendline reactions, and liquidity sweeps. I built the setup 
on the full context of everything we’ve already layered in. Institutional and retail position-
ing. Pinpointing supply and demand zones. Timing entries off volume profiles, key levels, 
and market structure.

Pillar 5: Trade Management
All the analysis in the world doesn’t matter if your capital disappears. That’s what trade 
management protects. And to be honest, I think this is the forgotten principle.
Early on, I was obsessed with finding perfect trades. But I learned the hard way that 
even the best setups can fail without strong risk controls. So, I established clear risk 
parameters, including how much to risk per trade, how much exposure to carry, and how 
to size positions. 
I stopped thinking in terms of wins and losses and started thinking in terms of  
risk-adjusted outcomes. Trade management became my anchor. It gave me emotional 
control, sustainability, and, most importantly, long-term consistency.
The Synergy
Each pillar on its own is powerful. But when layered together, they create synergy.  
A setup that aligns across macro, sentiment, seasonality, structure, and execution.

A  M U L T I - L A Y E R E D  A P P R O A C H
I 
did over 400 live trading streams on YouTube. When I look back, I see that the biggest 
mistake most traders make is thinking that one tool or one chart is enough.
It’s like trying to drive at night with only one headlight. You can see part of the road 
ahead, but most of it remains hidden. And it’s what you don’t see that hurts you the most.
I saw traders obsess over technical setups alone, asking, “buy or sell” convinced that 
a clean pattern guaranteed success. Others would live and breathe economic reports, 
thinking that one inflation print or one rate hike would define the trend. Risking big on 
news trades. Some would latch onto sentiment, blindly following positioning data without 
understanding where price mattered.
They were looking at the market through a narrow window with tunnel vision. And the 
markets punished them for it. The price doesn’t move for a single reason.  It moves 
when multiple forces align. Fundamentals, sentiment, seasonality, valuation, technical 
structure, liquidity, risk flows. They weave together to create the conditions for a real 
move.
That’s why professional traders think differently. They layer information and stack  
confirmations. When enough pieces of the puzzle come together, they take action.
Like a lion patiently waiting to strike its prey.
LTA Concepts is a Decision-Making System
You start by scanning the environment. Macro first. Then sentiment. Then seasonality. 
Then valuation. Then technical confirmation. Then execution. Then risk control.
You filter every trade idea through that lens. You don’t skip steps. You don’t jump the 
gun. You stack evidence in your favor until the path is clear, and sometimes that means 
walking away. At other times, that means stepping in with conviction.
Either way, you trade with logical clarity, not emotion. The market punishes partial  
information. When you think in layers and build every trade on a foundation of real data, 
structure, and probabilities, you stack the odds in your favor.

How To Read This Book?
I wrote LTA Concepts in a logical sequence. Each section is stacked on the next, layer 
by layer. However, Section One was designed so that you can already execute. Read it, 
then go out and test it for yourself. If all you did was read Section One, you could

In Section Two, we start the logical sequence, step by step. Each section will build on 
top of the next. And it’s not just theory; I’ll give you example after example. 
Ultimately, I’ll provide a psychology masterclass, demonstrating how to tie it all together 
with real-world examples of execution and risk management. Trading is WAR, and I’m 
about to supply you with the weapons and strategy.
Stay disciplined and focused, soldier.

1
V O L U M E  I S  K I N G
T H E  B A T T L E  B E G I N S
Burn this next sentence into the mind: “Trading is war, and volume is your map to 
understand the battlefield.” I’m talking about the Volume Profile. I’m going to teach 
you everything you need to know about it. When you use volume correctly, it shows you 
where the battles have happened, where traders showed up to fight, if they retreated, if 
they were slaughtered, or if they stood their ground.
In this first chapter, I’ll show you one of the most powerful weapons in modern trading: 
the Fixed-Range Volume Profile. It reveals the hidden levels of interest, the front lines 
of the war between buyers and sellers, and where value has been accepted or rejected. 
The Origin of Volume Profile
Back in the day, traders would pile into “The Trading Pit” at places like the Chicago 
Board of Trade. It was loud, savage, and intense. They would yell, scream, and wave 
hand signals while frantically writing things down on paper to make a trade.
A trader named J. Peter Steidlmayer noticed something. He realized that most traders 
were only focusing on the price and how high or low it went, but they weren’t paying 
attention to the key levels of interest that lay behind the noise.
In the 1980s, he developed a system called the Market Profile. It was a smart way to 
see how much time the market spent at each price level; he used certain letters to mark 
every 30 minutes of trading and stacked them on top of each other to form shapes. The 
places with the most letters represented the price where the market stalled for a while. 
Based on his theory, this meant that traders agreed on that price and would defend their 
positions.
This helped him understand where traders felt prices were fair (fair value), where prices 
moved quickly (not balanced), and where the market didn’t like a price at all (rejected it).
However, as trading moved to a seamless digital environment, things changed fast. An 
enormous number of trades could occur simultaneously, leading to new discoveries. 
Tracking time alone was no longer enough. The next stage was examining the volume: 
how much volume was traded at each price rather than the length of time it stayed there.
This new perspective turned into the invention of the Volume Profile. And later, as plat-
forms like TradingView emerged, it evolved into something more powerful, known as the 
Fixed-Range Volume Profile. Now, modern traders could pinpoint any area on the chart 
and see exactly how much trading happened at each price in that range.
18
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 29 =====
Why Time Isn’t Enough
When the Market Profile was first introduced, it focused on volume through the lens of 
time. It showed how long price stayed at each level, assuming that the more time spent 
there, the more important the level was. But this doesn’t always hold true. A level might 
be visited often, taking up a lot of time, but still have low volume. Meaning there was no 
real interest.
On the other hand, volume in price tracks how much was traded at each price level, 
regardless of how long price stayed there. A level might have been touched for just a few 
seconds, but with high volume behind it, it became significant as big players stepped in 
and major value was exchanged.
Volume in price gives us a clearer picture of where the market truly cares, and this is 
what makes the Volume Profile such a significant advancement. It filters out the irrele-
vant noise, and reveals where the real battles were fought.
Interest Creates Opportunity
At the end of the day, it’s about what price levels are of interest. To some, the hidden key 
levels almost feel like “magic,” even though they’re just where the most volume happens 
behind the technicals. If no one is interested at that level, then chances are that nothing 
meaningful will happen there again. However, when there’s been clear interest; volume 
represents the real commitment or defense, which is why these levels are so powerful.
High-interest zones indicate where big players are positioned and where they might de-
fend again. These are the price areas where reactions are likely — what I’ve come to call 
“the magic touch,” a term I became known for. The Volume Profile helps us spot these 
zones with a sort of X-ray vision. It reveals the significance behind a price, not just the 
number itself.
Futures vs. CFDs — Reading the Right Map
Now, it’s essential to understand where your volume data originates. Most retail traders 
utilize CFD charts, particularly in the Forex market. CFDs are decentralized, and the 
data you see is specific to your broker. That means two traders that use two different 
brokers might see completely different volume on the same trading pair.
But in the Futures market, everything is centralized. One exchange. One feed. One 
unified record. Everyone sees the same levels, the same volume, and the same reac-
tions. That’s why professional traders use Futures data to make decisions. It’s more 
trustworthy, clear, and accurate.
However, even if you’re trading CFDs, you can still use the Futures chart as your refer-
ence point. That’s what we do: we use centralized volume from the Futures markets to 
read the battlefield and then execute trades on the CFD chart.
If you’re trading something like gold, oil, indices, or major pairs like EUR/USD or AUD/
USD, anything with a Futures equivalent, you should always reference the Futures data 
when available.

The Power of Fixed Range Volume
With Fixed-Range Volume, you can lock in a custom segment of price action and see 
where volume built up, where it vanished, and where the battle lines were drawn.
By removing the guesswork, you’ll see if your horizontal support and resistance lines 
matter. If a level has high volume, it means the market cared. If it has none, it didn’t. This 
enables you to make data-driven decisions and prioritize facts over emotions.
That’s why we say, “Volume is KING.”
Fixed Range Volume Profile – The Essentials
With the Fixed Range Volume Profile, we utilize concepts such as Row Size and Value 
Area Volume to gain intel into volume distribution within specific price ranges.
Row Size refers to the width of each price level within the volume profile histogram,  
determining the quality of volume analysis. Smaller row sizes offer finer detail, while  
larger sizes provide a broader overview.
Value Area Volume represents the total volume traded within the Value Area, the price 
range where a significant portion of trading activity occurs, typically accounting for 70-
80% of the total volume. This metric helps you gauge market sentiment and identify 
areas of significant trading interest or acceptance, which can influence future price 
movements.

PoC (Point of Control) | Standard 
 
The Point of Control is the price level within the volume profile where the highest volume 
of trading activity has occurred. It's the peak of the volume distribution curve. This level 
signifies the price point that has attracted the most buying and selling interest from mar-
ket participants within the analyzed range and time period.

VaH (Value Area High) | Standard 
 
This refers to the highest price level within the value area. The value area is typically 
defined as the price range where the majority of trading volume has occurred. In other 
words, VaH represents the upper boundary of this range, beyond which trading volume 
decreased significantly.
VaL (Value Area Low) | Standard 
 
Conversely, the Value Area Low represents the lowest price level within the value area. It 
is the lower boundary of the price range where the majority of trading volume has oc-
curred. Similar to VaH, VaL indicates a point where trading volume starts to drop signifi-
cantly below this level.

High Volume Node 
 
A High Volume Node in a fixed range volume profile indicates a price level or range with 
significant trading volume. It's a key area where many traders have bought or sold  
assets, often serving as support or resistance levels. Traders closely monitor these 
nodes to anticipate potential buying or selling opportunities and to understand market 
dynamics.
Low Volume Node 
 
In the context of fixed range volume profiling, a "Low Volume Node" refers to a price 
level or range on a chart where relatively little trading volume has occurred compared 
to the surrounding areas. These nodes represent zones where there has been a lack of 
significant buying or selling activity. 
 
A Low Volume Node can indicate a point of equilibrium or indifference in the market 
where neither buyers nor sellers have shown strong interest. Traders often view these 
areas as potential areas of interest for future price movements.

U
nderneath every chart is a battlefield where buyers and sellers are fighting for control 
over the key levels. The Fixed Range Volume Profile helps you see where these 
battles happen by mapping trading activity to specific price levels rather than time-based 
volume.
Unlike traditional volume indicators that track volume across sessions, the Volume

• Point of Control, or POC, is the price level at which the highest volume was traded.
• Value Area High and Value Area Low, is the range that holds 70% of total volume.

of where the real battles have already been fought.

===== PAGE 37 =====
Why The Volume Profile is a Game-Changer
The Volume Profile is built on real trading activity, not just price patterns. Static support 
and resistance lines based only on past highs and lows often miss the whole picture. 
However, the Volume Profile reveals where the real battle between buyers and sellers 
occurred.
• POC levels often act like magnets, drawing price back to them.
• Value Areas indicate where the market strikes a balance between buyers and sellers.
• Low-volume areas are often characterized by rapid price movement, as they have 
less liquidity.
When you combine The Volume Profile with Supply and Demand zones, you are  
using both price behavior and transaction data to guide your decisions. That kind of  
combination removes a lot of the guesswork.
Key Components of Volume Profile
The Point of Control, or POC, is one of the most important concepts. It is the price level 
where the most volume was traded within a given session or period. When the price is 
above the POC, it often acts as a support when retested. When price is below, it often 
acts as resistance. If price is hovering around the POC, it suggests a balanced market 
with no clear directional bias.
For example, if the S&P 500 has a POC at $4,500 and price is trading above it, a pull-
back to that level could offer a strong long opportunity. If price is below it, that same level  
becomes an area where sellers might step in.
27
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
V  O  L  U  M  E    A  N  A  L  Y  S  I  S
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
POC RETEST

===== PAGE 38 =====
The Value Areas represent the zone where about 70 percent of all volume occurred. 
The upper boundary is called the Value Area High, or VAH, and the lower boundary is 
called the Value Area Low, or VAL.
• If price remains within the Value Area, the market is in balance. 
• If price breaks outside the Value Area, it can signal a breakout or the start of  
a new trend.
High Volume Nodes, or HVNs, are clusters of heavy trading activity. These areas often 
become strong support or resistance zones. When price revisits an HVN, you can  
usually expect a reaction, either a bounce or a stall, as traders defend those levels.
Low Volume Nodes, or LVNs, are the opposite. They are price zones where minimal 
trading took place. Because liquidity is thin, price tends to move quickly through these 
areas. A move into an LVN often means price will rapidly seek out the next high-volume 
area.
For example, if Bitcoin enters a low-volume area around $40,000 and there are no 
strong sellers, it will likely continue rising quickly until it encounters resistance at the next 
HVN, around $41,500.

The Volume Profile Confirms Supply & Demand Zones
If a Supply Zone aligns with a High Volume Node, that area becomes a powerful wall of 
resistance. If a Demand Zone lines up with a Low Volume Node, price might rip through 
it quickly before finding balance again.
Imagine EUR/USD showing a daily Demand Zone at 1.1125 and the Volume Profile 
showing a High Volume Node around 1.1090. Instead of expecting an immediate bounce 
at 1.1125, you prepare for price to move deeper into the range, reacting around the  
real area of institutional activity.
Key takeaways from The Volume Profile:
• The POC levels often act as magnets for price.
• Value Areas indicate where the market is in balance.
• HVNs are zones of stability and reaction.
• LVNs are zones of fast movement.
• Combining The Volume Profile with Supply and Demand Zones gives you a massive 
edge in spotting high-probability trade setups.

T H E  W E E K L Y  C Y C L E
T H E  R H Y T H M  B E H I N D  T H E  M O V E S
M
arkets do not move randomly. There is a rhythm to price action that repeats itself  
every single week. Large institutions, hedge funds, and commercial traders build 
their strategies around these weekly structures, creating patterns that can be observed 
and used by those who know where to look.
Each trading week tends to follow a flow. The opening sets the initial tone, midweek 
reveals the true trend, and the Friday close often hints at what is likely to happen next. 
Understanding this natural cycle helps you avoid getting caught in emotional reactions 
and instead align with the broader forces driving the market.
Institutions do not measure their decisions based on minute-to-minute changes. They  
focus on weekly structures, monitoring the previous week’s highs, lows, and closing 
prices to frame their trades. By adopting this same perspective, you step away from ran-
domness and move into a rhythm that has been repeated week after week for decades.
Why mastering the weekly cycle is essential:
• Institutions structure trades around the previous week's highs, lows, and closes.
• Understanding the Development of the Weekly Candle. 
• Early-week moves, especially Monday and Tuesday, often serve as traps before the 
proper trend forms.
• Liquidity tends to flow toward last week's extreme levels before reversing.
• Major economic data release midweek and often drive the real directional moves.
This simple understanding changes how you approach the market. Instead of reacting 
to every movement, you learn to wait, observe, and act only when the real players show 
their hand.
31


===== PAGE 42 =====
The Sunday Open (SO)
The Sunday Open, or Monday Open depending on your time zone, becomes the first 
significant event of the week. It sets the liquidity positioning for the days ahead. You 
should watch closely for gaps up or down, noting whether price fills them quickly or  
continues in the gap's direction. Early aggressive pushes reveal institutional intent. 
How price reacts during the transitions between the Asian, London, and New York  
sessions around the Open can offer early signs of the market’s true sentiment.
It’s important to recognize that institutions frequently utilize the Sunday Open to create 
traps. This frequent setup involves price opening beyond last week’s low or high,  
sparking emotional reactions from retail traders.
For instance, gold might open below last week’s low on Sunday evening. Panicked 
sellers flood into the market, only to be absorbed by institutional buyers waiting for that 
exact moment. By Monday’s New York session, gold had reclaimed last week’s low, 
confirming that the early move was simply a liquidity grab. Those early sellers become 
trapped liquidity, fueling the move higher.
How institutions capitalize: 
• Previous Week’s High and Low, serving as magnets for liquidity. 
• Early-Week fakeouts, especially on Monday and Tuesday, lure impatient traders. 
• Prior Week’s Close, acting as the broader directional anchor for the new week.

How Price Moves Throughout the Week
As the week progresses, the flow of price tends to follow a reliable rhythm. Monday and 
Tuesday are where traps and fakeouts dominate. Impatient traders often get caught 
chasing early moves that reverse against them. By Wednesday and Thursday, institu-
tions have begun solidifying their positions. These midweek sessions typically coincide 
with significant economic releases, such as inflation figures or central bank updates, 
which add momentum to the actual move. Friday becomes the day of unwinding.  
Institutions take profits and reduce risk exposure ahead of the weekend, giving important 
clues about next week's potential continuation or reversal.
How to trade the weekly cycle: 
• Watch the Sunday Open for early liquidity grabs and institutional footprints. 
• Track last week’s highs and lows, as they are natural magnets for price action. 
• Stay cautious early in the week and look for midweek confirmation before committing.
• Analyze the Friday close to gauge sentiment and potential direction into next week.
To make navigating the weekly cycle more practical, we use the LTA Concepts -  
Session Breaks and Sunday Open Indicator™  on TradingView. This tool automatical-
ly marks the Sunday Open and shows daily session breaks. It reveals liquidity shifts in 
real-time and helps you stay aligned with where institutions are operating.

T
he market moves in patterns, and those patterns often repeat over time. Those who 
recognize these recurring structures gain an edge by anticipating price reactions 
before they occur. One of the most effective ways to analyze market structure is through 
the Weekly Volume Profile, a method that reveals where institutional traders are  
positioning themselves.
Instead of relying only on price movements, the Weekly Volume Profile highlights 
where the most buying and selling occurred in the previous trading week. It displays high 
probability support and resistance zones based on actual trading activity, while offering a 
broader and more strategic view of market positioning.
We break it down into three key segments:
• Early Previous Week, or EPW VAH, POC, and VAL. 
• Previous Week, or PW VAH, POC, and VAL. 
• Current Week, or CW VAH, POC, and VAL.
Each of these levels reveals the market structure and institutional behavior, helping you 
refine your entries and exits.
36


===== PAGE 47 =====
The Early Previous Week profile focuses on a trading week before the previous trading 
week, capturing early market sentiment and positioning. This matters because it reveals 
where early institutional positioning occurred, identifies early support and resistance  
levels, and often signals where price might return before the next move.
For example, if gold's EPW POC is at $3,926 and price moves above it, you may look 
for a retest of this level as support. If price remains below it, that same level could act as 
resistance.
The Previous Week profile captures the full activity of the last week. It shows where 
institutions engaged the most with the market and has been proven to be reactive due to 
the relevancy and importance of the data from the previous trading week. 
• It provides a strong reference point for price. 
• It acts as a major reaction zone when revisited. 
• It helps gauge future price direction based on the previous week's dominant levels.
Imagine the S&P 500 has a PW VAL at $5,935. If price approaches that level from 
above, you may expect a bounce if buyers step in. If price breaks below it, it signals that 
weakness is likely to continue.

hink of a marketplace where thousands of traders are buying and selling every

===== PAGE 52 =====
Daily volume levels are crucial for short-term trading because they help identify where 
price is most likely to react.
Most traders are tempted to use the current day levels too early, but that can be  
dangerous. Early in the session, there is not enough trustworthy data. Using Current  
Day levels too soon can lead to poor decisions and false signals. The Volume Profile 
becomes more useful after a full session has closed. That’s why intelligent traders rely 
on Previous Day (PD) and Early Previous Day (EPD) levels for more stability.
PD and EPD levels each have their role. You can use PD levels when the market is 
trending clearly, reacting cleanly to the POC, VAH, or VAL. If the session is stable and no 
major overnight events have occurred, PD levels tend to dominate.
However, when there is a major overnight movement or when institutional traders are 
active outside normal hours, EPD levels become much more important. In trading,  
overnight sessions leave footprints that traders can use.
There are numerous ways to identify high-probability setups when the Volume Profile 
is used correctly. If price is above the PD VAH, it signals strength and buying opportuni-
ties. If price is below the PD VAL, it signals weakness and potential for selling. If price  
hovers near the PD POC, the market is balanced and likely to stay choppy until a  
breakout occurs.
EPD levels add even more depth. They show where large traders were active overnight. 
If price touches an EPD level and quickly moves away, it means institutions are defend-
ing that level. If price breaks an EPD level with strong volume, momentum often follows 
in that direction.

ometimes, markets consolidate, build energy, and prepare for a major move. In these

play.
Fixed Profiles, also known as Fixed Ranges, are structure-based. They help identify 
key levels within areas of consolidation, accumulation, or distribution, regardless of how 
much time has passed.
The difference between time-based and structure-based profiles:
• Time-based profiles, such as PD and EPD, reset automatically with every session 
and capture short-term liquidity zones and institutional positioning.
• Fixed Profiles, on the other hand, are manually selected to capture a larger structur-
al story, showing where the market has been building pressure before a breakout.
When the market is trending, PD and EPD levels work well. But when price enters a 
range, Fixed Ranges offer much deeper intelligence. They help identify breakout 
points, reveal whether buyers or sellers are accumulating, and set the roadmap for the 
next expansion.
Fixed Ranges become essential when a clear area of consolidation is present. Not all 
markets trend smoothly. Often, price moves sideways for an extended period as big 
players either build or unload positions. Understanding whether the market is  
accumulating for an uptrend or distributing before a downtrend becomes crucial.
Inside a Fixed Range, price usually does one of two things: 
• Accumulation, where smart buyers load up before a breakout higher.
• Distribution, where large sellers quietly exit before price drops.
Macro structure, liquidity behavior, and volume confluence help traders distinguish  
between the two. Once the range breaks, fixed levels such as the Fixed POC, Fixed 
VAH, and Fixed VAL offer clear entry and exit points.
46
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 57 =====
When price breaks out of a Fixed Range, two patterns usually appear:
• CERC stands for Consolidation, Expansion, Retracement, and Continuation.  
This pattern is clean. Price breaks out, retraces into the consolidation area, confirms 
the breakout, and then continues trending.
• CME stands for Consolidation, Manipulation, and Expansion. In this case, price 
fakes a move out of the range, often into one of our key levels, to trap traders before 
breaking out in the real direction.
Determining whether a market is likely to behave with CERC or CME depends on macro 
structure, liquidity positioning, and the reactions around Fixed Levels.

Fixed Ranges have clear phases you can expect:
• Consolidation, where price moves sideways and volume builds.
• Expansion, where price breaks out of the range.
• Retracement, where price returns to retest critical volume zones.
• Continuation, where price moves away from the range cleanly.
Example 1: Fixed Range POC (Fixed PoC)
The Fixed POC inside a range marks the highest volume area. It often acts as a pivot 
when price returns for a retest after the breakout.
For example, if a chart forms a clear consolidation zone. The Fixed POC sits at the  
center where most volume has been built. If price breaks below the range and later  
retests the Fixed POC successfully, that offers a clean short entry with solid risk-reward.

Fixed VAH marks the upper volume boundary. Following the breakout, a retracement 
into the Fixed VAH offers a high-probability continuation setup.

Key takeaways for mastering Fixed Profiles:
• Fixed Ranges are structure-based and help spot accumulation and distribution.
• Macro structure and volume confluence are critical for identifying breakout direction.
• Fixed levels such as POC, VAH, and VAL offer breakout retest opportunities.
• Recognizing CERC and CME patterns increases trade accuracy around ranges.
• Combining Fixed Profiles with PD, EPD, and higher timeframe market structure  
provides a comprehensive market view.

S W I N G  V O L U M E  P R O F I L E
M O M E N T U M - B A S E D  A P P R O A C H
I
n this chapter, we’ll cover the Swing Profiles — one of the most effective ways to  
trade within an aggressive trend. For those moments when the market is pushing 
relentlessly, making higher highs or lower lows without giving you a clean retracement 
into a previously established zone.
When price doesn’t revisit yesterday’s level or the previous week’s structure, we are not 
going to sit and wait. Instead, we’ll adapt and build structure from the most recent leg of 
momentum itself. That’s where the Swing Profile becomes essential.
Even in strong trends, there’s often a small intraday or intra-session retracement. That 
minor pause, the quick dip or pop before continuation is the space where Swing POC, 
Swing VAH, and Swing VAL live. When you understand how to read those levels, you 
gain the ability to enter into trending moves with precision.
You’ll see these concepts again in the next chapter when I show you the Entry  
Execution Models, especially Entry Model 2, which is built around using these swing 
levels as confirmation triggers.
You don’t always get a textbook retest of your Daily or Weekly anchor. However, price 
does respect structure formed within the swing. That’s where you set your alerts, watch 
behavior, and strike when the market gives you confirmation.
I’ll show you three core examples:
52
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 63 =====
Example 1: Swing Point of Control (Swing POC)
Let’s say we’ve been trending down. You take the Swing High (highest point including 
the wick) and the Swing Low (lowest point including the wick), and build your volume 
profile between those two points.
You’ll often see price reacting to both the Swing VAL and Swing POC. But it’s the Swing 
POC — the level with the highest volume — that often becomes the turning point.  
This is where the market regains its footing and the trend resumes.
Example 2: Swing Value Area High (Swing VAH)
In a trending market, the Swing VAH often acts like a ceiling — until it doesn’t. In our 
example, the Swing VAH becomes the level where price finds support after a pullback, 
and then takes off again.
When price reclaims the VAH and holds above it, it signals continuation.  
This level becomes your trigger — not just for entries, but for strategic alerts.

Imagine the market is falling, then consolidates. It pulls back and tags the VAL — but 
can’t push through. That rejection tells you momentum is still down.
Sometimes you’ll even see liquidity stacking just below, which hints that price is about to 
continue in the original direction.
Using Swing Profiles for Entry Execution
These Swing Profile levels, POC, VAH, and VAL aren’t just reference points. In our  
system, they play a critical role in trade execution, especially for Entry Model 2, which  
is our confirmation-based entry model.
This model focuses on using price reactions around these Swing levels to confirm  
directional bias before entry. If price respects the Swing POC or reclaims the VAH, that’s 
your cue. The volume tells you where the market has already shown its hand.
These levels are the trigger mechanism for real trades, used actively by LTA traders 
inside live execution environments. We’ll explore this in more depth in the next chapter, 
where Entry Model 2 is broken down step by step, showing how Swing Profiles can be 
used for real-time confirmation entries on the micro timeframes.

8
E X E C U T I O N 
M I C R O  E N T R Y  M O D E L S
T
o put it simply, markets move because of a constant battle between buyers and sell-
ers. Every time price reaches a key level, these two forces fight for control. If buyers 
win, price moves up. If sellers win, price moves down. Professional trading is not about 
placing random entries and hoping to be right. It's about reading this battle correctly.
Think of it this way, the market is like an auction house. Prices rise when there are more 
buyers than sellers and fall when selling pressure takes over. Understanding this auction 
process is what separates informed traders from those who rely on luck.
Every execution model in this chapter is designed to help you identify the signals of  
control before entering a trade. By combining price action with volume profile data, we 
can determine which side has the upper hand and execute trades with confidence. 
• When price tests a key level, the battle between buyers and sellers intensifies.
• If buyers hold the level and sellers fail to push lower, rejection wicks form, one of the 
key signals in our Entry Model 1.
• The third candle confirms control. If buyers dominate, price moves up. If sellers take 
over, price moves down.
This structured approach removes uncertainty and aligns entries with the volume profile 
and macro bias so you can step into the market with confidence.
A structured entry process is crucial for long-term success. It removes emotions and 
makes your trading more systematic.
56


===== PAGE 67 =====
The benefits of having a systematic entry process:
• Clear stop loss placement.
• Efficient risk management.
• Objective decision making.
• Easier tracking and analysis for improvement.
Following a defined process enables you to execute precisely and adapt based on  
feedback rather than emotional reactions.
Entry Model One:  
The Double Wick Confirmation (Medium/Higher Time Frame)
In trading, certain strategies and techniques are utilized to maximize profits and mini-
mize losses. One such strategy is the Double Wick Confirmation, which is a strategy we 
look for when the price reaches a key level such as POC (Point of Control), VAH (Value 
Area High), or VAL (Value Area Low). 
 
The Double Wick Confirmation forms under specific conditions. In this example, price 
approaches from below. The body of a candle touches a point of interest, flips down-
ward, and closes, leaving behind a wick. Following this, price wicks upward again and 
flips once more. 
 
This flipping action is a crucial signal. After the second candle closure and flip, we look 
to enter the market with our stops strategically placed above the wick or a high from the 
past. This preventive measure is taken to protect our assets and avoid unnecessary 
losses.
 
Our goal after entering the market is to seek a continuation upwards or downwards.  
We aim to target levels of liquidity or any of our previous discussed key levels, which  
are typically areas where large volumes of trades have been executed.
57
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
V  O  L  U  M  E    A  N  A  L  Y  S  I  S
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
EM1


===== PAGE 68 =====
Entry Model Two:  
Internal Swing Confirmation (Medium/Lower Time Frame)
The internal swing confirmation entry is a strategy that requires patience and strategic 
timing. The first step in this process is to wait for the swing to form at one of our key 
levels. These key levels include the Point of Control (POC), Value Area Low (VAL), and 
Value Area High (VAH).
 
The formation of the swing at these levels signals a potential opportunity for us to take 
advantage of market movements. With the LTF (Lower Time Frame) Swing in place, we 
then apply the volume profile to the structure that has been created. This gives us a de-
tailed picture of trading activity, providing insight into the market's direction and strength.
 
This is how we can internally develop the LTF Swings such as the LTF PoC, LTF VaH, 
LTF VaL.
 
The next stage of the process is to monitor the prices and wait for them to return to 
these key levels. This is where our patience truly pays off. With prices back at the key 
levels, we can then employ the double wick entry strategy, but this time, on the Lower 
Timeframe. This strategy involves entering the market after the price has tested a level 
twice and has shown signs of reversing.
 
The internal swing confirmation entry is a process that involves waiting for a swing to 
form, applying the volume profile, and using the double wick entry strategy to enhance 
our trading position, giving us the extra confirmation we need in order to enter a position.

===== PAGE 69 =====
Entry Model Three:  
Confirmation of Internal Structure (Higher, Medium, and Lower Timeframe)
The confirmation of internal structure is a potent strategy, which is extensively used in 
trading to leverage the power of volume. In this approach, it is imperative that mitiga-
tion of one of our key levels (POC, VAH, VAL, etc.) and occurs as a first step before we 
engage.
Mitigation refers to the process of reducing the potential impact or seriousness of the 
key levels. This step is crucial as it sets the stage for the following trading activities and it 
often precedes a significant reversal in the market.
The next critical stage in this approach occurs if the internal structure is broken. The 
internal structure refers to the low/high levels that mitigated our point of interest. If these 
levels are broken, it signals a potential shift in the market, and we look to participate in 
the break. Participating in the break allows us to place ourselves strategically in the mar-
ket, calling it a momentum play. Throughout your testing phase, you will likely observe 
the following pattern repeatedly.
The process in question involves three main steps: consolidation, manipulation, and 
expansion.
59
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
V  O  L  U  M  E    A  N  A  L  Y  S  I  S
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
After the break of the high that initiated the manipulation 
to the key level, we anticipate a continuation to the upside
Previous Day PoC
EM3
Consolidation
Manipulation
Expansion


===== PAGE 70 =====
The first step of the process is consolidation. This occurs either below or above a 
key level, which serves as an important reference point for the operations that follow.  
At this stage, the main objective is to bring together different elements to create a strong 
foundation that will support the subsequent stages of the process.
 
The second step is manipulation. This step involves creating a false breakout into our 
initial key level, which could be the Point of Control (PoC), Value Area High (VaH), Value 
Area Low (VaL), or Sunday Open, for instance. The goal of this manipulation is to create 
a sense of movement or activity that might not necessarily reflect the true state of the 
market. This can often result in a shift in market sentiment or behavior, which can then 
be leveraged in the next step of the process.
 
The final step is expansion. Coming after the manipulation, this phase involves a 
larger movement in the direction opposite to the initial breakout. This is a strategic move 
designed to take advantage of the manipulated market sentiment and to secure a  
favorable position. 
This method, involving consolidation, manipulation, and expansion, ensures that liquid-
ity is taken from both sides before moving away from the initial key level. It's a strategy 
that's crafted to maximize gains while minimizing potential risks, and occurs again and 
again on the lower timeframes.
Entry Model Four:  
Continuation Higher (Higher, Medium, and Lower Timeframe)
This entry model focuses on continuation setups that often follow liquidity traps. These 
traps are powerful tools used by the market to shake out early traders by stopping them 
out, only to reverse the price direction aggressively. It’s a common scenario: price  
touches a well-known level, such as a Previous Day Point of Control (PD PoC), Weekly 
Value Area High (VaH), or Value Area Low (VaL), and then snaps back with force. Many 
traders fall into this trap by entering too soon. What we look for instead is confirmation.
 
Once price taps into the level and flips, the key is not to act immediately, but to observe. 
The confirmation we wait for is what we call a candle flip. A candle opens in the  
opposite direction, possibly creating a small wick, and then turns around, flipping back 
in line with the original trend. This flip confirms control — showing us who really holds 
power at that level. That’s the moment we act.
We enter not on the trap candle, but on the next one that confirms the flip. This gives us 
the upper hand, because by then the market has shown its hand. Our stop loss is placed 
just beneath the wick of the flip candle. By doing so, we’re not just speculating — we’re 
entering with precision, using structure and volume to guide our decision.
60
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
V  O  L  U  M  E    A  N  A  L  Y  S  I  S
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

===== PAGE 71 =====
This setup, however, comes with a critical caveat. Your bias must already be  
well-defined before you even think about entering. It’s not like Entry Model One, where 
the setup gives you a hint of what’s next. In Entry Model Four, your expectation needs 
to be clear beforehand. Are you expecting price to go higher or lower? Are you watching 
it come into support or resistance? If your bias is off, you’re likely to read the flip wrong 
and enter against the true move.
 
The core pattern we’re looking for is a three-candle-bar play. The first candle touches 
the level and shows some hesitation. The second candle begins to flip. The third candle 
confirms the direction. This sequencing matters. It’s what turns a vague idea into a  
tradable confirmation. You need to be able to read it with confidence. If you see price 
tapping into a key level — maybe from the other side — and you’re bearish, that level 
might be holding support. Your job then is to watch for that break, and ride the  
continuation, because that’s where you’ll find the edge.
 
This model works across the board: higher timeframes for position entries, medium 
timeframes for structure plays, and lower timeframes for refined precision setups. It’s a 
versatile entry model that teaches patience, timing, and the importance of reading how 
price reacts, not just where it lands.

Entry Model Examples
Example 1: Previous Weekly VaH 1H Bounce
We have a bullish price structure and are trending upward on the hourly chart. After 
marking out the Previous Weekly Range, the price pulls back into the Previous Weekly 
Value Area High (PW VaH). The high volume nodes around the VaH act as a potential 
support zone. This is our first clue for a potential bounce.
However, this isn’t guaranteed. That’s why we wait for confirmation using one of our  
entry models. In this case, two entry models are possible: one on the hourly and one  
on the 15-minute timeframe.
Focusing on the hourly timeframe, when you reach the VaH, it's critical for the first can-
dle closure to demonstrate a bullish formation, ideally with a long bottom wick closure. 
This shows that buyers are ready to step in and are in control. After the hourly closure, 
we prefer to see a bearish opening of the next hourly candle, followed by, in this case, 
a bullish flip. We can then enter, placing our stop losses under the wick of the previous 
candle closure. The longer the second bottom wick, the better, although this may not 
always occur.

Note that this initial entry model can occur on various timeframes, including the daily, 
4-hour, 2-hour, or even the 30-minute timeframe. This depends on several factors:
• What is the market volume? (High/Low)
• Are we pre or post London or NYSE Open?
• Are there any upcoming Red Folder News Events?
• Is this the first or second time we are reaching this key level?
Based on these factors, you can estimate the timeframe to monitor: Low volume equals 
inconsistency, so it's better to wait for a higher timeframe candle closure for additional 
confirmation rather than quickly entering. High volume, on the other hand, can lead to 
stronger confirmations when the candle closes around a key level. The period before the 
London or NYSE open can induce buyers or sellers to enter the markets, which can then 
be used for liquidity as we often see significant manipulation around the open. 
 
If you enter beforehand, pay close attention to your initial trade setup and apply  
conservative risk management. The same applies to upcoming Red Folder News events. 
If you expect sudden price movements, this can also coincide with price manipulation, 
which you should try to avoid. If price tapped in to this specific PW VaH before, it might 
be wise to wait for extra confirmation. This is where waiting for the candle development 
on the higher timeframe is most sensible. You could also opt for the Lower Time Frame 
(LTF) Swing Confirmation Setup (Entry Model 2) as an alternative.
Finally, with Entry Model 1, it's crucial not to rush into any positions! If you're 10 minutes 
before the 30-minute or hourly candle closure, wait for the candle to close and observe 
how the next candle develops before entering.
If you're 30 minutes or less before the 4-hour closure, it's better to wait for this closure 
before looking for a potential entry.
This is extremely important because most manipulation occurs during these periods.  
The market is fractal, so you'll see many candle closures on different timeframes,  
which can induce many traders to enter the market just before the new candle opens.
Now, let's look at the second entry model. We'll use the same initial setup from the 
PW VaH, but this time on the 15M instead of the hourly. Before we delve into Entry  
Model 2, let's discuss the advantages and disadvantages of waiting for a confirmation:
The Pros:
You can observe price moving away from our main key level, as in this specific  
example further indicating that more buyers are stepping in. You are likely to increase 
your Risk to Reward ratio as you don't need to set your SL below the hourly wick at the 
PW VaH, allowing you to have a lower win rate, while still being profitable.

The Cons:
You have to wait longer for your setup to play out. If the price doesn't retrace into your

charts, as the price can move quickly, requiring you to monitor the charts constantly.
Example 2: Previous Weekly VaH 15M Entry (Entry Model 2)
In this specific example, after tapping into PW VaH on the 1-hour timeframe, we scale 
down to the 15-minute timeframe. As discussed in the previous chapter about Swing 
Profiles, the advantage is that you can also use this on a lower timeframe when looking 
for entry possibilities.
You do this by drawing a Swing Profile from the first candle that touches our initial 
key level, towards the internal swing high, the highest point of our internal swing, both 
marked with a circle. From there, we generate a volume profile, this time only from our 
internal swing. The benefit of this approach is that you can directly confirm which zone 
within that swing is your highest level of interest.
Once the price retreats to the LTF Swing PoC, we look for the same confirmation as we 
did initially on Entry Model 1. The bearish 15M that reached the LTF Swing PoC indi-
cates a possible bounce, but it isn't a sufficient confirmation, so we wait for the next 15M 
to develop.
When the next 15M candle opens, we see a significant push into the LTF Swing PoC. 
However, we wait for the break of the previous candle structure.
This break occurs when the third 15M candle opens. It opens bearish, flips bullish, and 
surpasses the previous two candles that reached our second key level.
We enter at this flip, setting our SL once again below the previous (internal) candle.

In this example, we identify a specific pattern on the chart called a three-step formation:
Consolidation - This occurs around our key level. There may have already been  
multiple interactions with our key level, but without any follow-through. This often  
indicates that we are still building liquidity, or that buyers and sellers are both active, 
waiting for either one to tire out so the price can continue to move.
Manipulation - After consolidation around our key level, we manipulate the price to 
make traders believe that we have officially broken our key level, in this case, the PD 
VaL.
Expansion - We then aggressively reverse the price, trapping many traders who entered 
the market during the manipulation phase. Our entry comes in at this point. In this  
example, we enter the market when we break the lows, placing our stop loss (SL) above 
the previous highs and targeting the Wednesday lows. Remember, this often happens 
during specific market openings, such as the London Open & NYSE. Therefore, it's one 
of my favored entry models during these time periods.
However, it can happen at any time and is definitely something to be aware of.  
The entry model gives you the patience you need, rather than entering when you’re still 
consolidating.

Range Profiles. Importantly, these profiles are strategically combined with any one of  
our four entry models. This combination provides a rich understanding and practical 
demonstration of potential trading scenarios, also on different timeframes.

Micro to Macro Timeframes: Fractal Execution
Markets follow fractal patterns, meaning price action repeats across different timeframes.
How to Apply it:
1. Start on the 15M timeframe. If an entry model forms but fails, move to the 30M chart.
2. If the pattern holds on 30 minutes, wait for confirmation.
3. Expand this process to 1-hour, 2-hour, or 4-hour timeframes as long as your trade 
management process allows.
A failed entry on one timeframe may still be valid on a higher timeframe. 
Larger timeframes provide stronger confirmations so you can prevent getting shaken out 
too early.
Mastering these entry models will give you an edge. Stay disciplined and refine your 
process.

B E Y O N D  F U N D A M E N T A L S
S M A R T  M O N E Y ' S  E D G E
I
nstead of trying to guess every move in the market, we pay attention to what the big 
players are doing. A lot of new traders believe that if they understand things like interest 
rates, inflation, and GDP, they’ll be able to predict where the market is headed. And that 
sounds smart, but it’s not so easy in real life.
In the past, we considered "fundamentals" to be items such as economic reports,  
government policies, and central bank news. But over time, our thinking changed.  
The word “Fundamentum” comes from Latin and means foundation.
In our strategy, economic data is one kind of foundation; it’s the cold, hard facts.  
However, there’s another side to the story: the way traders behave, how they feel, and 
how they react. That’s what we call sentiment data. Of course, we understand econom-
ic data and study it with respect. However, we don’t use it as our primary tool for making 
trading decisions.
 
Why not? 
We don’t have the speed, early access, or real inside information that big institutions do. 
When big news comes out, the market often reacts before we even finish reading the 
headline. So, instead of trying to predict all the news on our own, we decided to follow 
the money. We track what the smart money is doing, the traders who are usually ahead 
of the game.
 
Let’s look at the U.S. Dollar as an example:
 
Several factors can impact the dollar, including job reports, inflation numbers, interest 
rates, retail sales, consumer confidence, and unemployment claims, among others. But 
which one is the most important right now? Which one has already been "priced in"? And 
how do we know when to enter a trade?
 
That’s where things get messy, and people end up guessing. You might hear, "The job 
report was great, so the dollar should go up," but then it goes down. Why? Perhaps  
traders expected that report, or maybe too many people were already betting on the  
dollar, or potentially something bigger is coming next week. The point is that it’s  
unpredictable and can also be used for manipulation.
We also recognize the significance of interest rate differences between countries.  
When one currency offers higher returns than another, investors typically shift their  
money into the stronger one (basic macroeconomics). A great example is the carry 
trade with the Japanese Yen. It’s when traders borrow Yen because it has super-low 
interest rates, and then they use that money to invest in higher-return assets, such as 
other currencies or dividend-paying stocks. Sometimes, they even reinvest it back into  
Japanese stocks.
72
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

===== PAGE 83 =====
This kind of information is essential because these significant financial moves shape 
markets. So, we don’t ignore them. We’ve studied them, and we get it. But here’s the 
thing we realized: even when all the economic logic makes sense, smart money is usu-
ally ahead of it. They make their moves before the news becomes public. By the time 
everyone sees the headline and starts reacting, the big traders are already in position.
 
That’s where many Retail traders get tricked. They see a strong number in the news and 
jump into the market expecting a clear move. But the market is like an auction, and it 
doesn’t just follow your “logic.” It follows imbalance. If too many people expect  
something to happen, the opposite often does.
 
You’ve probably seen it happen: a strong jobs report comes out—and the market drops. 
People end up confused. However, when you know the positioning data beforehand, it 
starts to make sense.
 
So what do we do instead?
 
We track sentiment, who is buying and selling, and what levels they care about.  
The Commitment of Traders (COT) report reveals the positioning of different groups, 
including Commercials, Large Speculators, and Retail Traders. You will learn about this 
in the coming chapters. It helps us identify when the market is stretched too far in one 
direction, and it alerts us when a significant shift might be approaching. It’s the evidence 
we need to solve the crime.
 
That's what matters: Data
 
Because if you don’t have data, you’re guessing. Guessing will never build a solid  
trading strategy. To be clear, economic data still matters, as it drives economic activity.  
It shakes things up and gives us context, so we pay attention to it.
 
However, when it comes to actually placing trades and building a system that works  
consistently, our edge lies in watching sentiment and positioning. We don’t have to 
guess what the Fed will do. We watch how the “Real Smart Money” is positioned before 
the meeting. We don’t have to assume how the market will react to a GDP number.  
We examine who is already long or short and identify the key levels. 
 
Do you understand?
Debunking the Myth: All Indicators Are Lagging?
There’s a widespread belief among retail traders that all indicators are lagging. And for 
the most part, they’re right. Traditional indicators, such as moving averages, MACD, RSI, 
and Bollinger Bands, rely on candlestick closures. That means by the time they give you 
a signal, the market has already moved. You’re always one step behind, reacting instead 
of anticipating. 
 
But that’s exactly where our fundamental approach diverges. The proprietary XLimitless 
indicators built into the LTA Concepts ecosystem, which we will discuss, are not  
designed to mirror price; they are designed to unlock context.
