M A R K E T  S E N T I M E N T
A S  A  L E A D I N G  I N D I C A T O R
S
entiment is the mood of the market, the invisible but powerful force that often moves 
price before the charts reveal anything obvious. Technical analysis shows what has 
happened. Fundamentals explain why it happened. But sentiment? Sentiment tells you 
what is likely to happen next.
Those who master sentiment learn how to spot shifts before they are visible to every-
one else. They see the signs of exhaustion before the reversal and notice the hidden 
strength before the breakout.
Sentiment moves like a pendulum, swinging between fear and greed. When everyone 
is bullish, buyers get exhausted. There is no one left to buy. When everyone is bearish, 
sellers dry up. Price often turns when emotion is at an extreme, not where you logically 
expect it.
Think about Bitcoin in late 2021. As price touched $69,000, the world was euphoric.  
Social media screamed, "To the moon!" Your taxi driver told you to buy it. Yet, beneath 
the surface, smart money was offloading. Open Interest was falling even as price rose. 
No new major buyers were stepping in, and sentiment had reached an extreme. The 
crash below $30,000 was not a surprise to those who were watching the Smart Money 
and Retail behavior instead of the price alone.
When you want to measure these emotional extremes, there are two main tools you 
can rely on: The Commitment of Traders (COT) report and Open Interest (OI).
The COT report gives you a window into the positioning of different types of traders. It 
breaks down into Commercials, who hedge their business exposure, Large Speculators, 
the hedge funds and institutions chasing profit; and Retail Traders, who are usually late 
to the trend.
76
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

===== PAGE 87 =====
Commercials: The Hedgers
Commercials are often referred to as users, producers, or hedgers. They do not enter 
the market to speculate. No, they participate because they have a genuine business 
need. Imagine a wheat farmer who knows his harvest will come in six months. If he fears 
prices might drop by then, he can sell wheat Futures today to lock in the current price 
and protect his income. Commercials operate out of necessity, not greed. They are often 
the "smartest money" in the market at extremes because they base their actions on  
actual production and consumption.
A simple formula explains their behavior:
Commercial Behavior Reason
Price too high Sell Futures to lock profits
Price too low Buy Futures to lock costs
 
To make this even clearer, here is an example:
Scenario Physical Market Futures Hedge Overall Result
Wheat price rises 
from $5 to $7
Farmer gains $2 per 
bushel on wheat crop
Farmer loses $2 per 
bushel on Futures 
position
Net neutral: gains in the 
physical market offset 
Futures loss
Wheat price falls 
from $5 to $3
Farmer loses $2 per 
bushel on wheat crop
Farmer gains $2 per 
bushel on Futures 
position
Net neutral: Futures  
profit offsets physical 
loss
Even if their Futures hedge moves against them, Commercials are rarely at risk of real 
harm. If the price of wheat rises after a farmer sells Futures, he loses on his short Fu-
tures position. But his physical crop is now worth much more, so he benefits from higher 
spot prices. The gain in the physical market offsets the loss on the hedge. If prices fall, 
he wins on the Futures hedge because he sold at a high price, protecting his revenues 
even though his wheat now sells for less. In both scenarios, the farmer secures predict-
able cash flow and business stability.
This ability to balance outcomes is why Commercials can always survive price 
swings. The value is not tied to one side of a bet; it’s anchored to the physical goods 
they produce or consume.
77
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
M  A  C  R  O   A  N  A  L  Y  S  I  S   &   S  E  N  T  I  M  E  N  T

===== PAGE 88 =====
Large Speculators: The Trend Followers
Then you have the Large Speculators, also known as Non-Commercials. These are the 
major financial sector players: hedge funds, asset managers, and institutional traders. 
Unlike Commercials, they have no physical product or business to protect. Their sole 
goal is to make a profit by riding market trends. They buy when prices are strong and sell 
when prices are weak. They are momentum chasers, fueling trends further than logic 
would typically allow. In our process, we pay close attention to the positioning of Large 
Speculators because it helps us track momentum plays. When these traders get  
overloaded on one side, it often marks the late stage of a move.
Retail Traders: The Emotional Crowd
Lastly, there are the Non-Reportables, commonly known as Retail traders. These are the 
smaller players in the market, often trading based on emotions rather than information. 
Retail traders tend to buy tops and sell bottoms because they chase after what has  
already happened. They see green candles and feel the fear of missing out (FOMO), 
and see red candles and panic. When 80% or more of Retail traders are heavily  
positioned to one side, it usually signals that a reversal is close. The crowd is rarely early 
to the real move.
Interpreting Open Interest with Price Action 
Open Interest shows us another layer of the story. This data is also included in the 
COT report, which tracks the number of active contracts at any given time. By observing 
whether participation is growing or shrinking, we gain insight into how truly committed 
the market is.
Market Behavior Open Interest Reaction What It Means
Rising Price + Rising OI New buyers entering Trend continuation (Strong Trend)
Rising Price + Falling OI Short covering Trend exhaustion (Weaker Trend)
Falling Price + Rising OI New shorts entering Bearish confirmation
Falling Price + Falling OI Longs closing Weak sell-off, potential consolida-
tion or reversal
If price rises but open interest falls, the trend is likely burning out. If price falls and OI  
rises, new shorts are entering the market with conviction. These are the signals you 
learn to recognize when you go beyond the surface.
78
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
M  A  C  R  O   A  N  A  L  Y  S  I  S   &   S  E  N  T  I  M  E  N  T

===== PAGE 89 =====
Then we have Retail Positioning, the ultimate contrarian signal. Retail traders, driven 
by emotion and limited information, often get caught at the worst moments. When Retail 
positioning shows 80% or more on one side of the market, it is a major warning.
During the 2023 gold rally, Retail traders continued to short the market even as the price 
continued to climb. Their short positions fueled the rally. Only when they finally flipped 
bullish did gold top out and reverse. When too many are short, a squeeze higher often 
becomes inevitable. When too many are long, the fall can be brutal.
However, sentiment is not a signal in itself; it's a powerful confluence that either  
strengthens or invalidates a technical setup.
Picture this. You are analyzing EUR/USD. The COT report indicates that Large  
Speculators hold extreme amounts of long positions. Open Interest is falling, suggesting 
traders are closing positions. Retail sentiment shows that 85% of traders are also long. 
Technically, EUR/USD is sitting at a weekly supply zone.
Without sentiment, you might only see a resistance level. With sentiment, you  
understand why that resistance level is more likely to hold. The market is overcrowded 
with longs, and there is little buying power left.
Still, timing remains crucial. Sentiment extremes can last longer than expected, and 
just because hedge funds are heavily long does not mean price will collapse  
immediately. Smart traders wait for confirmation. A failed breakout, a bearish engulfing 
candle, or a clear divergence between price action and market sentiment gives you the 
green light. They scale into their positions carefully, as smart money does, and they 
respect the dominant trend until clear signs show otherwise. Over time, this creates a 
repeatable process, a sentiment playbook that sharpens your edge.
First, always check the COT report.
Are the institutions heavily long or short? 
 
Then, analyze Open Interest. Are more traders entering the market or stepping away? 
Next, align your sentiment analysis with major technical levels. Are you approaching a 
key supply or demand zone? When sentiment and macro technicals align, you develop a 
strong, confident bias. And finally, layer in other confluences, such as valuation models, 
seasonality, or fundamental drivers, to build an even stronger case.
Sentiment analysis transforms noise into valuable information. It transforms you from a 
reactive trader into a proactive one, allowing you to position yourself ahead of the crowd 
rather than chasing after moves that have already occurred.  
 
Sentiment reveals who is behind the move and how committed they are, and that is 
where you acquire your real edge.

Y
ou just learned about the power of positioning. What if you could listen in on the  
biggest players in the market, seeing exactly where they are placing their bets  
before the crowd even notices? A weekly scorecard that shows who is actually buying 
and selling. 
That is precisely what the Commitment of Traders (COT) report offers. Published by the 
Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), it provides you with a direct look into 
the behavior of the largest players in the Futures markets.
Every week, the COT report hands you clues. It shows you which team the profession-
als are betting on, where the amateurs are rushing in, and when the momentum might 
be running out. When you know how to read it, it not only shows what is happening but 
also hints at what might happen next.
Markets move because of supply, demand, and positioning. When everyone is already 
loaded onto one side of the boat, it does not take much for the boat to tip. The COT 
report helps traders spot these crowded conditions before they are reflected in the price 
action.
The traders who understand sentiment have an edge. They can feel when a market is 
getting top-heavy or when fear has opened the door for opportunity, and they no longer 
react emotionally. They position themselves ahead of the crowd.

===== PAGE 92 =====
The Evolution of the COT Report: 
From Monthly Snapshots to Weekly Insights
As we mentioned in the beginning of the book, the original Commitment of Traders 
(COT) report was first published in 1962. At that time, it was released by the Commodity 
Exchange Authority (CEA), which was a division of the U.S. Department of Agriculture 
(USDA). The CEA was responsible for overseeing U.S. commodity Futures markets prior 
to the formation of the CFTC.
To level the playing field, Congress demanded more disclosure. The COT report was 
born from that movement. Its mission was simple: to shine a light on who was buying 
and selling so markets could operate more fairly. Initially, this report was only released 
monthly, offering minimal insight. As markets grew and the need for greater transparency 
increased, the CFTC transitioned the COT report to a weekly release, providing traders 
with fresher and more actionable data.
Over time, the report evolved into one of the most powerful sentiment tools available, 
offering X-ray vision into the hidden currents that move prices beneath the surface.
The Commercials who hedge their business operations, the Large Speculators who 
chase profit through trends, and the small Retail traders who often arrive late to the  
party. Each group leaves behind footprints, and by reading the COT report, you can 
trace where smart money is positioned and where emotional money is getting 
trapped.
A simple metaphor is watching a tug-of-war match. One side is gaining ground, but you 
notice they are starting to lose their grip. That's the insight you get when reading the 
COT report correctly.
When Commercials begin increasing their long positions, it often hints that smart money 
sees value at current levels. When Large Speculators are at record “long” exposure, it 
warns that a trend could be overheating. When Retail traders are leaning heavily to one 
side, it signals that a reversal might not be far away.
82

Why Historical Patterns Matter More Than Reactions
It’s critical to understand that the COT report is not a timing tool. Don’t make the  
mistake of thinking that because Commercials are long or Large Speculators are at an  
extreme, price must immediately react the following week. This is what uneducated  
traders believe, and they end up confused, claiming that the data does not work.
That is not how the COT data should be used. It is a tool for context, not prediction. The 
edge comes from studying historical patterns, not reacting to single snapshots. At LTA, 
we have heavily backtested thousands of instances across different markets, currencies, 
and commodities. We have built an extensive library of data that tracks how sentiment 
shifts tend to unfold over time. It’s through this research that we know where the real 
edge lies and why patience is key.
A clear example of this occured in September 2024. The majority was certain that the 
U.S. dollar would drop further after the Federal Reserve announced a 50 basis point rate 
cut. News headlines screamed bearish dollar forecasts, and Retail traders rushed to sell 
the dollar aggressively.
But when we analyzed the COT data, we noticed something crucial. The Non-Reporta-
bles, or Retail traders, were already extremely short positioned. At the same time, Open 
Interest was falling sharply. And from a technical standpoint, we were in major weekly 
support. This told us two things. First, the downward trend was weakening. Second, 
the aggressive short positioning suggested that the 50 basis point cut had already been 
priced in.

depends on the big players positioning. If a major announcement catches the market off

the news, the market often moves in the opposite direction.

Then it happened: EUR/USD reversed sharply. Those late buyers were caught off guard,

smart money and emotional money collide.
You confirm trends by tracking whether hedge funds are still adding pressure or pulling 
away. You catch participant divergences when Retail enthusiasm grows even as Large

12
S E N T I M E N T  E X T R E M E S
C O M M E R C I A L S  A N D  R E T A I L
W
hen it comes to major turning points in the market, two groups matter most:  
Commercials and Retail Traders. The Commitment of Traders (COT) report  
reveals these extremes, but only if you know what to look for. When Commercials reach 
a bullish or bearish extreme, and retail traders are heavily positioned on the opposite 
side, it often marks the moment when the market is about to shift aggressively. Before 
we go deeper, it's important to highlight a key distinction. 
There are two types of extremes that we can reference:
• Net Positioning Extremes: This measures the sum of long minus short contracts 
held, which historically has been massive longs or shorts based on total net  
numbers. This is visualized by examining the actual net line and identifying when it 
has been at an extreme historically.
 
87
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
 Retail Bullish Extreme
 Retail Bearish Extreme


===== PAGE 98 =====
• Relative Oscillator Extremes: This measures current positioning relative to a  
specific timeframe, such as a six-month, one-year, or multi-year range. Oscillator 
extremes are typically represented by using tools that standardize and normalize the 
positioning into a 0–100% range, highlighting when we are at the 80–100%  
thresholds.
Both approaches offer valuable data, but they serve different purposes.  
Net Positioning Extremes tend to capture broader macro shifts where participation has 
reached an actual historical peak. Oscillator Extremes highlight more tactical shifts 
within ongoing trends and can be quicker to react but may require more confirmation.
Understanding the type of extreme you are dealing with is crucial for making the right  
decision. Most traders focus too much on the Large Speculators (Non-Commercials). 
They watch hedge funds adding or reducing positions and assume that is the key to 
spotting tops and bottoms. But that is not where the true contrarian edge lies.
Large Speculators play an important role, but they behave differently. Their actions 
tend to reflect the trend itself. They ladder in and out of positions, scaling exposure over 
time. Their influence shapes momentum but rarely captures the extremes where markets 
snap and reverse sharply.
That said, it's also important to note that Large Speculators can occasionally create  
divergences. When their positioning begins to diverge sharply from price, it can  
sometimes hint at broader trend exhaustion. However, these signals are rare and require 
far more nuance to interpret correctly. We will dive deeper into these rare divergence 
signals in the Laddering Process later. For now, our focus is on the real contrarian  
indicators: Commercials and Retail.

As a reminder, Commercials, the producers, hedgers, and users of commodities and 
currencies act not to speculate, but to survive. They sell when prices are high relative to 
value and buy when prices are low relative to value. Retail Traders, on the other hand, 
tend to do the exact opposite. They pile into trends too late, chasing what has already  
happened, unaware that the market is about to punish their impatience and FOMO.
When you see Commercials reaching maximum long or short positioning, and at the 
same time Retail reaching maximum short or long exposure, you are looking at an  
environment where the imbalance is unsustainable.
The beautiful part? It's not subjective. This trend has been consistently observed across 
various currencies, commodities, and indices throughout history.
Real-World Examples of COT Sentiment Extremes
Dollar Index (DX1!) – Commercials Bullish Oscillator Extreme vs  
Retail Bearish Oscillator Extreme
In September 2024, when the Federal Reserve announced a 50 basis point rate cut, 
headlines screamed bearish forecasts for the dollar. Retail Traders aggressively  
shorted the dollar, expecting it to collapse further. But the COT data showed a different 
story. Retail Traders were already extremely short, while Open Interest was falling, and 
the technicals showed we were sitting inside major weekly support.
Commercials were scaling into bullish exposure, and Retail was fully committed to the 
downside. The imbalance was clear.
Oscillator Extreme Example: Commercials hit 100% long extremes while Retail were 
positioned 0% on the other side, based on a six-month window. The result? The dollar 
bottomed, trapping Retail Traders on the wrong side and forcing a sharp rally.

M A R K E T  D R I V E R S

A
s we move forward from understanding COT sentiment extremes and contrarian 
positioning, it is critical to now shift our focus. While Commercials and Retail  
positioning often signal when markets are about to snap and reverse, Large Speculators 
play a completely different role.
Large Speculators are not primarily reversal signals; they’re momentum drivers.
The mistake many traders make is assuming that all participants behave the same way 
and that an extreme positioning always means a reversal is imminent. However, that is 
not true when it comes to Large Speculators, which are made up of hedge funds,  
investment managers, and large trading institutions that operate on momentum. They 
are trend followers by design. Their goal is not to fade the market but to ride its strength.
Where Commercials and Retail tend to mark turning points, Large Speculators define 
the actual progression of trends. When Large Speculators are aggressively building  
positions, the trend is often still alive and well. It is only when their laddering process 
slows, stalls, or diverges from price that warning signs begin to appear.
There are rare occasions when price continues to climb, and Large Speculators begin 
offloading long positions quietly. This divergence, where price and speculative  
positioning disconnect, often signals that the trend is running out of fuel. However, it's 
essential to acknowledge that this alone is not always the sole trigger.
More often, it is the Commercials who lead the actual reversal. The Commercials,  
orchestrating their positioning along with Retail sentiment, are the true architects of  
major tops and bottoms.
Still, by tracking the laddering behavior of Large Speculators, you can monitor the 
health of the trend. When Large Speculators increase their exposure and Open Interest 
rises alongside price, it's one of the strongest indications that the trend is healthy, strong, 
and likely to continue.
When Open Interest rises while Large Speculators add, participation is expanding. 
When Open Interest is falling, and Large Specs are reducing, conviction is fading.  
This interplay between net positioning and Open Interest is something we will explore 
even deeper in the next chapter.
The ability to build confluences upon confluences, positioning, Open Interest, and  
technicals allows you to operate with a much sharper edge.

LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

===== PAGE 107 =====
The Laddering Process: How Positions Develop Over Time
One of the reasons COT data is so powerful is that positions are not built in a day. They 
are built over time. Slowly. Methodically. This is what we call the Laddering Process.
Large Speculators do not simply jump into massive trades overnight. They accumulate 
exposure over weeks, months, and even quarters. This gradual buildup is evident in the 
COT report, revealing the extent to which institutions are committed to the current trend.
To see this in action, let us look at a real-world example: the Japanese Yen (6J1!)  
positioning from early 2025.

In this example, we can see how Large Speculators steadily increased their long  
positions week after week, aligning perfectly with the uptrend on the chart. As long  
positions climbed, Open Interest rose alongside, confirming that participation and  
conviction were expanding. This is the very definition of a strong, healthy trend.  
Rising prices, increasing Large Speculator longs, and rising Open Interest form one of 
the strongest confluences you can find.
Additionally, the reduction of short positions played a reinforcing role, reducing selling 
pressure and allowing the trend to strengthen even further. It's the dual effect of longs 
being added and shorts being covered that marks a healthy expansion phase.
From our extensive backtesting and research, we found that a critical filter for confirming 
the laddering process is consistency. Specifically, we want to see a minimum of three 
consecutive weeks of consistent additions to long or short positions, depending on the 
trend direction. When this occurs alongside a strong technical confirmation, it  
significantly increases the probability of trend continuation.
This consistency matters because it filters out random fluctuations and shows genuine 
institutional commitment. Trends driven by layered, consistent participation are far  
stronger than trends built on random short bursts of positioning.

Quick Checklist for Identifying a Healthy Laddering Process
• At least three consecutive weeks of adding longs or shorts, in-line with the trend.
• Rising Open Interest along with rising long (or short) exposure.
• Trend direction confirmed by strong technical structure.
• Reduction of shorts in uptrends or longs in downtrends reinforces trend strength.
• Alignment with additional confirmations such as intraday trend, seasonality factors 
or macro supply and demand (Sections III & IV).
Following the evolution of positioning week by week reveals the underlying dynamics 
of market sentiment. Early in the cycle, we see Large Speculators slowly increasing 
their long exposure, aligning with rising prices and strengthening momentum. Open 
Interest rises alongside net long increases, confirming growing participation and trend 
conviction. Commercials, on the other hand, hedge into strength, adding to their shorts.
As weeks pass, positioning becomes more extreme—the laddering process peaks. We 
notice subtle shifts, Open Interest may stop rising, Large Speculators may slightly  
reduce their exposure. Meanwhile, Retail Traders usually become more aggressive right 
at the end, chasing the obvious trend. It's in these final stages that seasoned traders 
prepare for the inevitable, and they watch the structure unfold, layer by layer.
While the Laddering Process shows how trends expand, it instills patience and reminds 
you to respect the force of momentum until real cracks begin to show. By understanding 
the unique role each participant plays, you position yourself not as a blind follower but as 
a trader aligned with the underlying currents shaping the market.

14
O P E N  I N T E R E S T 
T H E  T R U E  H E A R T B E A T  O F  T H E  M A R K E T 
O
pen Interest is one of the most underrated indicators in all of trading. Despite its 
clarity and the wealth of information it provides, it often gets overlooked. We don't 
know exactly why that is. It may be because it does not show up directly on most price 
charts. Perhaps it is because it requires space, patience, and context to be used  
properly. It may be harder for traders to access and interpret compared to more common 
indicators. Whatever the reason, what is clear is that Open Interest offers a level of  
intelligence into market behavior that very few tools can match. 
We have already mentioned Open Interest several times in previous chapters, from COT 
Sentiment Extremes to the Laddering Process of Large Speculators. Now, it's time to 
bring it fully into focus.
Because while positioning tells us who is active in the market, Open Interest tells us how 
committed they are.
Imagine walking into a room. Not just seeing who is there, but feeling the energy, are 
people entering with excitement, ready to stay? Or are they quietly slipping out the back 
door? Open Interest captures that energy.
101


===== PAGE 112 =====
It tracks the total number of open contracts in the market—contracts that have been 
created and are still alive, not yet closed. Unlike volume, which resets every day, Open 
Interest builds a storyline over weeks and months.
It tells you not just where price is moving but whether new players are entering the game 
or if the trend is running out of steam.
Look at it this way. The Dollar Index has been declining into a major macro weekly  
demand zone. Open Interest has dropped significantly, showing that positions are  
being closed and participation is thinning. Meanwhile, when analyzing the Commitment 
of Traders report, we can see that Commercials have reached a strong bullish extreme. 
The technical structure confirms significant support is nearby.  
 
We will explain exactly how we define and identify these weekly demand zones in  
Section IV when we dive deeper into macro technicals. Smart traders recognize that 
these conditions create an environment where a reversal is not only possible but highly 
probable. They prepare accordingly, aligning themselves with the smart money rather 
than chasing the last legs of weakness.
Open Interest reveals the true heartbeat of a trend. It answers the questions price alone 
cannot. It shows whether new money is entering the market, if the move is real or  
fading, and where liquidity magnets are forming. When price rises and Open Interest 
rises alongside it, we know fresh buyers are fueling the move.  
 
When price increases but Open Interest falls, it suggests that positions are closing and 
that the move may be running out of steam. When price falls and Open Interest rises, 
new shorts are likely entering, strengthening the downtrend. However, when price falls 
and Open Interest drops, longs are likely to exit and the market could be nearing a  
bottom.

Understanding Open Interest is even more critical when pairing it with sentiment and 
positioning data. While the Commitment of Traders report reveals the positions of  
institutions, hedgers, and Retail traders, Open Interest indicates whether their conviction 
is increasing or decreasing. It is essential to clarify that our LTA framework focuses  
specifically on Open Interest derived from CFTC data, which is directly tied to the weekly 
COT reports. This means Open Interest updates are aligned with the new report  
releases each week, rather than fluctuating daily like volume or intraday exchange data.
When Large Speculators are laddering into a trend and Open Interest is rising alongside, 
the trend has real momentum. When Large Speculators are building, and Open Interest 
is stalling or falling, it’s a warning to be cautious.

What most traders overlook is how Open Interest is measured. Every new buyer 
matched with a new seller creates a new open contract and increases Open Interest by 
one. When two parties close out existing positions, Open Interest decreases by one. 
This measurement is aggregated and reported by the CFTC across major exchanges, 
giving you a reliable weekly snapshot of market commitment.
Open Interest is not just a number buried deep inside exchange data. It is one of the 
most underrated tools available to you. It reveals whether the move you see unfolding 
on the chart has real depth or whether it is being driven by short-term noise. While Retail 
chases surface-level candles, smart money is always watching Open Interest.
Inside our LTA framework, we do not look at Open Interest in isolation. We combine it 
with positioning data, technical structure, and macro factors. When we are tracking the 
Laddering Process, we specifically look for at least three consecutive weeks of  
increasing long or short exposure by Large Speculators, rising Open Interest confirming 
fresh participation, and strong technical structure in the trend direction. Each layer of 
confluence increases the probability of success.
A quick overview to help you interpret Open Interest dynamics correctly:

We must recognize that while raw Open Interest numbers provide clues, they do not 
directly reveal which participants are behind the move. However, using CFTC  
breakdowns (Open Interest Long/Short positions per participant category) allows us to 
see more precisely whether it is Commercials, Large Speculators, or Retail Traders  
driving the positioning shift.
One of the clearest examples comes from the Dollar Index itself. In early 2023, while 
most traders were focused on short-term price swings, Large Speculators steadily in-
creased their long positions, and Open Interest rose alongside the move. The technical 
structure confirmed strength, and smart traders aligned with the trend. When Open  
Interest eventually stalled weeks later, it provided an early clue that momentum was 
fading long before the larger reversal became evident on the chart.

Open Interest behavior often shifts dramatically around Futures Expiration periods. As 
Futures contracts near expiry, it is common to see Open Interest drop sharply as traders 
close positions or roll into new contracts. These expiries create liquidity shifts that can 
cause sudden changes in price behavior. We will explore this dynamic in much greater 
detail in the upcoming chapter on Futures, Options Expirations, and Market Rotation.
In addition to tracking raw Open Interest behavior, our Smart Money Intelligence (SMI) 
tool also tracks Open Interest extremes through an oscillator. When Open Interest  
reaches a historical extreme, such as pressing near the 0% or 100% level on the  
oscillator, it can act as a major warning signal. Especially when Open Interest quickly 
moves from a 100% extreme to a 0% extreme, it often suggests that the trend is  
exhausted and a reversal is likely approaching.
These Open Interest extremes do not always define exact timing, but when combined 
with other factors such as macro supply and demand levels, technical exhaustion zones, 
and Commercial or Retail positioning extremes, they dramatically strengthen the  
probability of a major shift occurring soon. 
In addition to sentiment-focused Open Interest tracking, we saw a need for a specialized 
tool that isolates pure Open Interest behavior across the broader market. That led to the 
creation of the XLimitless Open Interest Radar™ . Designed to detect liquidity shifts, 
hidden compression zones, and sudden breakouts, the Open Interest Radar comple-
ments the SMI perfectly. While the SMI ties Open Interest to market participant  
sentiment, the Radar gives a clear view of Open Interest movements themselves,  
allowing you to spot structural changes early.
While the Smart Money Intelligence (SMI) Indicator measures Open Interest as an oscil-
lator to capture extremes in sentiment and momentum, the Open Interest Radar focuses 
purely on tracking the actual raw Open Interest levels week by week. 
Each tool serves a different role: one highlights extremes that warn of possible turning 
points, the other tracks real liquidity entering or leaving the market structure.

For example, imagine we are in a strong uptrend. Suddenly, Open Interest begins to 
drop significantly. At the same time, price pushes into a major resistance area from a 
macro technical standpoint. When checking the Commitment of Traders data, we see 
that Commercials are positioned at a bearish extreme. All of these factors combined can 
act as a sell signal, highlighting that a reversal could be imminent.
Open Interest is the silent witness behind every trend. It reveals commitment, and 
exposes exhaustion. It highlights the difference between real conviction and short-term 
noise. It teaches you to read beyond price action and recognize when a move is truly 
supported or running on fumes.
Retail chases price, while smart traders monitor Open Interest in the background,  
reading the real flow of capital. Because if you want to ride trends with confidence and 
avoid getting trapped in false moves, you need more than just price. You need to know 
if the crowd is still in the room or if the real players have already left. Open Interest tells 
you that better than anything else.
As powerful as Open Interest is for understanding conviction and exhaustion, there is 
another piece of the puzzle that becomes crucial at certain times of the year:  
Futures and Options Expirations. Scheduled expirations often lead to significant 
liquidity shifts, Open Interest realignments, and sharp market rotations, which frequently 
catch unprepared participants off guard. In the next chapter, we will dive into how these  
expirations create hidden forces beneath the surface, how to anticipate the volatility they 
unleash, and how to align with smart money during these pivotal moments.

F U T U R E S  E X P I R Y
U N D E R S T A N D I N G  M A R K E T  R O T A T I O N
O
pen Interest taught us to track the silent flow of market conviction, now let’s extend 
that understanding with Futures and options expirations.
Most traders view expiry dates as just another line on the calendar, while professionals 
recognize that they reveal the positions of the big players. Hidden liquidity is flushed out, 
trends can shift, entire portfolios can rotate into new opportunities. And if you understand 
what is happening, you can position yourself ahead of the crowd.
Futures contracts are agreements to buy or sell something, like gold, oil, or even indi-
ces, at a set price on a specific date. However, unlike stocks, Futures expire; you cannot 
hold them indefinitely. When a Futures contract reaches its expiration date, it must either 
close its position and exit the market or roll over to a new contract and continue the trade 
into the next cycle.
This process creates waves of buying and selling. Big players, such as hedge funds 
and banks, adjust their positioning, causing sharp changes in prices, increased volatility, 
and significant shifts in liquidity. 
When Futures expire, decisions are forced upon large traders who control billions of  
dollars. Some will close their trades, some will reposition, some will hedge new expo-
sure. This constant recalibration creates visible effects in the market, including volatility 
spikes, temporary collapses in Open Interest, and the formation of liquidity imbalances 
around key price levels. New supply and demand zones emerge as capital repositions.
The secret lies in following these footprints. Open Interest becomes critical.  
Before expiry, Open Interest typically rises steadily as traders build their positions. 
During expiry week, Open Interest drops sharply as contracts are closed out. After  
expiry, new contracts open, and Open Interest begins climbing again, signaling where 
smart money is moving next.
108


===== PAGE 119 =====
Imagine the Dollar Index Futures are approaching expiry. The dollar, being highly  
sensitive to quarterly cycles, often reacts sharply around these expiration dates.  
Hedge funds and institutional players must decide whether to close their long or short 
contracts, roll them into the next quarter, or reposition entirely based on upcoming  
macroeconomic expectations. As they act, liquidity surges, price volatility increases, and 
important inflection points are often reached, creating powerful opportunities for those 
who closely monitor these cycles.
Trading Futures expiry effectively means tracking Open Interest carefully. Tools like the 
XLimitless Open Interest Radar™  give you an immediate advantage, showing when 
fresh contracts are opening or closing. Combining this with the Commitment of Traders 
report allows us to read what the big players are doing beneath the surface. The Volume 
Profile helps you pinpoint exactly where liquidity is clustering, highlighting supply and 
demand zones that matter during these rotations.
If Open Interest drops sharply without much price movement, it often signals consolida-
tion. Traders are closing out positions without new participants stepping in. This can be a 
powerful warning that a major breakout is coming once new money floods back in.
Picture the S&P 500 Futures heading into expiry. Early in the week, Open Interest rises 
as traders fine-tune positions. Midweek, price becomes erratic and choppy. By Friday, 
Open Interest collapses as contracts close. The following week, a new cycle begins, and 
Open Interest builds again, often revealing the true next trend. If hedge funds are seen 
unwinding long positions into expiry while Open Interest drops, it can hint that lower  
prices may follow soon.

T H E  F O R G O T T E N  L A Y E R S
U N C O V E R I N G  H I D D E N  T R E N D S
T
here’s something strange about financial markets. Have you noticed it? How retail 
traders are obsessed with what’s happening right now. The latest news headline 
or the most recent candle seems so important. They trade what they see, not what's 
happening under the surface. For them, it's about taking a setup right now, and if they 
can't execute within 5 minutes, it's probably not worth looking into. And the funny thing 
is, that’s why they’re always one step behind, and the retail positioning data proves this 
over and over again. Very few traders stop to ask the more profound questions and learn 
about what tends to happen repeatedly.
You’ve probably heard the saying, “History doesn’t repeat itself, but it often rhymes.” 
Nowhere is that more true than in the financial markets. But why is almost no one aware 
of this?
 
While the majority of the retail crowd is busy reacting to the present, some of the most 
successful traders in history have quietly built their edge by studying the past.
 
One of those traders is Larry Williams. In 1987, he took $10,000 and turned it into over 
$1.1 million in a single year during the Robbins World Cup Trading Championship. He 
achieved this by understanding market positioning and, most importantly, by studying 
these cycles. Seeing the underlying currents and flows, the repeating patterns that  
happen regardless of what the news is making you believe is happening at this moment.
 
Larry understood something most people still miss: markets have memory. This memory 
reveals itself through recurring patterns, rhythms, and seasonal tendencies, particularly 
in commodities and currencies. 
 
He built models around this, using seasonal blueprints that indicate when assets tend to 
top and bottom, based on decades of hard data. And he took it one step further. He real-
ized that an asset is not just defined by its movement in relation to other assets. Value is 
never isolated; it’s always relative.
 
It’s also critical to understand where the institutional traders and smart money are  
looking, and they take seasonal tendencies and cycles extremely seriously. That’s why 
seasonality is often called a self-fulfilling prophecy. If the smart money is betting on a 
seasonal trend, and the market confirms it, then chances are high that the move will 
come to fruition. Their conviction becomes the current under the surface.  
And that current is something you can ride if you know how to spot it.
 
That’s where most retail traders fall short. They look at gold on its own, or the dollar, or 
the Nasdaq. However, they often overlook the fact that these assets exist within a living 
ecosystem. It's the rotation of capital that moves markets from one narrative to another. 
From risk-on to risk-off and from overvalued to undervalued.
113
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 124 =====
And this is where seasonality and relative valuation come in.
 
Seasonality is one of the most underrated yet predictable tools in trading. Every year, 
liquidity tends to dry up in the summer. Risk assets tend to weaken in August. Gold finds 
strength heading into year-end, entering Q1—energy spikes in Q2. For the majority of 
traders, these are just random events, but in reality, they’re patterns shaped by human 
psychology on a massive scale.
 
In this section, we will slow things down and zoom out. We’re going to show you how 
to read the seasonal map of the market. We’ll examine cycles, patterns, and recurring 
trends across gold, silver, oil, the dollar, and major indices. I’ll show you how assets 
move in context and how the relationship between them reveals imbalance and  
opportunity.
 
Most importantly, you’ll begin to understand what Larry Williams saw decades ago:  
when you can anticipate seasonal behavior and compare value across correlated  
assets, you can trade with actual intelligence.
 
That moment of clarity gave him an edge decades ago, and it’s even more valuable now 
in a market flooded with noise and distraction. Because if you can understand how  
assets are connected, and when their historical patterns tend to repeat, you’ll begin to 
see trades before they become apparent to the rest of the world.
 
And I know some of you may be thinking, “I only trade on the lower timeframes.”  
Knowing this information gives you a considerable edge also on the lower timeframes, 
and it allows you to sync your trades with the current instead of fighting against it.

S E A S O N A L I T Y
C Y C L I C A L  T R E N D S  A N D  H I S T O R I C A L  P A T T E R N S
W
hen I first started trading, I was obsessed with what the market was doing at the 
moment without any foresight. But over time, I began to notice something: the 
same patterns kept appearing around the same times each year.
It was subtle at first: a rally in December, a slowdown in the summer. Movements that 
couldn't be explained by news alone, and this realization led me to discover one of the 
most powerful but overlooked forces in trading: Seasonality.
Most traders are so focused on the noise right in front of them that they completely miss 
the larger, recurring rhythms that shape markets year after year. Seasonality refers to  
recurring price patterns that emerge during specific times of the year, driven by  
fundamental cycles such as corporate earnings, tax deadlines, harvest seasons, and 
institutional rebalancing.
Markets are not random; they breathe in cycles. And once I saw it, I couldn't unsee it. 
Seasonality transformed my approach from reactive to anticipatory.
I realized that understanding seasonality was about positioning. Instead of asking  
"What is happening now," I started asking, "What usually happens around this time?"
It changed everything. When summer approached, I wasn't caught off guard by thin 
liquidity; I expected it. When autumn came, I was prepared for volatility. As December 
arrived, I knew to watch for the year-end flows that often drive markets higher.
Seasonality became like a weather forecast for the markets. It didn’t guarantee  
outcomes, but it informed me of the conditions to expect, allowing me to prepare and  
position myself accordingly.
But not every instrument exhibits strong seasonality. Some markets exhibit clear 
and reliable patterns. Others are noisy and inconsistent. I learned the hard way that you 
have to back-test and verify the patterns before trusting them.


===== PAGE 127 =====
The real seasonal patterns are powerful. Some seasonal narratives, such as the 
"Santa Rally" in December or the "Sell in May and Go Away" phenomenon, are widely 
discussed across various markets. I found they are particularly more relevant in  
commodities, especially in gold and related assets. In commodities, I observed gold 
strengthening in the final quarter as year-end portfolio adjustments drove buying. Crude 
oil prices spiked into the summer driving season. Even the U.S. dollar typically  
strengthens in January because companies bring money back home after the holidays.
Seasonality is so powerful because it reflects the positioning and anticipation of the 
“smart money,” which has the best access to information. Large institutions, hedge 
funds, and commercial traders, in particular, pay close attention to these seasonal  
cycles. Commercial traders, who often have deeper intelligence into fundamental  
supply-and-demand factors, align their positions with seasonal patterns, reinforcing 
these cycles year after year. Later on, I will show you clear examples of how commer-
cials have historically positioned themselves bullish when seasonal trends point upward, 
amplifying the accuracy and strength of seasonal moves. But it isn't only smart money 
driving these patterns. 
 
Seasonality also acts as a self-fulfilling prophecy, as many traders recognize these  
seasonal tendencies and believe they will repeat themselves; their collective actions 
actually help bring these seasonal patterns to life, making them more reliable year after 
year.The deeper I looked, the clearer it became; seasonality was the compass beneath 
the noise.
Once I started doing deeper research into the subject, I came across the work of  
Larry Williams. Larry is a legendary trader who won the prestigious Robbins World 
Cup Trading Championship, turning $10,000 into over $1.1 million in just 12 months, a 
record that still stands today. His accomplishments earned him widespread respect, and 
his work on seasonality opened up a whole new layer of understanding for me. Larry 
explained how powerful seasonal tendencies can be when combined with other forms 
of analysis, such as sentiment and technicals. He highlighted how certain months and 
weeks historically produced stronger trends depending on the asset class. Through his 
work, I realized that seasonality was a measurable, researchable, and applicable trading 
edge.
But I didn't stop there; I wanted to go deeper. Beyond the month. Beyond the quarter.
I realized that while knowing gold tends to perform well from September to February is 
valuable, what if I could pinpoint it even more precisely? Down to the week? Down to the 
day?
That's when I started working with seasonal forecasting models. I looked at 5-year,  
10-year, 20-year, and 30-year averages. I wanted to see, based on historical data, when 
cycle tops and bottoms tend to occur.
I built my tools, including XLimitless FutureScope™ . I studied charts that projected 
seasonality forward based on historical patterns. And what I found stunned me.

The true strength of seasonality lies in its ability to make you proactive, not reactive. 
You know in advance what you need to pay attention to.
Summer? You know, liquidity tends to dry up.
September and October? You know volatility tends to rise.
Christmas time? You know window dressing flows are likely.
And once you know these seasonal patterns, it’s all about asset selection. In Q1, you 
might focus more on precious metals like gold as they tend to find strength. In Q2, you 
may look for a strong pullback within that same sector, or you may shift your attention 
toward industrial commodities and the energy markets as economic activity picks up. 
In Q3, you become cautious about specific equities or currencies that have historically 
faced seasonal weakness. In Q4, you once again gear up for strength in risk-off assets, 
festive demand, and year-end rebalancing flows. You always have a plan and always 
know what to look out for. And the Sentiment, such as the positioning of the  
Commercials, will confirm every bit of it.
It becomes second nature to align your focus with the seasonal trends in front of you. 
And when those trends align with sentiment extremes or technical patterns, your edge 
becomes stronger and stronger.
This shift in thinking changed the way I see every market.

understanding seasonal patterns can significantly improve your trading.

S E A S O N A L  T E N D E N C I E S  I N  M O T I O N
L
et's first have a look at the Nasdaq. When considering indices, average Retail  
traders often chase headlines, every earnings report for each stock, and every  
sudden market move. They stay glued to the present, reacting to the latest noise and 
trying to outpace the flow of information. But despite the chaos, something deeper often 
begins to emerge.
Patterns. Rhythms. Cycles.
Over time, it becomes clear that the Nasdaq consistently displays distinct seasonal 
trends, moving in predictable cycles year after year. These aren’t just coincidences; they 
are repeatable behaviors embedded in the market structure.
Take April through July, for example. Historically, this period often marks the beginning 
of bullish momentum for the Nasdaq, typically emerging after a short-term pullback or 
consolidation. But why? April signifies the start of a new financial quarter, prompting 
institutional investors to rebalance portfolios and inject fresh capital into technology and 
growth-oriented stocks. 
Coupled with strong first-quarter earnings announcements in late April and May, investor 
optimism surges, lifting market sentiment and fueling a sustained bullish trend. This  
bullish run often carries through July, underpinned by stable market conditions and  
investor confidence, setting the stage for traders to position themselves proactively  
rather than reactively.
121
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
(FutureScope - 30 year lookback - 5 smoothing period)


===== PAGE 132 =====
But as summer deepens, another distinct shift occurs. From August through September, 
the Nasdaq historically faces headwinds. Let's consider the average performance over 
the last 30 years. These months have consistently shown bearish tendencies, driven 
by heightened caution, profit-taking following earlier gains, and reduced market partici-
pation amid lower trading volumes. Investors also become wary ahead of third-quarter 
earnings, adjusting their strategies and reassessing their risk exposure before entering 
the final quarter of the year. Recognizing this recurring bearish phase allows traders to 
prepare, manage risks effectively, and adjust their market positioning strategically.
Then, as October rolls in, the tone shifts once more. From October through January, we 
often observe a seasonal resurgence in bullish momentum. This period blends seam-
lessly with the broader yearly market cycle. It aligns with earnings season for Q3, where 
positive corporate results often reignite investor confidence. Add in the psychological 
impact of year-end performance targets, holiday consumer spending, and institutional 
portfolio adjustments, and the final quarter becomes a prime environment for a bullish 
push. This surge often extends through January, aided by fresh capital allocations at the 
start of the new year. Time and again, this October-to-January window has offered one 
of the most reliable seasonal opportunities in index trading.

What makes this seasonal intelligence valuable? It’s how they frame a trader’s mindset 
around timing and positioning. In a macro uptrend, you can anticipate periods where 
continuation is more likely, such as the bullish stretches from April to July or October to 
January. At the same time, you also learn to be more cautious during historically weaker 
phases like August and September. Knowing this in advance adds a powerful layer to 
decision-making. It allows you to approach the markets with context and preparedness.

Now, let's have a look at silver. We have already discussed gold, but silver is another 
commodity that exhibits clear and measurable seasonal trends. One of the most  
consistent patterns occurs between December and April, during which silver tends to 
perform exceptionally well. Based on a 10-year seasonal average, this bullish period has 
repeated itself across many years, including in 2023. Even though Q1 in 2023  
started with a pullback, the quarter still closed higher than where it opened, highlighting 
the strength of the underlying seasonal push. This recurring upward tendency is often 
supported by fundamental drivers, such as increased industrial demand, restocking  
cycles, and early-year inflows into precious metal ETFs. The strength in silver from  
December to April gives traders a valuable window to plan long-side setups aligned with 
the broader cycle.
On the other hand, from May through August, silver typically experiences a seasonal 
slowdown or outright weakness. This bearish window, again supported by 10-year  
seasonal models, becomes clear when you zoom out and analyze the trend.  
Price action in recent years has mirrored this pattern, often showing signs of exhaustion 
or correction during the summer months. There are a few plausible explanations for this. 
Industrial demand tends to cool during the mid-year period, investor focus shifts away 
from defensive assets, and overall market participation drops as the summer holidays 
approach. For traders aware of this tendency, it presents an excellent opportunity to  
reduce exposure, wait for a pullback, or explore opportunities to short that align with the  
seasonal pressure.

when global capital tends to flow into dollar-denominated assets, as risk sentiment  
becomes more cautious and equity markets often experience seasonal drawdowns.  
This window of dollar strength reinforces the intermarket dynamics we've observed so 
far—where one asset's seasonal strength translates into another’s weakness. It’s all

with foresight and intent.

And this shift doesn’t exist in a vacuum; it ripples outward. When the dollar weakens, 
it creates room for other currencies to strengthen. The Euro, British Pound, Australian 
dollar, and even emerging market currencies often catch a tailwind during this phase, 
as capital rotates away from dollar safety and into higher-yielding or more risk-sensitive 
assets. This inverse relationship plays out consistently in the seasonal rhythm of the 
currency markets.

C O R R E L A T I O N S
I N T E R M A R K E T  R E L A T I O N S  &  C R O S S - A S S E T  L I N K S
H
ere’s one of the things that evolved my trading: I started to notice correlations. My 
first real discovery was with the U.S. Dollar Index. I realized that when the dollar 
was rising, gold was often falling—and when the dollar weakened, gold gained strength. 
It wasn't a perfect mirror, but the pattern was there. That simple observation opened a 
doorway into a new way of viewing the markets and a genuine edge.
I realized the whole market was like an ecosystem—a living, breathing network where 
every part affected the others. Like the oceans, the winds, and the tides, financial  
markets flowed and shifted together, each one pushing and pulling on the others in ways 
that were sometimes subtle, sometimes violent. There is only a limited amount of  
liquidity in the markets, and that liquidity doesn’t stay put. It moves from one sector to 
another, chasing opportunity, reacting to fear, and adapting to changing tides.  
Understanding this movement became essential to understanding how the whole system 
breathes. The good news is that you can use real data to support these claims rather 
than reacting based on feelings.
Markets don’t move alone, they move together. Stocks, bonds, commodities, and curren-
cies are interconnected, much like the gears in a machine. When one turned, the others 
moved too. Sometimes smoothly, sometimes aggressively.
Initially, I noticed the small details. Bond yields would spike, and stocks would suddenly 
wobble. The U.S. dollar would rise, and gold and oil would begin to decline. I didn’t fully 
understand it yet, but I knew one thing: the markets were talking to each other.
This realization was another piece of the puzzle. 
 
128
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 139 =====
To put it in simple terms, think about the Nasdaq. The Nasdaq moves due to the  
collective behavior of all the stocks within it. Apple, Microsoft, Amazon, and so many 
others. If these individual stocks start moving higher or lower, the Nasdaq follows. The 
index is just the surface. To understand it, you have to go deeper, down to the roots, to 
the building blocks that make it up.
It wasn’t enough to stare at one chart anymore. It wasn’t enough to trade a setup  
without context. To truly understand the market, I needed to see the flow of money  
between assets.
Institutions, hedge funds, and so-called smart money understand this deeply. They are 
incredibly aware of the correlations and interconnectivity between different sectors.  
They are also highly tuned into the seasons and the natural rhythms of the market.  
The reason why seasonal patterns work over and over again is because the largest 
players in the game use this information to make their trading decisions. Their behavior 
reinforces these patterns year after year. It all comes down to psychology, the repetitive 
habits and cycles of human behavior on a global scale. Ultimately, the person with the 
most information and data wins.
At LTA, this became a cornerstone of our approach. We follow cross-asset flows to 
understand when risk appetite is growing or shrinking when liquidity is shifting from one 
asset to another, and when major players are quietly adjusting their bets.
Positive and Negative Correlations
Before diving into specific examples, it is important to understand the basic building 
blocks: positive and negative correlations.
A positive correlation means that two assets tend to move in the same direction.  
For example, under normal market conditions, gold and silver tend to move in tandem, 
often rising and falling together. If gold is rallying, silver will likely follow suit.
129

LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
GC1! SI1!

===== PAGE 140 =====
A negative correlation means that two assets tend to move in opposite directions. 
A classic case is the U.S. Dollar Index (DXY) and gold. When the dollar strengthens, 
gold often weakens. When the dollar weakens, gold typically gains strength.
These correlations are not random. They happen because of how money flows  
between assets, how risk is priced, and how investor psychology works across different 
market environments.
Recognizing these relationships helps you to build higher-probability trading ideas. 
When you understand how assets should be moving in relation to each other, it becomes 
much easier to spot when something is confirming a move or when a hidden divergence 
is warning you something might be about to change. Understanding positive and  
negative correlations is the first step in decoding the market's language.
And this is exactly where seasonality became even more critical. If we could determine 
the seasonal direction of one major asset, such as the dollar, gold, or oil, we could then 
anticipate the potential movement of the assets it was correlated with. If seasonality  
suggested that gold was strong in Q4 and the dollar was historically weaker during the 
same time, that alignment provided us with another powerful clue.
We use seasonality as a foundation and then cross-check it with technical confirmations 
and intermarket flows. It’s like watching a weather forecast and then stepping outside to 
feel the wind.
We do not guess the direction, we listen to the story the markets is telling us. It’s about 
building a narrative that makes sense across timeframes and assets. The mistake I see 
traders make is thinking that markets move in straight lines, isolated from one another. 
They don't.
Every move, every surge, every collapse, it's all connected.
Learning to read these flows is like learning a new language. At first, it feels foreign. But 
over time, it becomes second nature. With it comes an edge few traders ever master.

ing too heavily in one direction and how to use that information to anticipate major turns.
What you are learning now about intermarket relationships, correlations, and seasonality 
is stacking those layers of confluence. The power comes from layering sentiment,  
positioning, intermarket flows, and seasonal patterns together to build a strong, confident 
trade idea.
Because when multiple pieces of the puzzle align, you see the market more clearly.
Keep in mind, it doesn't mean that you need every single confirmation possible. If you 
wait for every signal to align perfectly, you will end up not trading at all. Trading is about 
stacking enough evidence to tilt the odds in your favor; it’s not about perfection.

Do you see how important it is to stack these confluences? Do you see how relying  
solely on technical analysis would leave you without the full picture and the real edge?
If you do, we move forward into the next layer of confluence, Valuation.  
We will examine the valuation of an asset, measured not only in its own right but also in 
relation to interconnected, positively correlated, or negatively correlated assets.  
Understanding relative value gives us another decisive advantage when it comes to 
aligning sentiment, positioning, seasonality, and technical structure into a  
comprehensive trading war map.
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S

20
V A L U A T I O N
W O R T H  T H R O U G H  M A R K E T  C O R R E L A T I O N
T
here’s a word traders love to throw around, “undervalued.” You’ll hear it everywhere. 
A stock is undervalued, gold is overvalued, and the dollar is cheap. But how do we 
know? What are we comparing it to?
At first glance, the answer seemed obvious. If the Relative Strength Index (RSI) was 
low, an asset must be undervalued. If it was high, it must be overvalued. It felt simple 
and easy to follow. But that logic didn’t hold up for long, and the more I thought about it, 
the more I realized something didn’t add up. Markets don’t exist in isolation, and price 
levels alone don’t tell the whole story.
The problem with RSI is that it only compares an asset to itself. It tells you whether price 
has moved too far, too fast, relative to its recent past. But markets don’t move in  
isolation. They move in relation to each other. Gold doesn’t just float in space; it moves 
in response to the dollar and other correlated assets. Oil moves in response to economic 
growth. Currencies rise and fall in sync with interest rates, commodity flows, and global 
capital rotation. 
And that’s when it hit me, “true valuation can’t be measured in isolation; it has to be  
measured in relation to something else.”
That’s the heartbeat of relative value. But before we apply any valuation model, we need 
to ensure that the relationship we’re looking at makes sense. Just because two assets 
often move together or move in opposite directions doesn’t mean they always will. This 
is why confirming the correlation first is so important.
That’s where the Correlation Coefficient (CC) tool comes in. This tool helps measure 
how closely two assets are connected; rather than assuming a correlation, it tests it. A 
score close to +1 indicates that the assets move together. A score near -1 means they 
move in opposite directions. And anything near zero? That’s a red flag, the relationship 
may not be reliable at all.
This step is crucial. Many traders wrongly assume that inverse or positive correlations 
are guaranteed; they’re not. Correlation changes over time, in different environments, 
and in response to market conditions. So before applying a valuation model, we always 
check correlation strength first using the Correlation Coefficient (Tradingview). Only 
when it passes that test, we move forward with relative valuation.
134
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 145 =====
To make this more clear, let’s look at two real-world examples. First, consider the Euro 
and the U.S. dollar. These two are historically negatively correlated. When one rises, 
the other often falls. If you apply the Correlation Coefficient (CC) tool to EUR/USD and 
the Dollar Index (DX1!), you'll usually see values close to -1, confirming a strong inverse  
relationship on the daily timeframe. This gives us confidence that if the dollar is 
stretched, EUR/USD likely reflects that imbalance.
On the other hand, look at gold and silver on the daily chart. These two are typically  
positively correlated, meaning they often rise and fall together. When you run a  
Correlation Coefficient between gold (GC1!) and silver (SI1!), the result is usually near 
+1. That confirms the relationship, which then becomes a reliable foundation for relative 
valuation.

However, even the strongest historical patterns can surprise us. Take oil and the  
dollar. The assumption is they are always negatively correlated. It makes sense since 
oil is priced in dollars, so when the dollar increases in value, oil usually becomes more 
expensive for other countries to purchase, which puts downward pressure on oil prices. 
However, if we examine the correlation coefficient through the start of 2025 on the daily 
chart, it indicates a positive correlation.
That means they’ve been moving in the same direction, at least for now. And this is 
exactly why blindly trusting historical relationships can be dangerous. Market conditions 
change, liquidity shifts and Central Banks intervene. Correlations can break, flip, or fade 
altogether.
The correlation coefficient tool helps us avoid this mistake. It tells us what’s happening, 
and that’s what makes it so valuable. Once we confirm that the two assets are strongly 
connected positively or negatively, we can confidently move forward using our valuation 
framework.
Once a correlation is confirmed, we use our valuation indicator to spot opportunities 
based on stretched relationships that are due to snap back into balance. That’s where 
the real edge lies.
Here’s the key difference: while the RSI reacts to the speed of price, valuation measures 
the relationship. RSI tells you, “this asset is moving fast.” Valuation asks, “is this asset 
moving too far away from its fundamental counterpart?” It’s a different question and a 
more important one.
Take the dollar and gold. When the dollar strengthens too quickly, gold often lags or 
weakens. But sometimes, the gap between them gets too wide. 
That’s where the opportunity lies.

Remove the guesswork so you can see what’s stretched and know what has room to 
snap back. That’s the edge valuation gives you. And when combined with sentiment, 
seasonality, and macro positioning, it becomes one of the most powerful tools in your 
entire trading system.
So, how does our valuation tool work? While we won't delve into every technical detail, 
the concept is straightforward: we calculate the percentage change of an asset over a 
specified period and compare it to the percentage change of a related asset. We then 
calculate the difference between the two and normalize it, placing the result on a scale 
from -100 to +100. This helps us see how far one asset has stretched away from its  
usual relationship with the other. If the stretch is too wide, it often snaps back.  
That stretch is our edge.
We’ve built a growing library of backtesting results that demonstrate how this logic un-
folds over time. Here’s one example: As gold is in a macro uptrend, when gold becomes 
undervalued relative to the dollar, confirmed through both correlation and valuation, we 
often see price begin to stabilize and bottom out. This has happened again and again 
during periods when sentiment was fearful, but the value gap signaled something  
different. In the next section, I’ll walk you through a few of these real-time examples so 
you can see the logic unfold in practice.

Now, let’s look at the dollar against the Euro. These two have long shown a strong  
negative correlation, but what happens when that relationship becomes stretched?  
Using our valuation model, we can track when the dollar becomes overvalued compared 
to the Euro. These moments often occur when the dollar rallies quickly in response to 
macroeconomic news, while the Euro lags behind. When the correlation remains intact, 
and the valuation metric pushes into extreme zones, we often see the Euro begin to 
stabilize and bounce just as the dollar starts to cool off. This imbalance, confirmed by 
correlation and measured through valuation, creates a window for directional trades.
What about gold and silver? These two typically share a strong positive correlation.  
But now and then, silver gets left behind. Our valuation model enables us to identify 
when silver is undervalued relative to gold. When this happens, and the correlation 
remains strong, it’s often a sign that silver is due to play catch-up. These moments don’t 
just represent pricing inefficiencies - they reveal when capital flow into precious metals 
hasn’t been fully priced into silver yet. That’s when we start preparing to strike.

Another notable case we’ve observed is the relationship between the Nasdaq and the 
bond market. These two often share a negative correlation. When bonds rise typically 
due to falling interest rates, tech-heavy indices like the Nasdaq tend to benefit. However, 
when bond prices fall, and yields rise, risk assets often retreat. That’s the natural push 
and pull between growth expectations and borrowing costs.
What’s especially interesting is when the NQ1! becomes undervalued relative to the 
ZB1!, primarily because we have been in a long-term macro uptrend. In our backtests, 
these moments often set up strong buying opportunities. It’s not just about price looking 
cheap; it’s about the relationship signaling that equities are lagging behind where they 
should be if bond strength continues. That divergence is what makes it powerful.
Why does this work? Because bonds are often the first to move. They respond quickly 
to changes in inflation expectations, Fed policy, and macro risk. Stocks, especially those 
in the tech sector, tend to catch up later. When valuation reveals that gap, it provides us 
with early intelligence and a chance to act before the rest of the market does.
This is exactly where valuation becomes part of a bigger macro-fundamental puzzle. 
On its own, it offers insight into price relationships, but when combined with other pillars 
of analysis, its power multiplies. Sentiment data can reveal how traders are positioned, 
seasonality can indicate which parts of the year tend to favor certain moves, and  
macroeconomic context helps us understand the broader picture.  
When all these tools align, when sentiment is supportive, seasonality is favorable, and 
valuation shows something is cheap or expensive relative to its counterpart, that's when 
conviction is highest. 
That's when data becomes a driving force behind a decision.
With our macro-fundamental groundwork in place, the next step is learning how to  
identify these dynamics visually on a chart. We’ll now shift gears into macro-technical 
analysis, specifically, how supply and demand play out in real-time and how you can 
spot those shifts not just in theory but on your screen.

21
S U P P L Y  &  D E M A N D
T H E  E X C H A N G E  T H A T  D R I V E S  M A R K E T S
T
o truly understand supply and demand, you have to step away from the chart. Before 
it became a trading concept, before people marked zones or planned entries, supply 
and demand was something far more fundamental; it was how civilization functioned.
From the earliest markets in Mesopotamia and Egypt to the spice trade of Venice and 
the Silk Road of China, the world has always revolved around one idea: exchange.  
People gathered to trade what they had for what they needed. Farmers brought grain, 
fishermen brought their catch, and artisans offered tools and textiles. But what made this 
work was the opposing needs.
Someone needed to buy. Someone was willing to sell. And somewhere in the middle, a 
price was agreed upon. Every deal was a test of value. How much is this worth to you? 
How badly do I need it? How scarce is the product? How urgent is the need? These  
fundamental questions built the foundation of what we now call price.
142


===== PAGE 153 =====
As trade expanded, marketplaces grew more complex. In ancient Greece and Rome, 
forums became hubs of commerce. In medieval Europe, local fairs and permanent 
market towns formed the backbone of economic life. By the 17th century, with the rise of 
Amsterdam and London, supply and demand began to formalize. The idea of price being 
set by competing bids and offers, rather than decree or tradition, was revolutionary.  
Markets were no longer just physical spaces; they had become mechanisms for  
discovering value.
And that’s the essence of it, “markets exist to find value between buyers and sellers.”
The birth of stock exchanges in the 1600s was built on this very idea. The Dutch East 
India Company issued shares, and people began to trade those shares in coffee houses. 
The price of a share wasn’t fixed; it was determined by the demand for it and the  
willingness of someone else to sell. Every tick in price was a negotiation. Every trade 
was a new agreement.
This is how supply and demand evolved from physical goods to financial instruments. 
The logic stayed the same, but the tools changed. Underneath it all, every trade from 
gold and grain to Tesla shares or Euro Futures is still just one person saying, “I want 
this,” and another saying, “Here’s my price.”
143
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
S  U  P  P  L  Y    A  N  D    D  E  M  A  N  D


===== PAGE 154 =====
That interaction, that constant back-and-forth, is what creates price. And when one  
side pushes harder, when demand surges or supply dries up, price moves. What  
drives markets are the people inside them. Every movement in price is shaped by the  
decisions, emotions, and urgency of those participating. Whether it's a surge of  
optimism, a rush of fear, or a quiet wave of confidence, the price reflects the collective 
psychology of the crowd in real-time.
Before we ever mark a level or define a zone, we need to understand that supply and 
demand is not a trading tactic. It’s the engine behind every market that has ever existed.
144
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S

T H E  T W O  S I D E S
F U N D A M E N T A L S  V S  T E C H N I C A L S
E
arly on, I used to treat fundamentals and technicals like two separate schools of 
thought, as if I had to pick a side. However, over time, I began to realize that they 
weren’t at odds. In fact, they were two sides of the same coin.
Everything we’ve covered up to this point—sentiment analysis, valuation extremes,  
seasonality patterns, positioning by commercials and speculators—is all part of the 
fundamental picture. These are the deeper forces that shape the flow of capital and the 
direction of markets. This is the why behind the movement. Fundamentals build your 
macro bias; they tell you where pressure is building and where the market is likely to 
shift in the bigger picture.
However, here’s the challenge, fundamentals don’t move price on a day-to-day basis. 
They create expectations. You can build the most solid macro case for a move, but 
without the right timing, that conviction can turn into frustration fast. Fundamentals are 
essential, but they’re not precise enough for execution.
And this is where technical analysis comes in.
Technicals aren’t about the why, they’re about the when. They help you read structure, 
identify imbalances, measure volume, and spot real-time shifts in behavior. You can see 
when price is coiling, when it's ready to break, when it's respecting a level, and when 
momentum is shifting. Technicals respond, showing you what the market is doing right 
now.
So, what happens when you use only one side of the coin?
If you rely purely on fundamentals, you might be early, sometimes painfully early. You’ll 
see that something should move, but it just doesn’t yet. If you rely purely on technicals, 
you might catch a beautiful pattern that has no real backing, no real reason to hold, and 
it collapses the moment volatility returns.
But when you blend both, something changes. You’re not reacting emotionally to every 
move. You have a direction from your fundamentals and a sniper execution for your 
technicals. You know what you’re looking for, and you know when it’s happening.
This is what LTA Concepts 2.0 is built on: a macro-fundamental framework that provides 
conviction, layered with a technical structure that delivers precision.  
It’s the moment when sentiment, valuation, and seasonality align, and price confirms it 
with imbalance, a breakout, or a clean shift in structure.
It’s not about choosing sides; it’s about knowing what each side offers and combining 
them to build something that works.
