LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 157 =====


===== PAGE 158 =====
23
A U C T I O N  M A R K E T  T H E O R Y
B U Y E R S  V S  S E L L E R S
F
rom a technical trading perspective, you’ve likely come across the term “Supply and 
Demand” more times than you can count. It’s one of the most searched and  
talked-about topics in trading. But here’s what most people miss: before you ever draw 
a zone on your chart, you need to ask a more important question. What do those zones 
represent? What’s happening beneath the surface that gives those levels meaning and 
makes price react to them?
Have you ever wondered why price sometimes explodes out of a range with no  
warning, or stalls at the same level for hours? Over time, I began to notice that price 
wasn’t random at all. It was responding to something beneath the surface, pressure 
building, decisions being made, liquidity shifting hands.
At first, I was just trying to make sense of those moves. Some days, a news event made 
everything clear. Other times, nothing seemed to explain it. The candles moved, but the 
“why” remained hidden.
I began to notice patterns in the pauses and bursts. Price would coil, then surge. Ranges 
would hold, then snap. There was rhythm. What I didn’t realize at the time was that I was 
watching the mechanics of the auction—the process of buyers and sellers negotiating 
value in real-time.
I had forgotten that every candle is a collective decision. It's so easy just to get lost 
watching the candlestick charts. However, price only moves because someone steps in, 
driven by fear, urgency, or conviction.
This brings us to a turning point: imbalance.


===== PAGE 159 =====
Imbalance is when that urgency becomes visible. It’s when one side, either buyers or 
sellers, completely overpowers the other. You’ll know it when you see it. Price doesn’t 
hesitate, it doesn’t range, it just runs. The candles stretch, volume expands, and the 
auction no longer negotiates. It drives in one direction with ease.  This is when price tells 
you something meaningful just happened, someone acted with size, and the other side 
didn’t respond.
But imbalance doesn’t come out of nowhere.
Before we talk about what sets the stage for imbalance, we need to understand what  
imbalance means. In the context of Auction Market Theory, an imbalance occurs when 
one side of the market completely overpowers the other. Price doesn’t hesitate or rotate; 
it runs. You’ll see candles stack, volume build, and directional momentum take over. It’s 
a break from negotiation into action. That kind of movement indicates that buyers or  
sellers are no longer debating; they’re executing with urgency.
Now, for that kind of imbalance to happen, something else must occur first. The market 
must enter a phase of balance where buyers and sellers are relatively well-matched. The 
price fluctuates within a tight range, volume builds evenly, and neither side gains control. 
Large participants quietly accumulate or distribute positions, liquidity is absorbed, and 
the conditions are created for that eventual break. That’s why balance is so important; 
it’s the calm before the move.
Once I adopted this perspective, Auction Market Theory began to make sense. It helped 
me understand how price behaves within balance and how it behaves once the balance 
is broken. That balance-to-imbalance shift showed up everywhere.
Here is an example that stands out. One morning, the Euro was trading in a tight range, 
quiet, slow, with no momentum. Then the inflation data came out. It shocked the market. 
Within minutes, price exploded through resistance. Volume spiked. There was no  
rotation back. Buyers had taken control: Imbalance. And now, I could see it unfolding 
instead of just reacting to it.
149
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
S  U  P  P  L  Y    A  N  D    D  E  M  A  N  D
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
Bullish engulfing candle 
Inflation data 
release


===== PAGE 160 =====
This was a big “Aha” moment; strong areas of demand or supply aren’t created during 
the breakout; they’re created before it. They’re built in the balance, in the absorption, 
in the positioning. The imbalance is just the moment that exposes it. So if you’re only 
watching for the move, you’re late.
It became clear that all of this—the negotiation, the pressure, the imbalance—was  
simply supply and demand in motion.
As we move forward, we’ll focus on mapping the auction in real-time and how to  
recognize balance or imbalance, which will help you spot and mark out the proper supply 
and demand zones.
This is where Auction Market Theory becomes practical inside the LTA framework. It 
gives us the structural awareness we need to know when the market is balanced, break-
ing, or searching for value. Everything we build in LTA, as we have already discussed, 
such as COT positioning, valuation, and seasonality, must ultimately lead to one thing: 
knowing where and when to act.
Take a simple example. Let’s say the commercials are heavily long; valuation shows that 
the Japanese Yen is undervalued against the DX1!, and seasonality indicates we are in 
a cyclical low. According to seasonal tendencies, we should be moving up. That gives us 
a bullish macro bias. However, if price remains stuck within a slow, sideways range or 
down trend, that’s not our entry. We wait. Then suddenly, price bursts out of that range. 
The volume picks up, the auction shifts and you can sense new demand emerging. That  
moment, that shift from balance to imbalance is what we’re watching for.
That’s our Macro Technical confirmation, and that’s the moment we take action.
Auction Market Theory doesn’t stand alone; it brings the rest of our work to life by telling 
us when those higher time-frame conditions are finally starting to express themselves in 
real price action.

B E H I N D  T H E  B R E A K
I D E N T I F Y I N G  T R U E  S U P P L Y  &  D E M A N D
Y
ou now understand how markets function through Auction Market Theory, and you’ve 
seen how imbalance leads to opportunity. Let’s take that one step further. How do 
we visualize the relationship between supply and demand? The easiest way to under-
stand how price responds to an imbalance is by learning to identify it directly on a chart. 
Let me show you how to recognize the clearest types of supply and demand patterns 
and how they set the stage for some of the most reliable macro trading setups.
Most trading gurus tell you that it’s about spotting “the perfect candle,” some textbook 
formation that had to look a certain way; at least, that's what you find on the internet if 
you do some research. But that is not the case at all; from my experience, the shape 
of the candle doesn’t matter as much as what it represents. It’s not about the wick, the 
body type, or the color; it’s about the shift that takes place. That means that if you limit 
your focus to doji candles with long wicks and small bodies only, you'll likely miss out on 
a wide range of potential opportunities over time. Your focus should be on understanding 
whether buyers or sellers are in control; I’ll discuss this in the next chapter. 
Here’s something interesting I learned: the clearest areas of supply and demand almost  
always formed right before a breakout, a point where structure broke, and volum surged. 
The candle that causes that break, the one just before price expands with strength, that’s 
the one that usually tells the story. That’s where the zone is created. It’s not about  
finding a perfect candle; it’s about understanding what that candle is doing and what 
came before it.
152
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

candle

===== PAGE 163 =====
And that’s what we’re looking for: the transition from balance to imbalance.  
The buildup, the hesitation, the coil, and then the release. That release is the market 
showing its hand. The zone is built from the actions taken in the moments just before 
that release. 
Now that we understand this conceptually let’s move into the more technical side of 
how this may look on a chart. One of the cleanest visual formations is what I call the 
three-candle bar play, commonly referred to as the four core patterns of supply and  
demand:
The easiest way I’ve learned to spot high-probability supply and demand zones is by 
watching for a break in structure. When the market is in balance, prices fluctuate  
within a range. But the moment that balance breaks, something important is happening. 
Often, it’s an increase in volume, a sudden directional push, or a candle that expands 
aggressively from a coiled area of price. That candle, the one right before the breakout, 
is almost always where the supply or demand is created. It’s the footprint of institutional 
action the moment one side took control.

Once you understand this, everything else becomes easier to see. That one candle, 
combined with a noticeable shift in volume or a strong imbalance, tells you where the 
zone starts. The key is to look at what price did before it moved and ask yourself,  
“was it balanced, or was it already breaking?”
Supply and demand patterns are visual representations of price movements that indicate 
potential areas of interest for traders. A sequence of price actions forms these patterns: 
an initial move (either upward or downward), a period of consolidation (the base), and a 
subsequent move in the same or opposite direction.
The four primary patterns are:
Rally-Base-Rally (RBR)
• Structure: An initial upward move (rally), followed by a consolidation (base), and 
then another upward move (rally).
• Indicates: A continuation of an uptrend, suggesting a demand zone was formed.
• Trading Insight: The base represents an area where selling interest is strong 
enough to pause the price's upward momentum.
154
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
S  U  P  P  L  Y    A  N  D    D  E  M  A  N  D
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
R
R
B
Rally
Base
Rally


===== PAGE 165 =====
Drop-Base-Drop (DBD)
• Structure: An initial downward move (drop), followed by a consolidation (base), and 
then another downward move (drop).
• Indicates: A continuation of a downtrend, suggesting a supply zone was created.
• Trading Insight: The base indicates a temporary pause before sellers regain control, 
pushing the price lower.
Drop-Base-Rally (DBR) 
• Structure: An initial downward move (drop), followed by a consolidation (base), and 
then an upward move (rally).
• Indicates: A reversal from a downtrend to an uptrend, forming a demand zone.
• Trading Insight: The base acts as a turning point where buying pressure overcomes 
selling pressure.

Rally-Base-Drop (RBD)
• Structure: An initial upward move (rally), followed by a consolidation (base), and 
then a downward move (drop).
• Indicates: A reversal from an uptrend to a downtrend, forming a supply zone.
• Trading Insight: The base signifies a shift where selling pressure overtakes buying 
pressure.
Spotting Strong Supply and Demand Zones
• Sharp Moves: Zones formed after an intense and swift price movement are more 
significant.
• Untested: Zones that haven’t been tested multiple times tend to be more reliable. 
But this doesn’t necessarily mean that tested zones are invalidated.
• Volume: High volume during the formation of the base can signify institutional  
interest.
• Three Candle Bar Play: When the third candle exceeds the first candle, we can con-
sider the base that has formed to be an area of supply or demand. This means that a 
Three Candle Bar Play also appear after a longer base/consolidation is created.

Understanding Fractals and Timeframe Selection
As you start identifying these patterns, it's easy to get caught up in marking every zone 
on every timeframe. However, within the LTA framework, we focus primarily on higher  
timeframes, starting with the monthly, weekly, then the 3-day, daily, 12-hour, and 8-hour 
charts. In this case, I always go from high to low, as the higher the supply or demand, 
the more reliable it is.
Why? Because supply and demand zones on higher timeframes are stronger, more 
respected, and more reliable. They reflect bigger institutional moves. Lower-timeframe 
zones often lack context, are absorbed quickly, or generate noise that pulls you away 
from the bigger picture.
Our job is to identify where the conviction started, and that usually appears first on the 
higher timeframes. Once we know where the key supply and demand is, we can then 
zoom in with volume and structure to time our execution. This is exactly why, in the LTA 
framework, we rely on a volume-based execution strategy when trading lower time-
frames. It allows us to see where participation shifts in real-time, providing clarity and 
confirmation at the micro level while staying anchored to the higher timeframe bias. I can 
assure you that the more you practice this, the more natural it will become. At first, 
your charts might look messy. You’ll question your zones, doubt your levels, and  
hesitate when price reacts. However, over time, that hesitation fades, patterns become 
more obvious, zones become clearer, and your confidence grows.

25
T H E  B A T T L E  F O R  C O N T R O L
D O M I N A N C E  D E C O D E D
O
nce you know how to draw supply and demand zones, it all comes down to using 
them correctly. If a demand zone takes out a supply zone, we can confidently say 
that demand is in control, especially when your fundamental data is backing up this exact 
claim. In this chapter, I will explain to you how a simple trick can help you understand 
who is in control: supply or demand. And no, it is not only looking for a simple break of 
structure or a surge in volume. In this case, we are looking for a macro structural shift.
Behind the candles and structures, trading is a war between supply and demand.  
And just like any battlefield, there’s always a side gaining the edge. The sooner you learn 
to recognize which side that is, the higher your probability of being on the winning side. 
Who’s in control shifts all the time. Sometimes, it’s subtle, like a slow grind upward.  
At other times, it’s explosive when a price launches out of a zone, the result of an  
imbalance. The origin of imbalance is always the same, one side overwhelming the other.
159


===== PAGE 170 =====
I want to remind you, looking at candlesticks only is not enough. We all look at them and 
see them on the chart, but what I mean is, what’s the real storyline behind them? Who 
absorbed the last sell-off? Who pushed through resistance without hesitation? Was it 
buyers stepping in with conviction or sellers running out of steam? These moments of 
transition are where control changes hands.
We observe these shifts by reading price behavior through the lens of Auction Market 
Theory and technical structure. For instance, when a market is balanced and  
consolidating, neither side has the upper hand. However, the moment price breaks that 
balance with momentum, it tells you something has shifted. Either demand overpowered 
supply, or supply overwhelmed demand.
The structures we’ve discussed—RBR, RBD, DBR, and DBD—are just the visual  
consequences of that shift. But that doesn’t always tell you who is actually in control, 
even though a new supply or demand has just been formed. One of the key confirma-
tions we look for is whether a zone is taking something out. In other words, when a 
demand zone is formed, does it take out a previous area of supply? If it does, that shift 
tells us something important: buyers overpowered the last area where sellers were in 
control. We refer to this as a macrostructural shift. Not always from a “Market Structure” 
standpoint, which is what most traders are looking at. No, in this case, it’s about a power 
shift that is taking place behind the scenes.
You’ll see this again and again. A demand zone forms, then price drives through a supply 
zone. That demand zone now becomes significant. It held, broke resistance, and is 
likely to become a strong continuation zone. Often, after this break, price will pull back 
and revisit the demand area before continuing higher. The same is true in reverse. If a 
supply zone takes out a demand area, the market may retest that supply before moving 
lower. What’s important to mention here is that this demand was created right before it 
took out a supply zone, marking its relevance and effectiveness.

===== PAGE 171 =====
This doesn’t have to be on the same timeframe; it could easily be when a daily supply 
zone has been taken out and an 8-hour demand zone was created beforehand. So, 
even though on the Daily timeframe it might look like just a strong bullish engulfing  
candle, in reality, a base was created on the 8h timeframe before a strong impulse, 
which then took out that supply zone.
These structural shifts give us the clearest indication of who is in control and where they 
have established that control on the chart. The aftermath of a battle where one side 
clearly won. That’s why those zones become so important; they represent areas where 
control was established.
However, just because a zone formed, that doesn’t mean control will last.  
That’s why we always pair structure with volume. Volume shows whether that move 
had weight behind it. A high-volume push out of a base means real conviction. 
A low volume move? That’s shaky ground.
Control is dynamic. It changes from day to day, sometimes even from hour to hour. Our 
job as traders is to track it so we can follow the most prominent players in the market.

26
D A T A  I N  Z O N E S
S T A C K I N G  P R O B A B I L I T I E S 
L
et’s imagine you’re trading purely based on supply and demand zones. You’ve 
marked your level, price drops into it, and then, without warning, it breaks straight 
through. You were confident it would hold, but it didn’t. And now you're left wondering, 
why did that zone fail?
This is the missing piece to the puzzle. The zone didn’t break because supply and  
demand stopped working; it broke because you didn't fully understand the context. What 
if you could change that? What if you had a way to confirm what was happening behind 
the scenes?
That’s precisely what happens when we combine macro fundamentals with our zones. 
Tools like the Commitment of Traders (COT) report allow us to see whether  
accumulation or distribution is taking place: real data and institutional positioning.
We can take a demand zone and confidently say this is a place where smart money was 
buying. On top of that, we can look at valuation. If the asset was undervalued when the 
zone formed, that adds another layer. Then, consider seasonality. Has this time of year 
historically supported the upward movement?  Stacking these confluences gives us even 
more reason to trust it.
On their own, supply or demand zones don’t tell the whole story. However, when you  
factor in tools such as sentiment, positioning, seasonality, and valuation, you gain a 
clearer picture.
163


===== PAGE 174 =====
Let’s start with sentiment. One tool we use is the Smart Money Intelligence™  which 
stems from the COT report. It helps us see what big institutions were doing when a zone 
formed. For example, if we’re in a contrarian phase and commercials (the smart  
money) are buying heavily while a demand zone is forming, that’s a strong signal. It 
means they’re stepping in while everyone else is scared.
During momentum phases, we focus more on Large Speculators. If they’re increasing 
long positions while a demand zone forms, that indicates they're driving the trend. This 
gives us more reason to trust that zone.
Imagine price touches a weekly demand zone, that might catch our attention. But if our 
Stealth Valuation Index™  also says the asset is undervalued compared to something 
like the dollar, we have a second reason to watch that level. It means the zone is backed 
by value.
If our FutureScope™  indicates that an asset typically performs well during this part of 
the year, such as gold in the first quarter, and it enters a demand zone with positive sen-
timent while being undervalued, that provides a powerful combination. We refer to that 
as a "stack" in LTA Concepts.
You don’t always need all four to line up. Sometimes, just one or two strong fundamen-
tals are enough. We’ve tested this across many markets, and some setups work better 
in commodities, others in currencies or indices.
The key is knowing how and when to use each indicator. If even one supports your zone, 
it could be enough. When two or three line up, you can act with real conviction.
Here’s a momentum driven example. In February, we spotted a clear demand zone on 
the Japanese Yen. Price dropped, then stabilized. But more importantly, the COT report 
showed that large speculators were buying heavily at the same time. That was the  
confirmation we needed. This bounce was backed by strong buying from key players.

surface. Commercials were absorbing liquidity, while retail and trend followers were likely 
panicking.
By using the Smart Money Intelligence™  indicator to spot these extremes, we can  
anticipate reversals with greater clarity. When sentiment is stretched and price taps into 
a major zone, the likelihood of a reversal increases significantly.

measures the performance of one asset relative to another. For example, in an earlier 
chapter, we examined gold in relation to the dollar. We observed how its undervalued 
status, based on that relationship, helped us identify a key turning point.

Now, with the understanding of how COT positioning and valuation extremes can help us 
validate an area of supply or demand. I want to bring back the importance of seasonality. 
Here’s a great gold example: On the daily chart, price tapped into a clear demand zone 
around the same time that our seasonality model (AI Futurescope™) signaled a likely 
cycle low. As you can see, it aligns with a historically strong time of year for gold, based 
on a 10-year average. That alignment gave us more confidence. When seasonality and 
structure confirm with each other, the zone becomes significantly more interesting.
Last but not least,  let’s talk about one of the most underrated topics: Open Interest. 
In another gold example, we observed price tapping into a weekly demand zone while 
Open Interest declined significantly, based on a 26-week comparison from our Smart 
Money Intelligence™ indicator. This decline in Open Interest suggested a drop in new 
speculative participation, hinting that the current trend was weakening as we slowly ap-
proached our demand zone. With fewer new participants entering the market and price 
reacting to a major zone, the likelihood of a reversal increased. 
This additional insight added clarity and confidence to the zone itself. Just think about 
this: if there is no “new money” and the “interest” is decreasing, where will the volume 
come from to break this strong weekly area of demand? At the same time, if the Open 
Interest is increasing rapidly, and you can confirm with the COT data who is buying or 
selling, you can also avoid taking unnecessary losses by combining macro structure with 
these additional fundamental layers. 
This is why Open Interest can help you understand whether a trend is strengthening or 
weakening, as well as whether a supply or demand zone is more likely to hold.

From a technical perspective, supply and demand will always be the starting point. But

I N T R A  D A Y
T R E N D  D E V E L O P M E N T  &  M A R K E T  S T R U C T U R E
B
y the time we reach the intraday timeframe within the LTA framework, much of the 
heavy lifting has already been done. We’ve covered the Macro-Fundamentals, 
including COT positioning and extremes, valuation, and seasonality, and I’ve demon-
strated how a change in Open Interest can stack on top of your decisions. We’ve also 
mapped out the key higher timeframe macro technical zones using Supply & Demand. 
But that still leaves a crucial gap, when exactly do we act, and if I want to enter a trade, 
does this require me to stick to the higher timeframes? The simple answer is no. We will 
use all of this information as context before moving to the intra-day to prepare for  
execution on the lower timeframes. 
This is something I have been thinking about for a long time. By nature, I'm a day  
trader, and by studying Larry Williams's material, I was convinced that I needed to find 
a way to merge these two worlds together, the macro analysis and historical data, with 
micro execution.
And after some time, I found an unconventional answer. I was able to turn something 
that usually only swing traders use into a day trading framework. Trades can be  
executed within the same day, or if we want to, we can hold the trade for a much longer 
period of time. This is the hybrid layer, the bridge that ties the broader story to the 
lower timeframe execution. The intra-day helps us zoom in on structure and trend  
development to spot where the macro story is starting to play out.
This allows us to test our higher-timeframe thesis in real-time. A weekly Demand zone 
with fundamental backing may provide a directional bias, but until we see how price  
behaves within that zone on the 4H or 1H chart, we’re still waiting for a real confirmation.
When people tell you that “the trend is your friend”, they are often right. However, what 
most traders overlook is that timing is crucial; otherwise, you will end up late to every 
party, missing out on all the fun. Another mistake I see traders make is that they jump 
into setups the moment price touches a key level. We’ve seen this over and over again 
during the release of LTA Concepts 1.0. But without an intraday confirmation, those 
entries often feel rushed or random. This middle layer gives us the confidence to act with 
intel. Therefore, we let the trend reveal itself first.
170
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 181 =====
Think about it like this: as someone from the Netherlands, I grew up riding my bicycle 
from a young age. Every single day, for years, I'd bike to school. Here's a fun fact: in the 
Netherlands, there are more bicycles than people! With a population of about 17.7  
million, there are roughly 23 million bicycles. If you're Dutch, you already know the  
daily struggle; we have one enemy, the wind, and a lot of it. Some days were great, while 
others were tough. With the wind behind me, I could get to school in just 20 minutes. But 
when the wind turned against me, it sometimes took up to an hour to arrive exhausted. 
I use this cycling experience as an analogy for trading, as trading shares many similari-
ties. Sure, catching the perfect top or bottom in the market sounds exciting, but how  
often do you think you can consistently pull that off? This is where the saying "the trend 
is your friend" becomes relevant. Following the market trend is like cycling with the wind 
at your back; it simply makes the ride smoother and easier. You simply need to know 
how to align it with the macro charts to execute it properly.
Intraday market structure tells us how price behaves around previous highs and lows. 
Are we putting in higher highs and higher lows? Are we forming clean ranges and  
breaking them with volume? Are we rejecting value areas or holding them? These clues 
let us see if the macro alignment is beginning to unfold.
Let’s start with an example: imagine we’re sitting in a macro demand zone with bullish 
seasonal and sentiment data behind us. Price drops into the zone, and we wait. On the 
4H, we start to see a shift. Structure flips from lower lows to higher highs. Volume starts 
to increase during rallies and decreases on pullbacks. That’s the beginning of a new 
intraday trend, and it confirms our larger thesis.
171

===== PAGE 182 =====
The best part? This doesn’t require predicting anything, we’re not forecasting, we’re 
waiting. We are letting price prove that the higher timeframe conviction is starting to play 
out, and when it does, we step in knowing the context. That’s important because if this 
doesn't unfold, we don't have to act, which will save us a lot of hard-earned money.
Now that we’ve laid the foundation let’s discuss how we define and recognize intraday 
trend structure. This is where subjectivity can creep in because what one trader calls a 
trend, another might see as a range.
To truly define an intraday structure, we look for at least two touch points. Whether it's 
a higher low and a higher high for a potential long or a lower high and a lower low for a 
short, these two reference points begin to shape the trend. Once these are established, 
we can begin to connect the structure and make adjustments as price develops further.
A trend develops over time. At the beginning, it may seem unclear. Then, as more price 
action unfolds and additional levels are tested, the direction becomes easier to read and 
more actionable. That’s why it’s so important to focus on identifying the early structure 
without needing perfection.
So, how do we address that subjectivity? By identifying the structure of the market itself. 
We already know that we’ve tapped into a macro demand zone. Sentiment, seasonality, 
and valuation may already be aligned, so we’re not starting from scratch. We’re narrow-
ing in, and even though the intraday trend might look unclear at the start, that’s okay. 
Our framework allows us to build into the trade as price confirms.
We look for clean structure and confirmation around key levels. That means watching 
how price behaves around supply and demand zones, previous highs and lows, and 
the edges of the prior week’s trading range. When a price begins to aggressively break 
structure, typically indicated by momentum candles or spikes in volume, we know that 
something is shifting.

This is where we also introduce the idea of a preparation phase and an execution phase. 
Not every trend starts cleanly. Sometimes price will break structure temporarily, then 
fade back into a range. This doesn’t mean the opportunity is gone; it just means the 
market is waiting to re-establish direction. Once we get two clear points and a break of 
structure, we can label that move as entering the execution phase.  
Until then, it’s preparation.
One of our most reliable markers is the break of the previous week’s high or low. This 
signals that we’ve cleared a significant barrier and opens the door to continuation.  
Trading above the previous week’s high or below the previous week’s low gives the 
move added probability, especially when aligned with macro data.

Why is this so important? Because the previous week’s high or low is a significant  
technical level that many market participants are watching. When price breaks through 
one of these levels, it shows that the market has accepted a new value area. This means 
that more buyers are willing to step in at higher prices, or more sellers are willing to  
offload at lower prices.
That kind of confirmation tells us a lot. It means we’re no longer stuck inside the prior 
week’s range. We’ve entered a new auction where new participants can drive price. 
When we trade above the previous week’s high in a bullish context or below the previous 
week’s low in a bearish one, we’re tapping into momentum that’s already been proven. 
This aligns beautifully with everything we’ve prepared on the macro level.

28
I N T R A  D A Y
A C C U M U L A T I O N  &  D I S T R I B U T I O N
W
hen do we anticipate a macro reversal? Good question. Here’s the answer.  
Positioning reaches extreme levels, valuation starts flashing green, and finally, 
price taps into a significant macro supply or demand zone, confirming that the trend 
may be nearing its end and setting up for a reversal. However, once this macro zone is 
reached, price rarely pivots instantly. Typically, the higher the timeframe of your macro 
zone, the longer it takes for price action to reveal its next move. Think about it: if you tap 
into a strong Weekly or Monthly zone, you’re setting in a strong top or bottom wick on 
the next candle, which then, from an internal standpoint, leads us to a significant period 
of consolidation. 
But what if I told you these ranging periods are among the most critical times to watch 
the charts? These are the moments when price is on the verge of a breakout or reversal, 
potentially giving rise to a fresh intraday trend and setting you up perfectly for new set-
ups and sniper executions.
Why is that important? These phases often serve as a bridge between the macro context 
and the intraday trend. They reflect the market's process of absorbing liquidity before a 
new move begins. Accumulation is the phase where strong hands quietly step in after a 
downtrend, absorbing all the selling pressure without allowing price to drop significantly 
lower. Distribution, on the other hand, is the opposite. It’s where strong hands start  
offloading into strength, topping out at the highs and preparing for the next move down. 
It’s when the crowd is the most aggressive and where the smart money is quietly setting 
up for a reversal. 
From an intraday perspective, recognizing these phases gives us a significant edge. 
Most traders view consolidation as “chop”, something to be avoided. However, within the 
LTA framework, this represents the early groundwork for the next move. These  
ranges are where the larger players position themselves, and it’s our job to recognize 
that positioning.
In fact, this is often where we get our first glimpse of an intraday trend forming.  
These ranges, if broken with strength, give birth to the trend. However, to get there, 
the market first needs to establish a structure, eliminate weak hands, and allow liquidity 
to stabilize. That’s why understanding accumulation and distribution is so valuable.
176
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 187 =====
Think of a macro demand zone. When price enters that area, instead of bouncing, it 
stalls. We start to see wicks on the downside, decreasing volume, and a general  
slowdown of bearish momentum. A failed attempt to move lower and quickly reverse is 
possible. That's the accumulation phase. It's not loud or obvious, it's subtle. But it sets 
the tone for what may come next.
Imagine price enters a macro supply zone. The rally begins to slow, momentum fades, 
and price starts forming lower highs. The market's character changes: distribution is  
beginning. Sellers step in, absorb demand, and preparing for the next drop.
It’s important to understand that these phases can take time. On a weekly chart, it might 
look like a single wick. But on a 4H or 1H chart, there’s a whole battle happening. That’s 
why we zoom in. To spot the signs before the breakout.
So, instead of expecting a sharp V-reversal when price taps a macro zone, we anticipate 
the process. We look for failed breaks, shifts in character, slowing momentum, and  
gradual development of structure. We’re not just waiting for a breakout; we’re studying 
the behavior inside the range by looking at different types of patterns that can occur 
while a large range is forming.

This is exactly where we bring back the concepts introduced in our volume analysis in 
Chapter 1: the CERC and CME patterns. Let me give you a quick reminder: the CERC 
stands for Consolidation, followed by a strong Expansion, then a Retracement, and 
finally a Continuation. This often plays out in cleaner trends where the buildup leads to a 
powerful and sustained move.
Let’s look at an example of Platinum, where price tapped into a Weekly Area of Demand. 
Beforehand, we had already seen that the Commercials were positioned at a bullish 
extreme and Retail Traders were heavily short. This suggested a potential reversal was 
near. As price entered the zone, it began to form a range. After the breakout, the CERC 
pattern unfolded clearly, followed by a consolidation, a sharp expansion, a healthy  
retracement, and then a clean continuation of the new trend.
The CME pattern, on the other hand, adds another dimension. It begins with Consoli-
dation, followed by a sudden Manipulation, a final shakeout that triggers stops or lures 
breakout traders, and then comes the Expansion in the opposite direction.  
These patterns occur frequently around macro zones. But then we ask the question, how 
do you know if it is a CERC or a CME pattern, and how do you know when to act when 
both occur? In both cases, we are already biased, and therefore, it doesn’t matter which 
pattern is developed in anticipation of an intraday trend.
Now, let me show you another great example of the CME pattern in action on the Dollar 
Index. After a prolonged downtrend, price entered a macro zone, which in this case was 
a weekly demand zone, and began consolidating for multiple weeks, specifically around 
6 weeks. Nothing seemed to be happening, and just as things looked ready to break 
higher, a sudden sharp move down occurred, a classic manipulation that likely trapped 
breakout sellers and triggered stops. But then came the real move, a powerful expansion 
upward.

for a final shakeout before the actual reversal began.  
 
This is what we call a news failure when the market uses the news event to manipulate 
traders before moving in the opposite direction. Combining manipulation with a news 
failure makes the reversal even more powerful.
But this raises a fair question: are these the only two patterns we can look out for?  
My answer would be absolutely not! There are a few more formations that deserve your 
attention.  
 
Take the classic Head and Shoulders pattern or the W formation. These patterns are 
often dismissed as “retail patterns” by some “expert” traders, but that completely misses 
the point. In isolation, sure, they might not mean much. But when these patterns emerge 
within the broader context, say, during a clear accumulation or  
distribution phase in alignment with the macro fundamentals lining up in the background, 
they suddenly become much more powerful.
Let’s look at an example of Oil, where we see a clear inverted Head and Shoulders  
pattern forming after tapping into a Weekly Demand Zone. This setup was confirmed by 
a strong bullish macro fundamental bias. Once the pattern was completed and broke 
out, it triggered the start of a clean intraday trend, which led to us taking multiple  
successful trades during this bullish run.

T R A D I N G  A R C H E T Y P E S
 C O N T R A R I A N  V S   M O M E N T U M  T R A D E R
Y
ou know how to identify the proper macro supply and demand zones while also  
being aware of the intraday trend; it’s time to understand the different types of  
market conditions. There comes a point in every trader's journey where frustration 
builds. One day, you're trying to short a top, feeling like a genius spotting the reversal 
before anyone else. The next, you're chasing a breakout, convinced this one will run. 
And you end up back at square one. 
It’s not the losses that hurt most; it’s the inconsistency. One strategy today, a different 
one tomorrow, without even realizing you’ve switched. That’s when I had to pause and 
ask myself a simple question: Am I betting against the crowd, or am I trying to ride the 
wave? And more importantly, when do I do which?
I realized that I didn’t need to pick one trading personality forever. I just needed to know 
when to step into which role.
In truth, most traders fall into one of two main archetypes:
• The Contrarian Trader thrives on extremes. They go against the crowd, bet on  
exhaustion, and step in when the market feels stretched to the limit.
• The Momentum Trader thrives on confirmation. They join strength when it’s already 
in motion, building into trends backed by macro data. 
183
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
Contrarian Phase
Supply

===== PAGE 194 =====
Each has its place, and each has its moment. The real edge comes from knowing which 
mindset belongs to which market condition.
The good news is you don’t have to choose one forever. In fact, within the LTA 
framework, we encourage you to do both. You need to know when to switch, and that 
adaptability is what creates consistency.
We don’t believe in locking yourself into one trading style. We believe in alignment. 
Sometimes, that means trading against an exhausted market. At other times, it means 
riding momentum when everything is aligned. What matters is clarity and understanding 
where you are within the market cycle.
The Contrarian Trader
Contrarian traders are wired differently. They’re trained to see what others ignore: the 
signs beneath the surface. They step in when the crowd is leaning one way. When Smart 
Money is quietly unloading, and Retail is still buying.
They hunt for COT extremes and sentiment imbalances, look for under or overvaluation, 
and time their entries at or near macro supply and demand zones. They’re not always 
early. But when they are, they’re often right before the reversal even begins.
Example: Gold is at yearly highs. The sentiment is euphoric. Retails are at peak longs. 
Commercials are heavy on shorts. Price enters a macro supply zone. That’s where the 
contrarian strikes.

The Momentum Trader
Momentum traders don’t need to be first; they care about conviction. They step in after 
the macro structure has shifted, when the breakout is confirmed, and when volume is 
backing it. When Open Interest confirms that, the move is fueled by new participation.
They wait for price to break above a multi-week or macro resistance, use our laddering 
process to follow the Large Speculators to scale into strength, and build their positions 
as the structure confirms the move.
Example: Gold breaks a major resistance level, Open Interest is expanding, and the 
Large Speculators are fueling the trend. Instead of being early, the momentum trader 
rides the wave.

Knowing When to Switch
The question is, what archetype is the market telling you to be? This is where the full LTA 
Framework comes in.
• If sentiment is extreme, smart money is stepping in, and you’ve hit a macro zone with 
undervaluation? That’s your signal to think like a contrarian.
• If price is breaking out, volume is surging, Open Interest is expanding, and the macro 
structure has shifted? That’s your invitation to go along with the momentum.
But here’s what many overlook: momentum usually begins where extremes end. When 
you go from extreme to neutral territory, there is no longer valuable information in the 
COT extremes or sentiment data. That’s where your focus should shift. At that point, 
Large Speculators start to take over, and when you zoom in to start tracking their  
laddering process.
This shift doesn’t just come from positioning data; it also comes from the chart. As we 
discussed in the previous section, if a Demand zone absorbs and removes a major  
Supply zone, we have a macro structural shift. That’s a signal and a confirmation.
Macro technical confirmations help guide you between archetypes. When Supply or 
Demand gets taken out, when the structure flips, that’s your moment. But only when the 
fundamentals are backing it. Otherwise, you will lack context.

Two Archetypes, One Framework
No matter which archetype you currently identify with or which phase the market is in, 
the key is adaptability. That’s the number one principle here.
The LTA Framework was not built for only contrarians or momentum traders.  
It was designed to help traders navigate both. And most importantly, to do so on lower  
timeframes.
Yes, we do apply swing trading techniques (and we’ll dive into that in the next chapter), 
but the core of our execution takes place on the lower timeframes. That’s where  
precision matters and that’s where our key levels and volume analysis come into play.
1. A macro zone is identified with our higher-timeframe supply and demand analysis.
2. An intra-day trend is identified, to understand short-term behavior.
3. Then, we zoom in to the lower timeframes for the actual execution.
Both contrarians and momentum traders can operate within the same structure, they 
follow different rules and have different risk profiles.
While the LTA volume-based system remains consistent in both scenarios, the risk  
profile and trade management need to adjust accordingly. For example, a contrarian 
trader may move their stops to breakeven sooner to protect against sharp pullbacks, 
while a momentum trader may require a more aggressive trading approach, allowing 
their trade to develop.

with key supply and demand zones on the higher timeframe.
The most important thing is clarity. When does the transition occur between one and 
the other? That’s the moment when most traders get confused and abandon their  
process.
You want to master both. It will ensure that, regardless of market conditions, you do not 
have to sit on the sidelines for weeks or months to find what you are looking for. The LTA 
Framework is built for traders who want to trade both and know exactly when to do so.

G I A N T S  A N D  S N I P E R S
U N I F I E D  A P P R O A C H  T O  M A R K E T  S T R U C T U R E
P
icture a hedge fund manager in a glass-walled skyscraper, scanning his  
multi-billion-dollar portfolio, slowly accumulating gold at a Monthly Demand Zone.  
His team has spent weeks planning this move, and each buy order is placed carefully, 
barely noticeable in the daily noise. Over time, this institutional accumulation builds a 
massive Demand Zone, one that could shape gold prices for months.
Now, shift your focus.
A day trader watches a 15-minute Volume Profile chart. The price approaches the  
Previous Day Point of Control (PD POC), the level where the most trading has occurred 
yesterday. The trader knows what’s coming, and a reaction is likely. A well-timed scalp 
trade could yield a quick 20 pips. Unlike the hedge fund manager, he doesn’t care about 
next month, just the next few minutes.
Same market. Different perspective.
This is why big traders move markets on high timeframes while smaller traders react on 
lower ones. But you don’t have to pick one side; you don’t need to be the giant or the 
sniper. In the LTA Framework, you become both. That’s the power of this system.
So why do we use Supply and Demand on higher timeframes and Volume Profile on 
lower ones? And more importantly, why is combining them one of the most unique and 
efficient strategies both for swing and day trading?
190


===== PAGE 201 =====
A Unified Battleground
Supply and Demand zones represent the footprints of institutional activity, moments 
when banks, hedge funds, investment banks, and global institutions move significant 
amounts of capital. Their execution style is deliberate, staggered, and most importantly, 
slow. This is why, within the LTA Framework, we track Supply and Demand so that we 
can tap into these underlying flows.
Take hedge funds, for example. They often run diverse strategies, building positions with 
independent risk models. Investment banks, on the other hand, typically operate as  
stewards of capital, managing vast sums for thousands of clients. Their job isn’t  
necessarily to beat the market but to facilitate exposure and execute according to client 
decisions. This leads to longer-term accumulation, spanning broader timeframes and 
forming strong Supply and Demand zones that don’t disappear overnight.
191
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
T  H  E   E  X  E  C  U  T  I  O  N   F  R  A  M  E  W  O  R  K


===== PAGE 202 =====
Traditional banks also play a significant role. When large commercial banks place orders 
on behalf of corporate clients, such as for currency hedging, commodity purchases, or 
bond allocations, these orders are often executed in chunks over time to avoid disrupting 
the market. This slow, methodical execution contributes further to the creation of signif-
icant Supply and Demand zone imbalances at higher timeframes. Much like investment 
banks, their activity reflects long-term positioning rather than short-term speculation.
Why these zones are powerful:
1. Big Orders Leave Footprints –  When institutions build positions over time, their 
execution alters the price behavior. These areas become historical references where 
liquidity has already proven itself. Price tends to respect them because they  
represent true market conviction.
2. Higher Timeframes Filter Out Noise – While most day traders focus on every tick, 
institutions map the broader landscape. That difference in perspective is exactly why 
these zones carry so much weight.
Now, consider the role of the day trader. For them, the world moves faster, they’re 
not trying to predict a 400-point swing over the next three weeks. They want a tight 
20-40-point move with maximum precision. And for that, Supply and Demand won’t cut 
it. That’s where the Volume Profile comes in.

LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 203 =====
The Volume Profile gives us X-ray vision. Where has the most trading occurred today? 
Where is price sticking, and where is it getting rejected?
Day traders use this in real time. They identify high-volume zones that act as magnets 
where the fight is currently taking place.
This is where the previous chapter becomes incredibly relevant. Whether you’re  
operating as a Contrarian or Momentum trader, your edge will always depend on one 
thing: timing. And this is exactly what the combination of higher-timeframe supply and 
demand, along with lower-timeframe volume, provides. The macro zone sets your  
directional bias, and the real-time volume confirms your entry level.
Imagine the S&P 500 bouncing from a Weekly Demand Zone, a clear sign of long-term 
institutional interest. That gives you the bias, but where do you enter? On the 1H chart 
the price begins to rotate around a high-volume zone. You track the reaction, wait for 
confirmation, then strike.

• Higher Timeframes (8H, 12H, Daily, Weekly, Monthly) → Identify where institutions

You don’t have to wait for days or weeks, and you don’t have to be early either.

Being aware of the higher-timeframe Supply and Demand zones aligns you with the true

H Y B R I D  M O D E L
T H E  B E S T  O F  B O T H  W O R L D S
T
here were moments in my trading career when I didn’t want to be behind the screen 
all day. Sometimes, life gets in the way, such as travel, family, or simply needing 
space from the constant movement of the charts. And in those moments, swing trading 
seemed like the answer. Fewer trades, longer holds, more freedom. But here’s the  
problem I was facing.
I didn’t want to learn two completely different strategies. Because if I did, that would 
mean I’d have to master both. And let’s be honest: mastering one strategy already takes 
immense time, effort, and dedication. Splitting my focus between two separate play-
books? That didn’t feel sustainable or smart over time. So I set out on a different path.
Instead of learning two different strategies, I wanted to develop one framework that could 
serve both styles. A strategy that works if you wish to swing trade or day trade without 
having to rewrite the rules every time you zoom in or out of a chart.
When I started diving deep into macro fundamentals, such as the COT data, seasonal 
cycles, and valuation models, I assumed that this type of data was only useful for swing 
traders and was intended to inform long-term trades on the Daily, Weekly, or Monthly 
timeframes.
But the more I tested and studied, the more I realized these same macro tools can guide 
day trading decisions if you know how to merge the macro with the micro.
My goal was to create a hybrid model, one where I could develop a strong macro  
fundamental bias to then drill down into lower timeframes to execute.
That meant I didn’t have to choose; I could swing trade to have more free time, and I 
could day trade when I wanted to be more active. And the best part? I was always  
operating from the same framework, and that’s what gave me clarity and control.
So what do you prefer? Are you a swing trader? A day trader? Wouldn’t it be powerful to 
focus on one strategy, while still having the flexibility to trade any timeframe?
That’s what the Hybrid Model is all about. It gives you the freedom to trade the way you 
want without ever stepping outside of the structure that keeps you aligned with  
institutional logic and macro conviction.

Bringing it to life
In January 2025, the Japanese Yen reached a moment of divergence between Smart 
Money and Retail traders. The COT data revealed that Commercials had reached a 
strong bullish extreme, while Retail traders were heavily net short, clearly positioned at 
a bearish extreme. This was a textbook case of positioning imbalance, a classic setup 
where sentiment, valuation, and positioning aligned.
Shortly after, the macro technical structure validated what the COT data had hinted at. 
Price broke through a well-established macro supply zone and formed a new demand 
zone just below it. That was our structural shift. It confirmed that the macro direction had 
turned bullish, laying the groundwork for the next leg up.
Here’s the beauty of the Hybrid Model:
Option 1 – I could treat it as a swing trade. I entered from the newly created demand 
zone with the conviction that I was trading in alignment with institutional flows.  
My target? The next structural high or the following macro supply zone. In this specific 
case, that meant holding the position for around eight trading days, allowing the market 
to do the work while I stayed out of the noise.

32
O P T I M A L  R I S K - R E W A R D
C O N S I S T E N C Y  T H R O U G H  S T R U C T U R E D  R:R
I
’ll admit it: I spent my early days obsessing over the perfect entry, convinced that  
finding the right trading strategy by itself will unlock easy profits. But those who’ve been 
in the game long enough find out that the real battle is what happens after the entry.
This chapter dives into the system behind your trades, the risk-reward (R:R) blueprint 
that holds everything together. Because no matter how advanced your strategy, if your 
R:R model is random, you’ll never build reliable results.
The Problem with a "Flexible" R:R Model
For a long time, I used what I’d now call a “flexible” approach to risk-reward. Some 
trades had tight stops and small targets. Others aimed for a massive 5R gain. On paper, 
it gave me freedom. However, in practice, it provided inconsistent data and unreliable 
results. I couldn’t figure out what was working because the playing field kept changing. If 
the rules of engagement are constantly changing, then how will you correctly track your 
data? How can you possibly know what works in the short term, the medium term, or the 
long term if one trade is built on a high R:R and the next on a much smaller one?
How will you know it's the setup, the market conditions, or the risk model itself that  
influenced the outcome? Eventually, I decided to test both extremes and draw  
conclusions from real numbers, not just ideas. I conducted focused trials, first aiming  
for high-reward setups like 4:1 or 5:1, then shifting entirely to a more conservative  
1:1 model.
204
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 215 =====
The Reality of High R:R Trades
When I chased high R:R trades, here’s what happened. At first glance, they seemed like 
the dream. You risk $100 and potentially make $500. And even if you only win 2 out of 
every 10 trades, you’re still in the green.
But in order to hit those 5R trades, I had to keep my stop losses incredibly tight. That 
meant I was constantly getting stopped out on normal market fluctuations, often just 
before price moved in my original direction. I would set these big, ambitious targets only 
to see the market move 3R in my favor and then turn around before reaching my profit 
level. And if I wanted to close my trades within the same day, there was no choice but to 
tighten those stops even more. The other option was to move further down to the micro 
timeframes, making the odds even harder to beat. 
On top of that, you're going to lose a lot more trades. That's just a fact. I was losing a lot 
more trades, which is fine if you're running a high risk-to-reward strategy because  
mathematically it can still be profitable. However, the reality is that you must be able to  
mentally withstand those frequent losses. Over time, it takes a serious toll.  
Most traders lack the resilience to withstand the psychological pressure that comes with 
repeated losses. And when that pressure builds, it increases the chance of  
self-sabotage, stepping outside of your strategy, chasing trades, cutting winners too 
early, or refusing to take the next valid setup.
It became apparent that I was sacrificing too much. I had fewer trade opportunities as 
not many setups qualified under these strict parameters. And to find them, I usually had 
to go down to the 1-minute or 3-minute chart. It’s much harder to trade, and it’s harder to 
trust. My win rate tanked, and so did my confidence. The psychological toll was heavy. 
Taking 7, 8, or even 10 losses in a row just to hit one winner can mess with your head. 
Honestly, now, it doesn’t matter if the numbers make sense in theory. In practice, it’s 
brutal on the mind.
The Other Side – 1:1 R:R Model
So, I went to the other end of the spectrum. I started trading with a 1:1 risk-to-reward 
ratio. Suddenly, my win rate shot up. Trades were easier to find, and stops could be  
wider. I wasn’t getting wicked out nearly as often. There was more breathing room, and 
my account started to stabilize. However, that came with its own set of challenges as 
well. At 1:1, you need to win more than half your trades to make consistent progress. 
The pressure to be right on each trade builds up. And even small losing streaks feel like 
a massive setback because it takes so many wins to make back what you lost. You don’t 
feel like you're building momentum; you're just trying to maintain it.  
And that gets exhausting.
205
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
R  I  S  K   &   M  O  N  E  Y   M  A  N  A  G  E  M  E  N  T

===== PAGE 216 =====
The Sweet Spot: 2:1 Risk-to-Reward
I wanted to find a balance that didn’t go too far on either extreme. The sweet spot. Not 
so tight that every candle wick takes me out, but not so forgiving that I’m still break even 
after weeks of trading. I found the answer in a 2:1 risk-to-reward model.
With a 2:1 structure, I could keep my stops wide enough to withstand the expected  
volatility. My targets were still realistic, often aligning with natural macro levels or swing 
highs and lows. And psychologically, it felt more manageable. I didn’t need to win all the 
time; I just needed to be a bit more selective.
More importantly, I could finally track my performance clearly rather than relying on  
random variables. I knew my R:R, I knew what I was aiming for, and that gave me the 
data I needed to review and improve. 
 
And repeat the cycle, learn, do, reflect, optimize.
Quick Comparison Table
Risk-to-Reward Ratio Profit per $100 Risked Win Rate Needed for Profitability
1:1 $100 51%
2:1 $200 35%
3:1 $300 26%
4:1 $400 21%
5:1 $500 18%
A higher reward lowers the win rate needed, but it also lowers consistency. That’s why 
a 2:1 structure is my favored balance. As you can see here, a 2:1 allows me only to win 
35% of my trades and break even. That gives me the margin to breathe and to improve 
without being under immediate pressure to perform perfectly.
This alone is great for your psychology. The balance allows your mind to float with the 
current rather than swimming against it. The fact is that most of the psychological issues 
traders experience are rooted in a lack of system. For them, the extremes or random-
ness is like putting on a backpack full of bricks before climbing a mountain. When you 
use a 2:1 risk to reward, you remove the bricks.
206
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
R  I  S  K   &   M  O  N  E  Y   M  A  N  A  G  E  M  E  N  T

===== PAGE 217 =====
Why a Fixed R:R Ratio Works
When I stopped adjusting my targets and refined my R:R model, everything became 
sharper. After 10–30 trades, I could already see whether I was hitting the proper setups. 
At 50 trades, I had a reliable read on profitability. At 100 trades, I had clarity on what to 
scale, what to remove, and what to double down on.
I then discovered the hidden power behind risk optimization. Once I collected  
consistent data on my 2:1 model, I had something solid to work with. The numbers didn’t 
lie. They showed me exactly where I stood as a trader. As my win rate improved and  
I became profitable, that’s when I started optimizing my risk to reward ratio. Not by  
blindly chasing higher R:R trades again but gradually adjusting based on my data and 
looking for ways to stretch that 2:1 to 2,5:1 or even 3:1 over time.
And one of the best ways to do that? Closing losers earlier. If I could identify that a trade 
was breaking down before hitting my full stop loss, I could exit early and preserve  
capital. Smaller losses lead to greater returns. That naturally improved my profitability, 
even without changing my targets.
Another side to the coin is knowing when to let your trades run. It takes time and  
experience, but eventually, I learned how to recognize when a setup had momentum, 
when the structure supported it, and when it made sense to leave the trade open and 
push for more. But just like closing early, this too comes later. It’s not something you 
master overnight, especially if you do not have the data to back your “intuition” or  
“feelings.”
Trying to adopt a dynamic risk model right out of the gate is one of the biggest mistakes 
a trader can make. It’s simply not intelligent. At that early stage, you don’t have any 
proof, you don’t have any stability, and you certainly don’t have the data. You don’t even 
know if your strategy is working consistently. Without a structured system to track and 
measure, any variation you add only creates more chaos, both strategically and  
mentally. 
You can’t optimize something you haven’t proven. You can’t refine something you  
haven’t stabilized. Most traders try to start with advanced tools before they’ve mastered 
the basics, and that’s exactly why they fail. The dynamic stuff is earned; it comes  
after you’ve respected the grind. 
In the next chapter, I’ll break down the 2-2-2 Trading Rule, a simple yet powerful  
method designed to keep you in the game and stop you from ever blowing your 
account again. It’s about protecting your capital and building the kind of discipline that 
lasts. If your number one priority is survival and long-term growth, this is where it starts.

33
T H E  2/2/2  T R A D I N G  R U L E
H O W  T O  A V O I D  G O I N G  B R O K E
I 
didn’t come up with the 2-2-2 Rule to sound clever or create some perfect formula.  
I built it out of necessity. After years of trading, blowing up accounts, revenge trading, 
doubling down after a loss, I had to face the truth: I had no system in place to keep me in 
check when emotions ran high.  
 
That’s when I put the 2-2-2 Rule into play. It provided me with the structure I lacked and 
the protection I needed to establish clear boundaries for myself. The rules that would 
hold strong when my mindset was fragile. Rarely do the mistakes happen when you’re 
cool, calm, and calculated. They happen after you get stopped out, when you’re  
frustrated, or when you feel like you have to win it all back in one trade. That’s precisely 
what the 2-2-2 Rule was built to prevent. 
So here’s what it looks like:
1. A 2:1 Risk-to-Reward Ratio – Every single trade must offer at least 2R. Period.
2. Maximum 2% Risk per Trade – No trade ever risks more than 2% of capital.  
In riskier conditions, that drops to 1%.
3. Maximum of 2 Consecutive Trades Unless Secured – If two trades lose  
back-to-back, I step away and reassess.
It sounds simple because it is. However, don’t confuse simplicity with timidness;  
the 2-2-2 rule is powerful because it enforces discipline, structure, and data-backed  
risk management.
The first part of the 2-2-2 Rule is simple: never enter a trade unless your potential reward 
is double the amount you’re risking. This one change alone shifts your focus from being 
“right” to being “selective.” It stops you from entering every setup and instead trains your 
eye for quality. You’re not just looking for price action; you’re looking for asymmetry.  
And the beauty of this? You don’t even need to win half of your trades to be profitable.
209

===== PAGE 220 =====
Imagine you’ve taken ten trades. If you stick to a 2:1 risk-to-reward model, the math 
begins to work in your favor. At just a 20% win rate, meaning you’re wrong 8 out of 10 
times, you’re still only down 4R. That’s not blowing up, and it’s not devastation. It’s a 
small, controlled loss. As a matter of fact, that’s your absolute worst-case scenario at a 
20% win rate for every 10 trades you take. You're losing money, but that's not the point. 
It's the way you’re losing money in a controlled fashion. And with slight tweaks, you can 
bump it up to 30 or 40%, and you’re already breaking even or turning a consistent profit.
Let's calculate what would happen if you gain that one extra win, three in total.  
That gives you +6R on the wins and -7R on the losses, putting you at -1R overall.  
This means you’re already near breakeven with a 30% win rate, yet you lost more than 
double the amount of trades you won.
At four wins, you’ve made +8R, lost -6R, and are sitting at +2R profit. With five wins, 
you’re up +5R net. At six out of each 10 wins, you’ve gained a total of +8R.  As the win 
rate increases, the compounding begins to accelerate.  
 
Take a moment and study this table:
Win Rate Wins Losses T otal Gain 
(2R per Win)
T otal Loss  
(-1R per Loss)
Net Outcome 
(R)
Net Outcome 
(%)
20% 2 8 +4R -8R -4R -8%
30% 3 7 +6R -7R -1R -2%
40% 4 6 +8R -6R +2R +4%
50% 5 5 +10R -5R +5R +10%
60% 6 4 +12R -4R +8R +16%
Do you see what it shows? Once you follow this exact model, you start to collect reliable 
data. Trade after trade, your win rate begins to give you real intel. You see what works, 
and you identify where you're strong. Most importantly, you know what to fix because the 
numbers don’t lie.
Once you have 10, 20, 30, or 50 trades logged using this exact structure, you now have 
a war map for improvement. You’re tightening your execution, and every iteration takes 
you one step closer to profitability. Do you see how powerful that way of thinking is?

R  I  S  K   &   M  O  N  E  Y   M  A  N  A  G  E  M  E  N  T


===== PAGE 221 =====
When to Dial Back Risk to 1%
Now, let’s talk about nuance. Not every trade deserves full exposure. Some setups carry 
more uncertainty than others, and that’s when I scale down. If I’m counter-trend trading 
as a contrarian, or price has just tapped a macro zone but hasn’t confirmed direction yet, 
I drop my risk from 2% to 1%.
These are some of the key scenarios:
• I’m going against the macro or intraday trend.
•     The broader structure isn’t on my side. That means I’m fighting momentum. 
When that’s the case, even a good setup becomes more fragile. The 1% risk helps 
limit the cost, if I’m wrong.
• Price is pulling back into a major zone without clear confirmation.
•     When price taps into a key level, like macro supply or demand, but I don’t yet 
have confirmation of trend, that’s a moment of uncertainty. It might hold, it might 
not. Until price shows its hand, I reduce exposure.
• I’m building a profit buffer during a prop firm challenge.
•     In a challenge or funded account, my focus isn’t just on winning; it’s on not 
losing, and I want to emphasize this point. In the early stages of building up profit, 
I trade small, stay disciplined, and protect the capital first. Once I reach a certain 
level of comfort, I may increase the risk again. But not before that.
Scaling down doesn’t signal fear; it signals control. It means I understand the contextual 
weight. My edge isn’t fully formed yet, or the trade might take more time to develop. By 
cutting risk in half, I give myself breathing room.  
 
Allowing for uncertainty without putting my capital at unnecessary risk. This is how pros 
operate. They do not go full throttle on every trade. They adjust. They know when to 
press and when to ease off.
If you’re still building your system and still collecting data, you should begin with the 
lowest risk possible. Again, this isn’t because you are scared; you’re playing it smart. 
Running into the front lines of war blindly gets you shot. You haven’t earned the right to 
increase risk until you’ve proven your consistency.
211
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
R  I  S  K   &   M  O  N  E  Y   M  A  N  A  G  E  M  E  N  T
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 222 =====
The Two-Strike Rule
Now, let’s talk about the Two-Strike Rule. This a simple rule I follow that has saved me 
from tilt more times than I can count. If I lose two trades in a row, I stop trading for the 
day. There’s no third trade. No chasing. No redemption setup. I shut it all down, step 
away, and come back the next day with a clear head, even if I feel confident. Even if I 
think the next setup looks great. I respect the line. Because every time I broke it in the 
past, things had spiraled out of control quickly.
If I’m already up, if I’ve secured some solid wins, then I’ll allow myself to continue. But 
only within the limits I’d defined before the session started. Based on what I committed 
to when I was thinking clearly.
Here’s something most traders overlook: your two trades can’t be correlated.
If I take a long on EUR/USD and then a long on GBP/USD, and the dollar suddenly 
strengthens, both can lose together. That’s not two trades; it’s one dollar play split across 
two charts. I’ve had to learn this the hard way—correlation matters.
If trade number one loses and it is a dollar-based position, I don’t immediately follow it 
up with another dollar-based bet. I pause. I assess. And if I still feel there’s an edge, I’ll 
shift the asset or look for non-correlated setups.
Why does all this matter? Because when you’re trying to build a system that lasts, you 
have to respect the boundaries that keep you from compounding mistakes. You’re  
managing yourself. Stick to the Two-Strike Rule. It’ll save you more money and more 
mental capital than you realize.
I always tell traders that your first thirty trades are the most important trades of your  
career. If you can execute those thirty with discipline and adherence to your rules, 
then—and only then—should you consider increasing exposure. That’s when you’ve 
gathered a bit of data, and you know where you stand.
If you can survive those first thirty, you’re ready. Being disciplined for thirty trades in a 
row is one of the hardest things to do in trading. But if you can do that, if you can keep 
your emotions in check, your risk controlled, and your strategy intact for thirty straight 
setups, it changes the game instantly.
That’s what we’ll talk about next. In the next chapter, we’ll cover Managing Drawdowns 
and Emotional Discipline, and I’ll show you how to build the mindset and habits that let 
you stay consistent no matter what the market throws your way.

34
D I S C I P L I N E  O V E R  E M O T I O N 
B U I L D  T R U S T  I N  Y O U R  P R O C E S S
G
athering data is more important than individual trade outcomes. I used to measure 
my success by the result of individual trades. If I won a trade, I felt like a genius. If I 
lost, I questioned everything. My strategy, my execution, and even whether I should be 
trading at all. The problem? I was focusing on the wrong metrics.
That changed the day I realized consistency beats outcome. I wasn’t supposed to 
chase profits. I was supposed to collect data. It sounds logical right? My real job was to 
build a process I could trust. A process that could be refined, repeated, and scaled.
I began seeing trading as a series of experiments. Every trade became a data point, 
not a victory or defeat. My emotional highs and lows started to flatten. And over time, 
I wasn’t reacting to wins or losses. And believe me, the fixed risk-to-reward ratio also 
helped tremendously with optimizing this process.
Losing Doesn’t Mean You’re Wrong
Of course, I lose trades; everyone does. The difference is that I no longer attach  
emotions to them. I don’t see a loss as a failure. I ask myself, Did I follow my plan?  
If I followed the plan, it was a win, even if the trade didn’t work out. That mindset allowed 
me to see the bigger picture. Viewing losses as part of the process is not something to 
fear or avoid; it is a natural part of the process.
Your system can’t evolve if you’re not collecting the right data. And you can’t collect the 
right data if your trading is emotional and random. Every tweak needs to come from 
analysis, not frustration. When I committed to this approach, I started building long-term 
profitability. I leaned heavily on my framework, the 2/2/2 Rule, my 2:1 R:R structure, 
and my complete LTA Framework. The more I trusted that process, the more stable I 
became.
Traders love to obsess over strategy. But strategy without risk management is like  
driving a Ferrari without brakes. Without them, you’ll crash. I’ve seen traders with brilliant 
strategies get wiped out simply because they didn’t have any rules around risk.  
Meanwhile, average traders with solid risk control survive, grow, and thrive.
I never spiral into overtrading when I follow my 2/2/2 Rule. I set a fixed risk per trade so 
that no single trade can take me out of the game. And I stick with my 2:1 risk-to-reward 
ratio to ensure that my potential profits always outweigh my losses. 
214
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307


===== PAGE 225 =====
Turning 30 Trades Into a Breakthrough
You’ll probably recognize this. And trust me, this is extremely common. I used to fixate 
on the outcome of every trade and let that influence my decision-making instantly.  
However, when I have taken 30 trades, the questions I ask are different. I don’t ask, 
"How many did I win?" I ask:
Did I stick to my plan on every single trade? 
Did I only enter when my macro-level conditions were in alignment? 
Did I maintain consistent position sizing and stay within my risk limits?  
And most importantly, did I pause and reassess after two losing trades?
Those questions led to better answers. I found that if I was winning fewer than 33% of 
my trades, it pointed to a flaw in either my system or execution. However, if I reached 
40% or more, the strategy was working; I just needed to stay consistent. When I reached 
a 50% win rate or higher, it meant the edge was solid, and my focus needed to shift to 
maintaining discipline and scaling.
Surviving the Drawdown That Nearly Broke Me
I’ll never forget the time I hit a 10-trade losing streak. Before, I would have panicked 
and tried to force wins, abandoning my system. But this time, I did the opposite.
I leaned on the data. I reminded myself that my edge wasn’t gone, it was just in a rough 
patch. So I followed the 2/2/2 Rule. I kept my risk fixed and consistent. I maintained my 
2:1 model. I reviewed each trade with zero emotional bias, ensuring that every position I 
took was aligned with the macro-level structure.
By the time I completed my next 30 trades, I had recovered to a 43% win rate, and I was 
once again profitable. If I had given up mid-drawdown, none of that would’ve happened. 
That experience proved to me that discipline is the key to survival.
The Trap of Early Profit-Taking
We’ve all done it. The moment we see green on the screen, our emotions flare up.  
The urge to “take the money” kicks in hard. However, every time I closed a trade early at 
1:1, rather than letting it run to 2:1, I sabotaged my system. I was damaging the  
consistency of my entire model. And in most of those cases, the trade would’ve hit the 
target if I had just trusted the plan. That’s the danger of letting emotion override data.  
Because when you break your own rules, you can’t trust your numbers anymore.  
A winning trade against your rules isn’t a good trade. The human mind isn’t wired to see 
it that way, and it’s why we have to think counterintuitively to succeed in this game.

The Emotional Discipline Checklist
Anytime emotions try to take over, I slow myself down and run through a few mental 
checkpoints. I ask myself, am I trading out of fear or from a place of structure? Did I 
allow the macro levels to guide me, or did I rush my entry? Did this trade meet all my 
criteria, or did I bend the rules? Did I stay true to my 2/2/2 risk structure? And if I lose 
this trade, would I still take the exact same setup tomorrow? If I can answer yes to all of 
those, then the trade was a success, regardless of the outcome.
Discipline Wins in the End
The longer I trade, the more obvious this truth becomes: success is about staying  
consistent in how you execute your strategy. That’s what separates professionals from 
amateurs. Every trade you take, every entry and exit that respects the system, adds to 
your data. That data becomes proof. And over time, that proof builds confidence. So 
when the losses come, you don’t panic. You stay grounded and consistent, refining your 
edge like a samurai sharpens their sword.
