35
P R E C I S I O N  T R A D E  C O N T R O L
S T R A T E G Y  B U I L T  O N  D A T A
H
aving a good strategy is essential; how you manage each trade matters more.  
That’s where money is made or lost. Even when I followed my rules, price would still  
sometimes reverse and hit my stop loss, then go exactly where I expected. Other times, 
I took profit too soon and missed out on bigger gains. I had to change something.
The answer? You guessed it, Data. I had to stop thinking about each trade individually, 
and look at the bigger picture. What has worked over hundreds of trades? That’s how I 
started improving, by tracking what worked over time and learning from it. In this chapter, 
I’ll walk you through how I manage trades depending on the market, the trend, and how 
a trade is behaving. 
218


===== PAGE 229 =====
1. Moving Stop Loss to Breakeven at 1R
Let’s start with protection. As a contrarian trader, I always look to protect my capital early. 
That’s why I typically move my stop loss to breakeven once a trade reaches 1R. But I 
only do this when the macro trend has not yet shifted. In other words, when I’m going 
against the dominant trend and price is still in transition, I want to lock in the risk as soon 
as possible.
However, when the macro trend has already shifted, and I’m trading in line with momen-
tum, I give the trade more room to breathe. I let it play out fully, even if that means sitting 
through a pullback. That’s the difference between surviving as a contrarian and thriving 
as a momentum trader. This naturally brings us to the next layer of management: how 
and when to add to trades that are working.
2. Scaling In – Only for Swing Trades
Not every trade deserves more size. In fast-paced, lower timeframe environments like 
day trading, scaling in adds stress and risk. But in swing trading, when the picture is 
clearer, and the timeframes are larger, there’s room to build into a position.
If I plan to hold a trade longer, and the macro conditions are in place, I may start with a 
smaller entry. As the trade confirms itself, maybe with a strong retest or a new  
momentum push, I’ll scale in. This allows me to achieve a better average entry, reduce 
early risk, and add only when things are going in my favor.
When done right, it helps me stay patient and build positions gradually, especially in 
trending markets where the structure supports it. When it comes to structure, not all set-
ups have the same confidence behind them. Here’s how I adjust my risk depending on 
the market.
3. Changing Risk Depending on the Market
Your strategy may remain the same, but your risk should adapt to the changing condi-
tions. I treat contrarian trades and momentum trades differently, not just in execution but 
in position sizing. When I’m trading against the trend, I keep my risk lower. That’s usually 
1% per trade. I know there’s more uncertainty, so I protect myself accordingly.
When I’m aligned with the macro narrative and price is moving with clear strength, I’m 
comfortable increasing my risk to 2%. That’s when I want to press because those are the 
trades with momentum and backing. This risk flexibility helps me keep losses small when 
conditions are tough and capitalize more when they’re in my favor. Now, let’s move on to 
something that’s often overlooked but just as crucial: timing.
219
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S

4. Managing Trades Based on Time and Sessions
Not all hours are equal in trading. The timing of your trade can completely change how 
it plays out. Let’s say I took a trade during the London session, if we’re heading into the 
New York Open and price is still hovering around my entry, I often cut the trade. That’s 
when volatility spikes, manipulation increases, and things get unpredictable. Unless I’m 
already in profit, I prefer to be flat before the open.
Later in the day, during the final stretch of New York, if a trade hasn’t gone anywhere, 
I’ll usually close it. Volume starts drying up, price gets sluggish, and holding the position 
adds unnecessary exposure. If there’s no volume, there’s no reason to stay. However, if 
the market remains active and my trade is running smoothly, I let it play out. It’s all about  
reading the rhythm of the sessions.
Let the Data Guide You
Everything I’ve just shared, how to protect capital, when to scale in, how to adjust risk, 
and when to exit based on time, it all only works if you have a consistent system. The 
biggest mistake I used to make was tweaking too early. I’d try something for five trades, 
change it, then change it again. 
The lesson? Don’t change anything until you’ve got data. Wait for 100 trades.  
Only then will you see what’s truly working. Use your trades as your teacher. Track 
everything and see what needs fixing. That’s how you grow and how trade management 
turns into a real edge.

C O N Q U E R  T R A D I N G  B I A S E S
O V E R C O M I N G  M E N T A L  R O A D B L O C K S
L
et’s be honest. These days, everyone’s quick to say, “It’s my psychology,” when they 
blow a trade, freeze up, or revenge trade their way into a drawdown. You’ll recognize 
yourself in some of the biases we’re going to cover. But here’s the truth most traders 
don’t want to hear: you can’t blame your mind if you never built a real system to begin 
with.
Psychology matters when all the underlying layers are already in place. That means 
having a working strategy, a proven risk model, consistent execution, and precise data 
tracking. If you’re missing any of those? You don’t have a psychology problem; you have 
a skill gap.
A baby doesn’t learn to walk without first falling many times. You’re going to make  
mistakes, you’re going to feel every emotion in the book, but it’s through those falls that 
you learn how to stand. And only once you have something solid under your feet can you 
begin working on the one thing that separates average traders from the elite.
With that in mind, let me show you the biases that sneak up on every trader, no matter 
how experienced. The ones that quietly kill your edge and sabotage your progress.
My Brain Became My Worst Trading Enemy
This might sound backward, but even after I had a solid strategy in place, backtested, 
refined, and proven with data, I still wasn’t making consistent profits. And that’s when I 
learned the problem wasn’t my system. The problem was me.
Every time I stuck to the process, I made money. It was that simple. However, something 
strange would happen the moment I got close to a target or a milestone, such as pass-
ing a challenge, reaching a profit goal, or hitting a streak. I would start forcing trades. I 
began seeing setups that weren’t really there. I would stretch the rules, take trades too 
early, or chase price long after the setup was gone.
Boredom was another killer. For some weeks, I didn’t get a single A+ setup.  
Occasionally, two weeks could pass without a trade meeting my criteria. And even 
though I knew A+ setups were the ones that made me money in the long term, I couldn’t 
deal with sitting on the sidelines. So, I would justify taking dumb trades and convincing 
myself that a “B-minus” setup would do. It was my need for a cheap dopamine rush, the 
urge to feel the adrenaline. To feel in control.
It was those moments, right at the edge of progress, where my mind became my biggest 
obstacle. I know you have felt the same; this happens to nearly every trader who starts 
to take the game seriously. Let me expose these biases, call them out, and put a face to 
the name. Because once you can identify them, you can stop them dead in their tracks.
222
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

===== PAGE 233 =====
Let’s Break Down These Biases One by One
Confirmation Bias
I thought I was being “too detailed” by finding reasons to stay in a trade. In reality, I was 
ignoring everything that didn’t support my bias. It wasn’t about being rational or being 
disciplined. I unconsciously wanted to be right. This led to holding onto trades way past 
invalidation, refusing to cut losses, and convincing myself I was still in control.
Overconfidence Bias
After a few winning trades, I felt untouchable. I started increasing lot sizes, taking setups 
I normally wouldn’t touch. Taking setups that were under the “standard” and throwing 
caution out the window. That high didn’t last. Because when losses hit, they hit hard. I 
learned that overconfidence can lead you to ignore risk and skip the checklist. And when 
you do that, you lose your edge.
Anchoring Bias
There were moments when I focused so intently on the entry price that I forgot why I 
had entered the trade in the first place. Price would move against me, but I’d say, “It just 
needs to get back to breakeven.” That mindset kept me in dead trades far longer than I 
should have stayed, costing me more capital than necessary. The market doesn’t care 
about your entry or your emotional attachment. 
Loss Aversion
This one is painful. I’ve had trades where I held a losing position for days to avoid taking 
the hit. Deep down, it felt like if I didn’t close the trade, the loss wasn’t real. But avoiding 
the loss didn’t fix anything; it only amplified it. Meanwhile, I’d cut my winners at 1R  
because I was scared of losing what I had. Human beings are wired to avoid pain twice 
as much as move towards pleasure. This is your ancient survival brain at work.
What Helped Me Break Free
I began to approach trading as a professional career. Like a business. Like a process.
• I built a rules-based trading plan. Entries, exits, risk, everything was predefined.
• I journaled every single trade. Not just the win or loss but what I felt during the trade, 
what made me enter, and what made me exit. 
• I stopped chasing perfection. One trade doesn’t define me, but a hundred trades 
executed in-line with my strategy show me who I am.
• I actively challenged my thinking. Every time I felt emotional, I paused and asked: 
Is this logic or ego?
So ask yourself: Is your system solid, or is your psychology leaking all your edge?
If you want to last in this game, then mastering your mind isn’t just a suggestion. It’s the 
only way forward. In the next chapter, we’ll break down exactly how we do that by build-
ing emotional discipline and resilience, especially when the markets turn against you.

37
R I S K  I L L U S I O N
T H E  P S Y C H O L O G Y  O F  P O S I T I O N  S I Z I N G
H
ere’s a lesson I learned the hard way. I thought risking 2% was a conservative  
approach. But when that 2% turned into red numbers on my screen? It didn’t feel  
conservative; it felt personal. That’s the difference between theory and practice.  
On paper, it’s just math. In real life, it’s emotion. And those emotions mess with your  
decision-making more than you think.
It’s crazy how quickly your brain can overreact to a string of small losses. Take three 
losses in a row at 2%, and suddenly, you’re down 6%. You know it’s only 6%, but it  
feels like everything. That’s where the psychological weight of position sizing comes in. 
It’s about your relationship with risk.
Most traders struggle with risk because they can’t do the math; that part is easy.  
However, the brain reacts to risk as if it were a threat. And in those moments, logic goes 
out the window and is replaced by fear, doubt, and the urge to get it back.
Emotionally Reacting vs. What I Do Now
Whenever I had a few losers in a row, I used to increase my lot size, thinking I could 
make it back faster. Almost every time I did that, it got worse. I would either blow up a 
week’s worth of gains or spiral into a drawdown that would take me a month to recover 
from. My brain told me, you’re smart enough to fix this. But the smarter move was to 
slow down, not press harder.
When you lose a trade, your body produces cortisol, the stress hormone. A 2011 study 
published in the journal Scientific Reports tracked hormonal reactions in traders and 
found that cortisol levels can rise by as much as 68% during volatile market conditions. 
Another study from Cambridge University showed that elevated cortisol doesn’t just 
make you feel anxious; it changes the way you process risk. It makes you more likely to 
avoid pain, take impulsive actions, and overcorrect.
So when you lose a trade and instantly feel the need to "get it back," it’s not just your 
ego talking; it’s your biology trying to escape discomfort. That’s why so many traders  
revenge trade or double down right after a loss. They’re not trading a setup; they’re  
trying to solve a chemical imbalance.
Knowing this is key. Because it means you’re not weak, you’re human. But being human 
in the market without a system is dangerous. And that’s why rules exist: to protect you 
from your most natural impulses.
Now? I have a rule. If I lose two trades in a row, I either stop for the day or reduce my 
risk. I step back, I analyze rationally, and I don’t let my brain drive the trades. My trading 
system is the pilot, and my emotions can take a seat in the back of the plane.
225
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

===== PAGE 236 =====
The Pain of Winning Too Early
Let's flip this on its head. Winning trades can be just as dangerous. Have a few wins in a 
row, and suddenly, you’re the king of the market. You start increasing your position size, 
not based on data, but confidence. And when the loss eventually comes, because it will 
come, it hits harder than it should.
That false sense of momentum makes you take bigger risks right when you should be 
playing defense. The market has no idea you’re on a winning streak, and it doesn’t care. 
That's exactly why your position sizing needs to stay consistent until the data proves 
otherwise.
And here’s where the biology kicks in again. That same Cambridge study that revealed 
cortisol spikes after losses also found that testosterone levels rise after winning trades. 
This creates the classic “winner effect.” Traders become overconfident, take on more 
risk, and assume the next trade will also work in their favor. It feels like momentum, but 
it’s a chemical high. And if you’re not aware of it, it leads straight to emotional  
overtrading.
Understanding that both losing and winning come with emotional and hormonal  
reactions is the first step to staying grounded. Wins aren’t just wins; they’re also triggers. 
And unless you stay conscious of how you respond, they can be just as dangerous as 
losses.
The 3 Layers of Position Sizing
1. Baseline Risk
This is my default risk per trade. Usually between 0.5% and 2%, depending on the  
account type and goal. I never go above this unless I have a data-driven reason to build 
larger positions, primarily for swing trades.
2. Situational Risk
If I’m trading a contrarian setup or the macro trend hasn’t been confirmed yet, I cut my 
risk in half. If everything aligns, momentum, macro, intraday, volume, and I’m confident, 
I stick with my full risk, and in some cases, I’ll find ways to scale into multiple positions 
over time.
3. Performance Risk
If I’m on a losing streak, I keep my risk neutral until I stabilize; I’ll never increase my risk 
in a losing streak. Even if I’ve executed well and have 300+ trades to prove my system 
works, I understand that confidence doesn’t erase the randomness of drawdowns. A 
good system can still hit a rough patch. Therefore, I won’t scale up based solely on past 
performance. I’ll wait for the current stability to return. Consistency is the signal. Not just 
data.
226
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

You Need to Earn the Right to Risk More
This is the truth most people overlook. You can’t just decide to trade at 2%; you have to 
earn that right. You earn it by following your rules for 30+ trades. No revenge trades, no 
skipped entries, no hesitations.
If you can do that, if you can stay disciplined through those first 30 trades, then you’re 
on the right track. And you might think 30 trades is easy work. Well, let’s see about that. 
Because within those 30 trades, a lot can happen.
You might hit three losers right off the bat and start questioning your entire system. You 
might go on a five-win streak and get tempted to over-leverage “just once.” You might be 
in a slow market where boredom kicks in, and you want to force a trade. You’ll see other 
people winning on social media and feel like you’re missing out. You’ll doubt yourself. 
You’ll want to skip a setup due to fear or jump in early because of greed. In 30 trades, 
your entire psychological playbook gets exposed.
However, if you can handle it, that’s when you can safely start increasing the position 
size, as you now have data to back it up. Now you’re in the rational driver's seat.
But if you can’t do 30 trades in a row without following the plan? You have no business 
increasing risk. You’re not ready yet. And that’s okay because every trade you take the 
right way is one step closer to becoming the trader you want to be.

38
C O N T R O L  I L L U S I O N
L E A R N I N G  W H E N  T O  W A I T
I
t’s easy to believe we have more control than we really do, and sometimes, that  
confidence can turn into something dangerous—a belief that we can control the  
outcome. This phenomenon is known as the illusion of control. It can lead to bad habits, 
such as holding losing trades, overtrading, and the mistaken belief that we always need 
to act. To evolve as a trader, we need to learn to recognize this bias and know when to 
act and when to wait.
The Illusion of Control
We think we can control random things. A study by psychologist Ellen Langer in 1975 
showed that people often believe they have control, even when they don’t. In trading, 
this occurs when we think we’ve “figured out the market,” even though the market is 
inherently uncertain.
This bias is common in trading because it often feels like something that can be fully 
mastered with enough knowledge. You make a few good calls and suddenly feel in  
control. However, the truth is that markets are unpredictable. And when you feel too  
confident, you take trades you shouldn’t.
The Problem Starts in School
Part of this mindset starts early. Think about school; from a young age, we’re taught 
that there are correct and wrong answers. You either pass or fail, and uncertainty is only 
going to hurt you in the final exam. Making mistakes is seen as a failure, and this kind of 
black-and-white thinking teaches us that being wrong is something to be ashamed of.
But trading doesn’t work like school. Trading is about probabilities, not certainties. You 
can follow every rule perfectly and still take a loss. You can do everything right and still 
get it wrong.  It’s part of the game. The market doesn’t reward the most academic person 
in the room. It rewards the one who knows how to stay in the game, make smart  
choices, and manage risk.
This is why many highly academic people struggle in trading. They’re trained to rely on 
facts and straightforward answers, no matter the circumstances. They feel the need to 
be right and take losses personally. But trading is messy and unpredictable. It requires 
thinking in terms of probabilities and managing emotions, things that aren’t taught in  
textbooks. A study published in the Journal of Behavioral Finance found that individu-
als with more formal education tended to perform worse in trading environments that 
required flexibility and intuition. They were more likely to overthink, overtrade, or try to 
force control when none existed.
229
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

===== PAGE 240 =====
In another study, titled Trading on Illusions, researchers found that traders who  
believed they had more control performed worse than those who understood their limits. 
The study found that individuals who were overconfident traded significantly more and 
ultimately earned less money. So, believing you have control can lead to more trades, 
more stress, and worse results.
The Problem with Overtrading
This false sense of control often emerges through overtrading. This is where the illusion 
of control becomes dangerous. Overtrading happens when we take trades we shouldn’t, 
simply because we feel the need to “do something.” The market’s moving, so we believe 
we need to be part of it. We see a tiny pattern or some candle formation and convince 
ourselves that it’s worth acting on, even if it doesn’t meet our plan. And because we’ve 
been trained in school to always have answers, we fall back into that pattern of doing 
instead of waiting.
We tell ourselves we’re “working hard” or “being productive” by staying active in the 
market. But more trades don’t mean better trades, or better results. In fact, more trades 
often mean lower quality setups, more emotional decisions, and less discipline.
This behavior creates a vicious cycle, and each trade feeds a short-term feeling of  
control. But the more you act without structure, the more likely you are to lose. And the 
more you lose, the more you feel the need to get it back, leading to even more poor 
trades. Eventually, you won’t be trading your system anymore. You’re just reacting  
emotionally. This is why overtrading is one of the most dangerous symptoms of the  
illusion of control. It feels like controlled action, but it’s the exact opposite.
Acquire Smarter Trading Habits
To break free from the illusion of control and the trap of overtrading, you need to build 
smarter habits into your daily routine. It starts by creating clear rules that define when 
you will enter, where you will exit, how much you will risk, and when to step away. These 
rules form the framework that guides you when your emotions try to take over.
Keeping a trade journal is another powerful tool. When you write down the reason  
behind every trade, how it played out, and how you felt during the trade, you can see 
the reflection of your behavior. Over time, it becomes easier to spot your destructive 
patterns. Strong risk management is your safety net. Use a stop loss, and keep the risk 
per trade small and consistent. This helps you avoid the kind of emotional damage that 
leads to more bad decisions.
And finally, patience. It may sound simple, but waiting is one of the most challenging yet 
crucial skills in trading. Waiting for the setup and waiting for the market to come to you. 
It’s the opposite of the illusion of control, self-control, and self-discipline.
230

LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

My Final Thoughts
The market doesn’t care how smart or skilled you are; I’ve learned that the hard way.  
You can’t control what will happen next, but you can control how you respond. That’s 
where real power comes from. Don’t let your brain trick you into overtrading or acting 
without a clear reason. The best traders are those who know when not to trade, as they 
recognize when the odds are stacked against them. It's funny how that works, right?
Forget about being right; it’s about staying in the game long enough to let your edge play 
out. So the next time you feel like you have to take a trade, ask yourself: "Is this part of 
the plan, or just my mind trying to feel in control?"
In the next chapter, we’ll delve deeper into how top-performing traders think, as the  
primary difference between retail and institutional traders lies in their mindset.  
We’ll explore what it really means to operate like a professional and how to develop the 
kind of mental edge that puts you in the same league as the best.

M I N D S E T  S H I F T
T H E  C O N S I S T E N T  T R A D E R ' S  S E C R E T
S
croll through YouTube, and you’ll see it: titles like “100% win rate,” “I called the top,” 
or “Never lose again with this secret strategy.” These so-called gurus present trading 
like they always know exactly what’s going to happen. Rarely do they talk about their 
losses, and when they do, it’s with a smirk and a spin, like it wasn’t a loss at all. That 
kind of messaging seeps into your mind without you even realizing. It makes you believe 
that you should always be right, too, as if it’s a sin to lose a trade.  
That delusion is dangerous. 
Again, we’re taught in school to get the correct answer. We reward those who guess 
correctly, not those who adapt creatively. The market ruthlessly exploits that mindset, 
because each trade is not a math test. No one cares how hard you studied; the market 
moves how it wants regardless of how you feel. Your job isn’t to predict, it’s to anticipate 
and respond. That shift in mindset is what separates consistent traders from emotional 
ones. 
The Trap of Trying to Be Right
Most traders don’t know they’re predicting until it’s too late. They write out a scenario, 
but what they’re really doing is telling the market what has to happen. The zone must 
hold. The pattern must work. If it doesn’t, confusion sets in. Emotion kicks in.  
The structure goes out the window.
The danger isn’t just the loss. It’s what happens next. Revenge trades. Rule-breaking. 
Questioning your entire system after one trade. This occurs because the brain craves 
certainty, but the market only offers probability.
When traders feel that need to control or force an outcome, it’s usually ego disguised 
as confidence. But confidence should come from preparation, not from trying to prove 
you’re right.
That’s why scenario-based thinking is so powerful. It creates distance between you and 
your bias. You say, “If this, then that.” You have an entry condition, a confirmation, and 
an invalidation. You follow the playbook. If the market shifts, you shift, too.
The LTA Strategy Is Built for Anticipating
I didn’t create systems to guess what happens next, even though you might think that 
when we refer back to our “Fundamental Bias”. I built tools to interpret what’s happening 
now and prepare to act ONLY if our rules are met.
Clear context followed by Macro Structure. However, we don’t assume that price must 
reverse when it reaches our zone. Instead, we map out a few potential reactions and 
assign clear behaviors to each one. This is what I call scenario mapping.
233
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

===== PAGE 244 =====
Let’s say price is approaching a weekly supply zone.
• If price rejects from the zone sharply, we mark that as our confirmation. This is when 
I’ll switch to the lower timeframe and look for an intra-day trend to develop or price  
trading below any of our important key levels.
• If price slowly grinds into the zone, holds, but doesn’t reject strongly, I wait, and  
maybe we get a consolidation structure that gives more clarity. In this case, I don’t 
take action.
• If price breaks through the zone entirely and structure shifts, I scratch the plan  
altogether. The market isn’t listening, and I don’t force trades that aren’t there.  
I simply mark out the next macro zone while I wait for a new data release that will tell 
me if my macro fundamental bias is still valid.
Scenario mapping allows me to define the action plan before price even gets there. 
I’m preparing for the battle. And this applies everywhere, from lower-timeframe intraday 
trades to larger macro plays.
Real-World Example: EUR/USD Short
Let’s break down a real trade example using this exact approach of anticipation rather 
than prediction. It will all make sense by the end of this walkthrough. We start at the 
macro timeframe. We got an alert from our sentiment data. Commercial traders hit a  
26-week bearish extreme, while Retail traders showed a 26-week bullish extreme.  
According to our 10-year backtest, this setup had a 75% success rate. While it confirmed 
a potential macro short bias, it wasn’t enough to act on its own.

trade setup, as the macro had now aligned with the technicals.

I told my VIP members, “If price breaks below the POC, we can look for shorts, and if it 
doesn’t, we stay out.” Simple as that. Our bias might be bearish, but the market needs to 
prove it.

This is why I firmly believe in structured pre-market and mid-week scenario mapping. 
Twice a week, I map out what could happen and how I’ll respond rather than what I  
believe will happen. That difference is everything. With a clear plan, you stay in control, 
even when the market moves differently than you expected.

Scenario Mapping in Action
One of the most underrated advantages of scenario-based trading is how it naturally  
reduces the need for constant monitoring and over-analysis. Over the years, I learned 
that I don’t need to be glued to the screen all day, every day. I usually conduct my  
detailed analysis only twice a week. Once during the Sunday or Monday pre-market 
planning session and again midweek to reassess and recalibrate.
Why twice a week? Because it gives me the space to think clearly and protects me from 
reacting to every small market move. If I overanalyze, I begin to see things that aren’t 
there. That’s when the temptation to force trades creeps in.
Instead, I map out a few potential scenarios in advance. I do this during calm moments 
when the market isn’t pressuring me into a decision. Then, when those scenarios begin 
to unfold throughout the week, I can trade them with high confidence—simply following a 
plan I laid out with a clear head.
The result? The accuracy of my trades improved, and my stress levels decreased.  
I had more flexibility to observe multiple markets at once. Scenario mapping laid the  
foundation for stress-free multi-asset trading. If I wanted to, I could monitor multiple  
currencies, commodities, or indices without feeling like I was being stretched thin or 
missing out. I wasn’t glued to ten charts all at once because I already knew what I  
wanted to see on each of them.
Trading became more straightforward, more focused, and much more enjoyable.  
I no longer felt the constant pressure to be in the market every day. I had already done 
the work in advance because 80% of the heavy lifting came from just 20% of the effort.
Strategic Thinking
Think about how much easier trading becomes when you're not trying to predict every 
single move. No longer glued to the screen, or chasing candles. You're thinking ahead 
with a clear and calm mind. This mindset shift holds everything together. Now let me ask 
you, can you see how much more powerful your trading can be if you take this  
seriously? It’s going to change the game for you.
Now that you’ve learned how to plan smarter, it’s time to start tracking those plans.  
What played out? What didn’t? Where did you hesitate? Where did you execute like a 
pro? That’s what we’ll cover next: how to journal your trades and review your progress 
the proper way.

J O U R N A L I N G
T H E  P A T H  T O  R E A L  P R O G R E S S
I
n the beginning, journaling was exciting, especially when I was winning.  
When everything looked green, and the trades worked out exactly as planned, I would 
stare at my journal for hours. It felt good, and it gave me a sense of pride. But the  
moment things went the other way, when I hit a losing streak, that’s when the real test 
came. Suddenly, the same journal that once felt like a trophy now felt like something I 
wanted to hide with shame. I wanted to reset it, delete the red, and start fresh,  
pretending the losses never happened.
That’s exactly what most traders do. They love journaling when they’re making money, 
but the moment it gets uncomfortable, they walk away from it. They lie to themselves 
and act like it’s not helpful. The worst part is they stop tracking their progress when it 
matters most.
Here’s the truth: the losses are where the work begins. Your journal isn’t supposed to 
boost your ego; it’s supposed to give you clarity regardless of how painful it is. If you're 
scrolling through someone's journal results on social media and it's all green, all perfect, 
don’t let that discourage you. You have no idea what that person went through to get 
there. You don’t know how many losses they had to take or if what they’re showing is 
even real.
It’s easy to show wins and share highlights, but growth doesn’t happen in the highlights. 
It occurs when you can see the cold, hard reality. The process itself will reveal whether 
those trades are legitimate or not, and this realism will set you apart from the crowd. If 
you’re looking at your journal right now and it’s filled with red, that’s not a sign of failure. 
That’s your starting point, which will allow you to hit the next level. If you can’t face your 
losses, you’re not taking this seriously. You’re lying to yourself. And no serious trader 
builds long-term success on fiction.
If this sounds familiar, don’t worry. If you can push through that resistance and make 
journaling a habit, even when it’s uncomfortable, you’re already ahead of most. Because 
now, you’re working with truth, and as they say, the truth will first piss you off and then 
set you free. Journaling is data collection; it’s where you get the raw information to  
improve. Journaling is not about writing down everything to feel productive; it’s about 
holding up a mirror you can’t ignore. One that shows you both the wins and the weak 
spots.
Keep it simple and don’t overcomplicate it. Don’t spend more time journaling just for the 
sake of it. Just make sure that what you write down actually helps you learn. The goal 
is a rapid feedback loop. If you’re serious about improving as a trader, at some point, 
you’re going to have to start tracking and making data-driven decisions.
239
LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

===== PAGE 250 =====
The real reasons you must journal
When you track each trade, you start noticing patterns you never saw in the moment. 
Your habits, good and bad, begin to emerge. And now that you see them, you can  
control them. Just like goals, you can’t hit a target you can’t see. It also holds you  
accountable. When it’s all written down, there’s no hiding from your decisions.  
You can’t say, “I followed the plan,” if your notes clearly show otherwise.
As you continue to do this, something powerful begins to happen: you build your own 
playbook. You will transition from directly copying others to refining what works for you 
within the LTA framework. Your trade selection improves as you begin recognizing which 
setups actually align with your edge and which ones consistently drain your account.
Additionally, journaling helps protect you from emotional spirals. When you’re in a  
drawdown or feeling frustrated, it stops you from trading impulsively. You’ve already 
established a reference point of what works, and you’re checking if your behavior aligns 
with your plan. From there, you can begin to optimize from actual data.  
Do you understand?
Building a Journal That Works
Let me be clear about one thing: I never review a trade based solely on the entry.  
I always start at the top, which means going through the entire LTA process, from macro 
fundamental analysis to macro technicals, to intraday trends, and finally, to execution. If 
your strategy isn’t clearly defined and your process isn’t structured, then journaling won’t 
be effective. Period. Without a clear framework, what are you even journaling? You’re 
just doing busy work and nothing will line up. Your data will be random because there’s 
no congruence between one trade and the next.
That’s why, before you even start journaling, you need to make sure your system is solid. 
You need to know what your entries look like, what your confirmations are, what  
invalidates a trade, and your risk parameters. This might sound like extra work, but it’s 
actually what makes reviewing trades so powerful. The former Navy SEAL Jocko Willink 
said it best: “Discipline equals freedom.”
By revisiting the complete trade journey, I can determine whether I overlooked a macro 
red flag, entered too early without sufficient confirmation, or followed my plan precisely. 
When I return to my journal, I’m reviewing a well-documented process.

Tools, Automation, and the Power of AI
There are many trading journaling software options available. Whether you prefer  
building your own spreadsheet or using a pre-built tool, the key is to make the process 
as effortless and consistent as possible. Automation can save time, reduce friction, and 
help you stay disciplined. Even when setting up your spreadsheet, keep it simple by  
using tables, drop-downs, or quick-entry sections. This way, updating it becomes an 
easy part of your process rather than a chore.
At a minimum, you should be able to add a small amount of text, maybe a few metrics, 
and even include a screenshot. If it takes too long or feels like work you’re dreading, you 
won’t do it. But if you streamline it, it becomes automatic.
Here’s a hack few traders use; we’re living in a time when AI can help you make sense 
of your journal. If you build it right, you can feed your spreadsheet to AI and ask it to 
analyze everything. Where do you win the most? What patterns show up in your losing 
trades? Which setups are your strongest? AI can find the patterns and give you feed-
back with more clarity than you'd expect. 
You want it to be clear, well-structured, and accurate. If your journal is messy, inconsis-
tent, or incomplete, the feedback you get will be just as vague. But if you organize your 
data correctly, AI can act like your personal analyst, showing you exactly what’s working, 
what’s not, and where your most significant opportunities are hiding. Use it to your  
advantage so you can optimize your feedback loop and learn exponentially.
Let’s break down how you can start doing this effectively. 
How to Journal Step by Step
1. Asset Traded – Which pair, index, or commodity did you trade? Track this to spot 
strengths and weaknesses across instruments.
2. Macro Bias – Write down the fundamental context: COT data, seasonality, or  
economic data that shaped your bias.
3. Technical Context – Identify both macro and micro confirmations. Was price  
reacting to a higher-timeframe supply or demand zone (macro)? And what  
volume-based key levels or reactions (micro) supported your decision?
4. Intra-Day Insight – Was the market trending, ranging, or breaking above or below 
the previous weekly range on the lower timeframes?
5. Trade Type – Was it a contrarian or momentum setup? This reveals which style suits 
you better over time.
6. Entry Snapshot – Screenshot each trade (before and after, if possible).
7. Risk Setup – Note your stop-loss, target, R:R, and position sizing.
8. Time of Entry – Note the exact time and which session (Asia, London, or New York) 
the trade was placed in. Over time, this reveals which parts of the day give you the 
best results.
241
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S

LTA Pub. No. RDP78-03307

9. Trade Duration – How long did you hold the trade? Was it a quick scalp, an intraday 
move, or a multi-day swing? This helps you understand your natural style and which 
durations best suit your edge.
10. Outcome – Was it a win, loss, or breakeven? Did it follow your plan?
11. Mindset Notes – How did you feel before, during, and after the trade?
Turning Your Journal Into Data
Once you’ve tracked a handful of trades using the steps above, it’s time to zoom out. 
Create a second table that gives you an overview of your performance. This doesn’t 
need to be fancy, but it should be clear and concise.
The best part is that most of this can be automated. If you create a spreadsheet that 
includes formulas and structure from the start, all you’ll need to do is update the input 
fields, and everything else updates automatically. That’s when your journal starts work-
ing for you. Below is a sample table layout that you can copy directly into your preferred 
spreadsheet tool.

Fill this out weekly or monthly to get a crystal-clear view of what’s working and what

review process before a trade ever happens. You can build scenarios, test outcomes,

have a complete feedback loop. One captures what you’ve done, and the other prepares 
you for what’s next. And maybe, just maybe, you’ll start seeing the kind of results that 
others dream about.

I
n the previous chapter, I discussed journaling and the importance of tracking your 
trades. I used to think I could just memorize patterns or react to structural shifts, and 
that would be enough. But after a while, I realized I was flying blind. I needed to know if 
what I was doing actually worked. It is easy to always be "in the moment", to take trades, 
have the mindset of "I’ll learn along the way" and never actually do any of the deep work 
beforehand.
I started digging through past charts. I wanted to know, does this strategy truly give 
me an edge? Is it something I can trust? That’s where backtesting came in. I had to do 
my homework to understand the market, and it paid off. I could see what would have 
worked, where I would’ve gotten trapped, and how often a setup delivered.
However, the reality is that most traders backtest technical setups, such as zones or 
patterns. And from my experience, that alone is confusing. Sometimes a pattern worked, 
sometimes it didn’t. Every move was different. The fractal market is too nuanced to 
provide us with any sustainable clarity on what we need to examine. I was missing the 
context needed to make it quantifiable.
So I changed how I did it. Instead of only backtesting technical chart setups, I began 
combining my technical analysis with the macro-fundamental context, as we discussed 
in our previous chapters.
Most of my testing happens on the higher timeframes, where the bigger story unfolds 
without all the noise. I form a macro bias first, then I examine the structure, and then I 
look at volume. That top-down layering gave me clarity. It gave my setups meaning.
And that’s what I teach now. It’s how we built this book, and it’s why our lower timeframe 
setups feel so sharp and clean; they’re backed by something bigger.
This entire shift in mindset is what led to the development of the XLimitless Alerts & 
Backtesting Library within our LTA community. A place where all this research,  
validation, and macro intelligence reside and are shared. A database of back-tested 
information, knowing exactly what works, and where we have our edge.
We’ve tested all of these repeatedly. And we put it all on the chart so traders can easily 
understand it. Whether it’s a shift in sentiment, a seasonal high or low, or valuation  
extremes. Every trade setup we pinpoint starts from real, tested data.

The Commercials vs Retail Signal on the Dollar
One of the strongest and most consistent signals we've backtested is based on the  
behavior of two key market participants: Commercials and Retail Traders.
When Commercials hit a bullish extreme and Retail hits a bearish extreme, especially on 
the 26-week average, we see a powerful trade signal. We refer to this as a  
contrarian confirmation, and it has proven to be highly accurate. In fact, when this 
condition is met, the setup has shown around 75% accuracy across historical data since 
2015, over the last 10 years.
What makes this so valuable is that it provides a clean, repeatable method for building a 
bias. And because it’s rooted in macro-fundamental positioning, that bias tends to last for 
weeks, sometimes even months.
• Indicator: XLimitless - Smart Money Intelligence (COT) - 26 Week Extreme 
• Discovery: When both Commercials and Retail are in opposite extremes, historical 
trade signals occur. 
• Direction Type: Both 
• Confluence Type: Leading Confirmation (1) 
• Additional Information: The Dollar is in a Macro Up Trend. Bullish signals occur 
when the Commercials are in a Bullish Extreme, and Retail in a Bearish Extreme 
(Trend Following), Short Term Bearish or Pull Backs occur when the Commercials 
are in a Bearish Extreme, and Retail in a Bullish Extreme (Counter Macro Trend).

The Retail Extreme on the British Pound
What we discovered is that retail traders are usually on the wrong side when they reach 
their positioning extremes, both on the bullish and bearish side.
According to our backtesting, when retail traders reach a bullish extreme,  
particularly around the 15,000 net positioning mark, it has historically signaled a macro 
bearish reversal. Even more importantly, this signal has demonstrated an 88% accuracy 
rate across over a decade of historical data.
• Indicator: XLimitless - Net Edge Retail (COT) 
• Discovery: Net Positioning 16,900 / 26,800 = Bullish Extreme (Bearish Reversal  
Signal) | Net Positioning -20,600 / -30,000 = Bearish Extreme (Short Term Pullback). 
• Direction Type: Both 
• Confluence Type: Leading Confirmation (1) - Use with Commercial Extremes 
• Additional Information: Retail behavior is sensitive and very accurate with the GBP. 
Works as a primary confluence when used with the COT Commercials. In a macro 
downtrend, Retail in a bullish extreme can signal the macro top. In a counter-trend, it 
can signal a short-term bottom, and vice-versa when the trend shifts.
This is evident in the chart above. Since 2011, nearly every time retail net positioning 
reached the 15,000+ level, price hit a macro top and reversed. This setup plays out on 
the weekly timeframe, which makes it even more powerful. Once this macro trend  
begins to roll over, it opens the door to an incredible number of high-quality trading  
opportunities on the lower timeframes, because now you have the right bias in mind.
And when this signal aligned with a macro downtrend and major supply zones,  
the results were incredibly accurate.

built into our alert system. When these behaviors reappear, we know what to look for: 
either a major top or a short-term bottom. It gives us clear triggers, reduces confusion, 
and makes us more consistent.
This repeatable pattern gives us another edge. Another reason to trust the macro  
process, and another way to stay ahead of the crowd.

Seasonal Weakness on the S&P 500
Our final example examines the S&P 500 and how we utilize seasonality to gain insight 
into potential market shifts.
Using our FutureScope™ indicator, which analyzes 5 to 10 years of seasonal  
behavior, we’ve noticed something consistent: August through October tends to be a 
weaker period for the index. What makes this particularly interesting is that this pattern 
holds up even during macro uptrends.
• Indicator: AI Futurescope 5 year, 2 Smoothing Period 
• Discovery: Following Major Seasonal Shifts 
• Direction Type: Bearish 
• Confluence Type: Additional Confluence (3) 
• Additional Information: ES1! typically experiences Bullish moves during May to 
August and Bearish moves during the summer months (August to October). Can be 
used in conjunction with any Leading Confirmation (1) or Additional Confluence (3).
In the screenshot above, you can see that despite a strong bullish market, the S&P 500 
regularly exhibits pullbacks or slower momentum during this time window. This is where 
seasonal tendencies give us a unique advantage; most traders don’t account for them.
As the calendar rolls into August and we begin to see signs of a weaker macro  
technical structure, the FutureScope helps confirm that these signs aren’t random. 
They’re seasonal and have happened before, with a high likelihood of repeating again.

Semi-Automating the Macro Process
Once we’ve done the backtesting and gathered the macro data, we don’t want to start 
from scratch every time we look at the charts. As there is no way we can stay up to date 
manually, even though we have back-tested everything in advance. This is where our 
alert system comes in.
Within the LTA community, we’ve established a comprehensive alert channel, which you 
can also set up yourself. These are built from macro data; once a condition is met,  
the alert goes off. And now we know it’s time to check the chart.
This helps us move fast, without burning our focus. Instead of checking every pair or 
asset manually day after day, we wait. When an alert goes off, it means our backtested 
conditions have lined up again. That’s our cue to head to the chart and start forming the 
macro bias.
From there, it’s just like we’ve always taught. Once the macro bias is in place, we shift to 
the lower timeframes. That’s when we look at structure, volume levels, and time our  
entries. However, now we’re not guessing. We’re not just reacting to price.  
We’re following a system that was built and tested, and tells us when to pay attention.
The best part is that this process makes everything easier. You don’t have to run a  
complete top-down analysis every day. You’ve already done the hard work. You’ve built 
the data and structured the conditions; now you wait for the signal.
This is what allows us to stay sharp, consistent, and efficient. Our macro view is  
automated. Our technicals are responsive, and our trades are fully aligned.

===== PAGE 268 =====
The Myth of a Top-Down Analysis
Many traders believe that top-down analysis is something you do all in one go, from 
macro to micro, in a straight line. But that idea couldn’t be further from the truth.  
It’s one of the biggest misconceptions out there.
The reality is that our process is layered over time. Once we’ve done the backtesting, 
collected the macro data, and built our alert system, it doesn’t all happen at once.  
Instead, we plant seeds for the harvest. 
For example, we might get an alert that the British Pound has reached a bullish retail 
extreme. That’s the first layer, and now we’re aware something might be setting up.  
But nothing has happened yet.
A week later, or maybe three weeks later, the market taps into a weekly supply zone and 
starts rejecting from it. That’s our second layer, a macro technical confirmation.  
Now we’re paying more attention.
Still, the trade might not be there. We wait, and maybe another week passes. Then,  
suddenly, price begins to break down intraday—a clean lower high forms, and the  
structure shifts. Now, we have our third layer, the intraday trend.
From that point forward, it’s game on. We now have a fundamental bias, a macro  
technical confirmation, and an intraday trend. That one setup leads to a whole wave of 
trades over the next few weeks, maybe even months, all while we ride the new macro 
direction, having a clear bias.
Eventually, a new macro bias shows up. A new retail extreme, and the cycle begins 
again. That’s why we built the alert system in the first place. Not to act fast but to act at 
the right time, avoiding the loss of valuable intelligence. And by the time all the layers 
line up, we’re already prepared. All we have to do is focus on the lower timeframes and 
execute.
Now that you understand this, let’s move forward. In the next chapter, we’re going to 
break down 10 real trade examples. These are comprehensive top-down walkthroughs, 
from macro to micro, to show you exactly how we implement the entire process.

===== PAGE 304 =====
G L O S S A R Y
LTA Key Levels & Volume Profile
Volume Profile
 
A histogram plotted on the price axis shows the volume traded at each price level over a 
chosen range. Highlights PoC, value area, HVNs, and LVNs that intraday traders use as 
magnets and pivots.
 
Sunday Open (SO)
 
The Sunday Open is the first price printed when the forex market opens after the  
weekend. Traders watch it to gauge the fresh mood and to anchor their weekly plans.
 
Previous Sunday Open (PSO)
 
This is last week’s Sunday Open. Comparing the two helps you determine if the new 
week started stronger or weaker than the previous one.
 
PoC (Point of Control)
 
Within any volume profile, the PoC is the single price at which most contracts are traded. 
Because so many people did business there, price often drifts back to it like a magnet.
 
VaH (Value Area High)
 
The top edge of the value area. It marks where trading activity began to thin out on the 
high side.
 
VaL (Value Area Low)
 
The bottom edge of the value area. It marks where trading activity began to thin out on 
the low side.
 
High Volume Node
 
A price level that shows a fat hump on the volume profile, indicating that a of trading took 
place and support or resistance may form.
 
Low Volume Node
 
A narrow section of the volume profile that exhibits minimal trading activity. Price can 
jump through this area quickly because not many orders are placed there.
Weekly PoC (CW PoC)
The PoC is calculated for the current trading week once at least two days have closed.

===== PAGE 305 =====
Weekly VaH (CW VaH)
 
The highest price inside this week’s value area. Draw it after at least 2 full trading days. 
Weekly VaL (CW VaL)
 
The lowest price inside this week’s value area. Draw it after at least 2 full trading days.
 
Previous Weekly PoC (PW PoC)
 
Last week’s PoC. Many traders mark it as a target or barrier.
 
Previous Weekly VaH / VaL (PW VaH / PW VaL)
 
The value‑area edges from last week.
 
Early Previous Weekly PoC / VaH / VaL (EPW)
 
Levels that come from any week before the immediately previous one. They are older 
but can still act as signposts.
 
Previous Daily PoC / VaH / VaL (PD PoC / VaH / VaL)
 
Yesterday’s high‑volume and value‑area levels. Intraday traders love them.
Sentiment and Positioning
Sentiment
 
The crowd’s mood. Are people mostly greedy or mostly scared? Sentiment often shifts 
before the chart does, so it can hint at the next move.
 
Commitment of Traders (COT) Report
 
A free weekly report from the CFTC, shows how three big groups are positioned in  
futures markets: Commercials, Large Speculators, and Retail Traders.
 
Commercials (Hedgers)
 
Real businesses that use or produce the underlying asset. They trade futures to lock in 
prices, not to gamble. They tend to buy near lows and sell near highs.
 
Large Speculators (Non‑Commercials)
 
Hedge funds, banks, and other money managers who chase profit by riding trends.  
They are momentum drivers, not hedgers.
Retail Traders (Non‑Reportables)
 
Individual traders trading small size. As a crowd, they often arrive late and overreact at 
extremes, which makes them a useful contrarian signal.
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
G  L  O  S  S  A  R  Y

===== PAGE 306 =====
Net Positioning Extreme
 
A moment when one group holds a historically huge net long or net short position.  
Think of it as the far end of the rubber band.
 
Relative Oscillator Extreme
 
Instead of raw contract counts, this measures where current positioning sits on a 
0‑to‑100 scale compared with the last six months or more. Readings near 0 or 100 flag 
short‑term extremes.
 
Laddering Process
 
The slow, step‑by‑step way Large Speculators build or unload positions over many 
weeks, fueling the life cycle of a trend.
 
Crowded Trade
 
A situation where too many traders are on the same side of the market. Crowded trades 
often unwind fast when the crowd panics.
Open Interest and Liquidity
Open Interest (OI)
 
The total number of live futures contracts that have not been closed yet. Rising OI  
indicates that fresh money is entering the market while falling OI suggests that traders 
are backing out.
 
Short Covering
 
When traders who were betting against the market buy back contracts to get out.  
Their buying can give a quick boost to price.
 
Liquidity
 
How easy it is to buy or sell without significantly affecting the price. High liquidity means 
many willing buyers and sellers are waiting.
 
Futures and Options Cycles
 
Futures Expiration
 
The preset date when a futures contract stops trading. Traders must close or roll their 
positions by that day.
 
Roll Over
 
Closing an expiring futures contract and reopening the same trade in the next delivery 
month so you stay in the market.
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
G  L  O  S  S  A  R  Y

===== PAGE 307 =====
Quarterly Cycle (90‑Day Cycle)
 
The March, June, September, and December expirations. These four dates usually  
create the biggest waves of liquidity and market rotation.
 
Market Rotation
 
The sudden shift of money from one asset or sector into another, often sparked by  
futures or options expirations.
Proprietary LTA Tools
Net Edge™ Indicator
 
Plots the raw long‑, short‑, and net positions of Commercials, Large Specs, and Retail 
on TradingView charts and fires alerts when any group reaches a historic net extreme.
 
Smart Money Intelligence (SMI)™
 
A 0‑to‑100 oscillator that normalizes COT positioning and open‑interest shifts so you can 
spot sentiment extremes, crowd divergences, and liquidity expansions at a glance.
 
Open Interest Radar™
 
Charts the weekly CFTC open‑interest totals contract‑by‑contract, making it obvious 
when real liquidity is entering, stalling, or flushing out of a market.
 
Stealth Valuation Index™
 
Compares an asset’s %‑change over a chosen look‑back to a benchmark (default DX1!), 
transforms that distance into a −100 / +100 scale, and flags when something is  
statistically under or overvalued.
 
FutureScope™ Seasonal Model
 
Blends 1‑, 5‑, 10‑, 20‑, and 30‑year averages to project likely seasonal highs and lows 
weeks or months ahead, great for spotting Q4 strength in gold or summer softness in 
indices.
 
XLimitless Alerts & Backtesting Library
 
Our internal database houses thousands of back‑tested sentiment, seasonal, valuation, 
and technical patterns. Pushes real‑time TradingView alerts whenever a high‑probability 
setup repeats.
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
G  L  O  S  S  A  R  Y

===== PAGE 308 =====
Intermarket & Correlations
Positive Correlation
 
When two markets tend to move in tandem, usually climbing or falling together.  
A reading near +1 on the correlation coefficient tool confirms the link.
 
Negative Correlation
 
When two markets typically move in opposite directions. A reading near ‑1 on the  
correlation coefficient tool confirms the inverse link.
 
Correlation Coefficient
 
A simple statistic (‑1 to +1) that measures how tightly two price series move together.  
We check it before trusting any relationship.
 
Risk‑On / Risk‑Off
 
Short‑hand for the market’s appetite for danger. Risk‑On means money flows into stocks, 
crypto, and growth assets. Risk‑Off means cash shifts into safe havens such as the  
dollar, yen, or bonds.
 
Cross‑Asset Flow
 
The migration of capital from one corner of the market to another (for example, out of 
tech stocks and into energy). Tracking these flows helps spot new trends early.
Seasonality in Practice
Seasonality
 
Regular patterns that repeat at certain times of the year, such as energy prices rising in 
winter.
 
Santa Rally
 
The tendency for U.S. equities to drift higher during the last week of December and the 
first two sessions of January.
 
Sell in May and Go Away
 
An old stock‑market saying that highlights the tendency for weaker equity returns be‑
tween May and October.
 
Window Dressing
 
When fund managers buy recent winners (and dump losers) near quarter or year‑end, 
so their portfolio statements look better.
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
G  L  O  S  S  A  R  Y

===== PAGE 309 =====
Driving Season
 
The late spring and summer stretch in which gasoline demand jumps, often lifting crude 
oil prices.
Relative Value Concepts
Relative Value
Judging an asset’s price by comparing it with a related market instead of only its own 
past moves.
Supply and Demand Mechanics & Patterns
Supply Zone
 
An area on the chart where many sellers have shown up before, so price may struggle to 
rise higher there.
 
Demand Zone
 
An area where strong buying appeared before, so price may find support there.
 
Auction Market Theory (AMT)
 
A framework that treats every chart as a live auction where buyers and sellers haggle to 
find fair value. Balance is the quiet haggle; imbalance is the sudden deal that rips price 
away.
 
Balance (Range)
 
A period when buyers and sellers are evenly matched, so price rotates sideways and 
volume is evenly spread.
 
Imbalance (Impulse)
 
The burst that happens when one side overwhelms the other. Candles stretch, volume 
spikes and price leaves the old range in a hurry.
 
Break of Structure (BOS)
 
When price smashes through the most recent swing high or low, indicating that the old 
pattern has been broken.
Macro Structural Shift
 
A BOS that also wipes out a major supply or demand zone on a higher timeframe,  
revealing a power change under the surface.
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
G  L  O  S  S  A  R  Y

===== PAGE 310 =====
Three‑Candle Bar Play
 
A simple way to spot where a zone starts: candle 1 begins a base, candle 2 stalls,  
candle 3 closes past candle 1’s range and launches the breakout.
 
Four Core Patterns 
• Rally‑Base‑Rally (RBR) – continuation demand
• Drop‑Base‑Drop (DBD) – continuation supply
• Drop‑Base‑Rally (DBR) – reversal demand
• Rally‑Base‑Drop (RBD) – reversal supply
 
Accumulation
 
Smart money soaking up sell orders after a down‑move, usually inside or just above a 
demand zone, preparing for a push higher.
 
Distribution
 
Smart money unloading into eager buyers near the highs, often inside or just below a 
supply zone, preparing for a drop.
 
CERC Pattern
 
Consolidation → Expansion → Retracement → Continuation. A healthy trend blueprint 
where pauses refuel the move.
 
CME Pattern
 
Consolidation → Manipulation (fake break) → Expansion the opposite way. Classic 
stop‑hunt shake‑out before the real trend begins.
 
Manipulation (Stop‑Run)
 
A fast spike that grabs liquidity above a range high or below a range low, then reverses.
 
Head‑and‑Shoulders / Inverted H&S
 
A three‑peak formation (or three‑trough for inverted) signaling distribution or accumula‑
tion when it forms at a macro zone.
 
W Formation (Double Bottom)
 
Two quick lows separated by a bounce often mark the end of the distribution and the 
start of a new up‑trend when the context is bullish.
 
Untested Zone
 
A fresh supply or demand area that price has not revisited since the breakout. The first 
retest is usually the strongest.
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
G  L  O  S  S  A  R  Y

===== PAGE 311 =====
Sharp Move
 
A rapid, long‑range candle sequence that creates a clean pocket of imbalance, hinting at 
institutional urgency.
 
Fractal (Multi‑Timeframe)
 
The idea that every big pattern contains smaller copies of itself on lower charts. We start 
on weekly/3‑day/daily, then drill down.
 
Higher Timeframe (HTF)
 
Any chart daily or above that guides bias and marks the most reliable zones.
 
Preparation Phase
 
The waiting period while structure forms and liquidity stacks. Entries are planned but not 
triggered.
 
Execution Phase
 
Begins when price confirms the HTF idea, usually with a BOS, volume surge, or valid 
CERC/CME break, so trades are launched.
Trading Archetypes & Styles
Contrarian Trader
 
A trader who looks for crowded extremes, over‑bullish highs or over‑bearish lows, and 
bets on the snap‑back once smart‑money footprints and macro data confirm exhaustion.
 
Momentum Trader
 
A trader who waits for breakouts backed by rising volume, Open Interest, and 
Large‑Spec laddering, then rides the established trend instead of fighting it.
 
Contrarian Phase
 
The market stage where sentiment, positioning, and valuation all flash extremes. Smart 
money begins to turn, and price stalls at a macro zone, prime hunting ground for  
contrarian setups.
 
Momentum Phase
 
The stage that follows the structural shift. Fresh money, rising Open Interest, and larger 
players drive price in one direction; momentum traders build positions here.
Swing Trading
 
Holding positions for days or weeks to capture bigger legs between macro supply and 
demand zones.
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
G  L  O  S  S  A  R  Y

===== PAGE 312 =====
Day Trading
 
Entering and exiting within the same session, using intraday volume‑profile levels and 
micro structure for precision.
 
Hybrid Model
 
LTA’s approach that lets you anchor bias with weekly/daily zones (swing) while timing 
entries on 15 m–1 h charts (day): one framework, two timelines.
Trading Psychology & Cognitive Biases
Confirmation Bias
 
The habit of searching for and favoring information that agrees with your existing view 
while ignoring evidence that contradicts it. This leads to holding losers too long or  
jumping into trades that only “look” supportive.
 
Overconfidence Bias
 
Feeling invincible after a winning streak and therefore oversizing or loosening rules.  
A chemical high (often fuelled by rising testosterone) that makes you underestimate risk.
 
Anchoring Bias
 
Locking your mind onto the entry price or a random reference point and refusing to adapt 
when conditions change. A common cause of "just get back to breakeven" behavior.
 
Loss Aversion
 
The brain’s instinct to feel the pain of losses about twice as strongly as the pleasure of 
equal‑sized gains. Encourages cutting winners early and holding losers, hoping they 
turn.
 
Illusion of Control
 
Believing you can steer random market outcomes simply through effort or insight.  
Creates overtrading and rule‑bending when the market doesn’t obey.
 
Overtrading
 
Taking far more setups than your plan allows, often boredom or the illusion of control in 
disguise. More trades ≠ better trades.
Winner Effect
 
The surge of confidence and risk‑taking that follows a series of wins is biologically linked 
to temporary spikes in testosterone.
X  L I M I T L E S S  -  L T A  C O N C E P T S
G  L  O  S  S  A  R  Y

===== PAGE 313 =====
Scenario Mapping
 
Writing out “if‑then” playbooks in advance (e.g., "If the price rejects weekly supply, then 
drop to 1 h and look for shorts"). Encourages anticipation over prediction.
 
Forecasting vs. Anticipating
 
Forecasting tries to call one exact future path; anticipating prepares for several probable 
paths and reacts when one confirms.
 
Trade Journal / Journaling
 
A structured record of every trade’s context, execution, outcome, and emotions. Turns 
random wins/losses into actionable data for improvement.
